  e metodologias no campo mais amplo da criatividade computacional.
abstract_en: 'The development of computational artefacts to study cross-modal associations
  has been an increasing area of research. By integrating insights from cognitive
  sciences, this research proposes a novel systematic approach to the computational
  exploration of relationships between music and abstract images grounded by perceived
  emotions. Given the abstract nature of this problem, we employ evolutionary programming
  techniques to customise the audio-visual dialogue. Additionally, to articulate the
  complexity of the problem, we propose a framework that facilitates the integration
  of knowledge into a computational approach.Building on the premise that humans share
  similar audio-visual perceptions, we investigated computational approaches for exploring
  perceptually relevant audio-visual relationships. To test our working hypothesis,
  we developed a series of experimental prototypes and co-creative experiences; namely,
  we evolved a set of music to a corresponding set of images, guided by the expression
  of four emotions: anger, calm, happiness, and sadness. Furthermore, we conduct user
  tests to evaluate whether the users'' perceptions were consistent with the system''s
  output and discuss the advantages of using evolutionary strategies, such as genetic
  programming. Results suggest an alignment between the users'' perceptions of the
  music-image pairs and the system''s results, supporting the integration of cognitive
  science knowledge. Key contributions of this thesis include the creation of a dataset
  that enables the transposition of theoretical associations between music, imagery,
  and emotions into the development of a co-creative tool. Overall, this research
  not only deepened the understanding of the importance of integrating cognitive and
  emotional dimensions for perceptually relevant audio-visual languages but also provided
  new tools and methodologies for artists and designers in the field of computational
  creativity.'

# Audiovisual Metaphors Based on Perceptually Relevant Principles

Ana Cláudia Leitão Teixeira Rodrigues

PERCEPTUALLY RELEV ANT PRINCIPLES

ABSTRACT
The development of computational artefacts to study cross-modal as-
sociations has been an increasing area of research. By integrating
insights from cognitive sciences, this research proposes a novel sys-
tematic approach to the computational exploration of relationships
between music and abstract images grounded by perceived emotions.
Given the abstract nature of this problem, we employ evolutionary
programming techniques to customise the audio-visual dialogue. Ad-
ditionally , to articulate the complexity of the problem, we propose a
framework that facilitates the integration of knowledge into a compu-
tational approach.
Building on the premise that humans share similar audio-visual
perceptions, we investigated computational approaches for exploring
perceptually relevant audio-visual relationships. To test our working
hypothesis, we developed a series of experimental prototypes and
co-creative experiences; namely , we evolved a set of music to a corre-
sponding set of images, guided by the expression of four emotions:
anger, calm, happiness, and sadness. Furthermore, we conduct user
tests to evaluate whether the users’ perceptions were consistent with
the system’s output and discuss the advantages of using evolutionary
strategies, such as genetic programming. Results suggest an align-
ment between the users’ perceptions of the music-image pairs and
the system’s results, supporting the integration of cognitive science
knowledge.
Key contributions of this thesis include the creation of a dataset
that enables the transposition of theoretical associations between mu-
sic, imagery , and emotions into the development of a co-creative tool.
Overall, this research not only deepened the understanding of the
importance of integrating cognitive and emotional dimensions for
perceptually relevant audio-visual languages but also provided new
tools and methodologies for artists and designers in the ﬁeld of com-
putational creativity .
Keywords: Audio-Visual Perception, Computational Creativity , Cross-
Modal Associations, Evolutionary Processes, Media Design Tools.
v
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

RESUMO
O desenvolvimento de artefatos computacionais para estudar asso-
ciações multi-modais tem sido um tópico de pesquisa com um foco
crescente. Partindo da integração do conhecimento de ciências cog-
nitivas em relações audio-visuais, esta tese propõe uma abordagem
inovadora e sistemática para a exploração computacional de relações
audio-visuais perceptualmente relevantes. Devido à natureza abas-
trata deste problema, utilizamos técnicas de programação evolucioná-
ria para customisar o diálogo audio-visual. Adicionalmente, de forma
a articular a complexidade do problema, propomos uma framework
que facilite a integração de conhecimento numa abordagem compu-
tacional.
De forma a testar a hipótese de trabalho, evoluímos um conjunto
experiências e protótipos, nomeadamente, evoluímos um conjunto de
músicas num conjunto correspondente de imagens, guiado pela ex-
pressão de quatro emoções (raiva, calma, felicidade, tristeza). Adicio-
nalmente, realizamos testes com utilizadores para avaliar se a percep-
ção do utilizador era consistente com a expressão do sistema. Os re-
sultados sugeriram um acordo entre a percepção dos utilizadores nos
pares música-imagem e os resultados do sistema, favorecendo a inte-
gração do conhecimento das ciências cognitivas. Discutimos ainda as
vantagens de utilizar estratégias evolutivas, como o caso da progra-
mação genética.
As principais contribuições desta tese incluíram a criação de um
conjunto de dados que permitisse transpor a interação entre música,
imagem e emoções; e o desenvolvimento de uma série modelos com-
putacionais co-creativos. De forma geral, os resultados desta inves-
tigação não só aprofundaram a importância de integrar dimensões
cognitivas e emocionais em linguagens audio-visuais, como também
forneceu novas ferramentas e metodologias no campo mais amplo da
criatividade computacional.
Palavras-chave: Perceção Audiovisual, Criatividade Computacional,
Associações Multidimensionais, Processos Evolucionários, Ferramen-
tas de Design Digital.

Figure 6 Auditory information processing. Sound waves
coming from a sound source triggers changes
in the air pressure. (a) These waves are fun-
nelled via the external ear (pinna) (b), and then
the tympanic member or eardrum transmits
the sound from the air to the (c) ossicles inside
the middle ear, which converts and ampliﬁes
vibration in air to vibration in ﬂuid. (d) Next,
the sound passes through the inner ear where
the hair cells of the cochlea form synapses to
(e) bring auditory information into the brain
(Cariani, 2019; Carlson, 2013). 17

Figure 47 Evolutionary process overview. ( 1) population
of candidate solutions and 1.2) selection of ( 2)
solutions sub-set; (3) reproduction, usually through
3.1) mutation or 3.2) reproduction; (4) new gen-
eration and 4.2) evaluation of the new space
of candidate solutions with a ﬁtness function.
This process is usually repeated for several gen-
erations until speciﬁc criteria are met. 70

1INTRODUCTION
1.1 motivation
Throughout history , humanity has been developing tools and art-
works that help us shape and better understand the world in which
we live. For instance, Music and Images are two common forms of ef-
fectively expressing ideas and emotions that are difﬁcult to convey in
other ways. When adequately expressed, these non-verbal languages
are rich sources of communication and can achieve more comprehen-
sive communication than verbal languages.
Famous scientists and artists such as Pythagoras, Kandinsky and
Collopy have contributed to new perspectives in Audio-Visual Re-
lationships (A VR) (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007; Collopy and
Fuhrer, 2001). Although many examples of audiovisual artworks ex-
ist, few authors have explored this relationship from a perceptual
and cognitively informed perspective, often resulting in arbitrary or
incomprehensible mappings.
Recent contributions in Cognitive Sciences (CS) have emphasised
the role of emotions and perception in the Audio-Visual Relationship
(A VR) (Albertazzi, Canal, and Micciolo, 2015; Caiola, 2022; Palmer
et al., 2013; Pesek et al., 2017), contributing to our understanding of
how emotions can be perceived and integrated within the context
of audio-visual stimuli. As computers became increasingly prevalent
in society , new possibilities for exploring A VR emerged. By better
understanding the impact of several variables in the computational
interplay of music and image, we may bring new ways to bridge the
two domains.
In this thesis, we propose to develop and study digital tools to
explore bridges that may emerge from computationally crossing the
domains of music, image, and cognitive sciences. To support our re-
search, we conduct an extensive theoretical analysis and systematise
the ﬁndings and relationships between these domains. This results in
a framework that enables us to systematically explore the structural
and perceptual-affective correspondences between music and image.
Then, through a series of experiments based on evolutionary pro-
cesses, we investigate structural and perceptual-affective correspon-
dences in a way that allows us to collect relevant data to better un-
derstand this complex and creative relationship and draw signiﬁcant
conclusions. The use of evolutionary strategies enabled us to search
effectively for solutions in a large and complex space. Such capabili-
ties were used to explore combinations of audio-visual features that
match a user’s preferences in an adaptive way (Moroni et al., 2014).
This approach allows us to forge novel processes in creating cross-
modal languages and explore new relationships that may emerge
from computationally combining the creative languages of Music and
1
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

2i n t r o d u c t i o n
Image. Additionally , the proposed research aims to provide new in-
sights for practitioners and researchers in media design and Compu-
tational Creativity (CC).
1.2 working hypothesis
We hypothesise that the generation of a system of audio-visual rela-
tionships beneﬁts from a multidisciplinary approach where ﬁndings
from cognitive science play an essential role in mediating this rela-
tionship (see Figure 1). Additionally , we argue that the relationships
of an abstract audio-visual language (A VL) can be extended through
evolutionary processes, as they have the power to evolve new combi-
nations that adapt to different contexts or user preferences.
Figure 1: Illustration of the hypothesis addressed in this thesis. We propose
studying a system of relationships by crossing the languages of
music, image, and PE, aided by the capacity of computational pro-
cesses to abstract this problem.
Some challenges that emerged during our research included ques-
tions such as: What sort of relevant psycho-physiological correspon-
dences might exist between images and music? What is the minimum
number of features required to represent a set of distinct emotional
states? How can we formalise and bridge existing ﬁndings from mul-
tiple domains? How can we explore audio-visual relationships using
digital tools? And which visual and auditory transformations are rel-
evant for the computational generation of cross-modal associations?
As a result, the following research questions are proposed to conduct
this work:
rq .1 What sort of aesthetic and affective-perceptual correspondences
may exist between image and music?
– Historically , we can ﬁnd several artistic experiments ex-
ploring the expression and connection between music and
image elements (Brougher, 2005; Moritz, 1996), as subjec-
tive as these processes may be. However, recent ﬁndings
propose an empirical way of bridging the two domains
through emotions and perception (Lindborg and Friberg,
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

1.2w o r k i n g h y p o t h e s i s 3
2015; Van Campen, 2010). Without disregarding historical
experiments on art and its expressive impact, we focus
on studying the possibility of connecting the two domains
through the perception of emotions.
rq .2 How can we map image and music computationally?
– The speed and modularity of current technology have al-
lowed new forms of creation, together with an ability to in-
terpret and evolve abstract concepts with some degrees of
autonomy (Boden, 2009; Boden and Edmonds, 2009; Noll,
1966; Sims, 1992). The expressive capacity of computers
opens new doors to developing and exploring creative lan-
guages such as images and music. Driven by the computa-
tional potential of generative processes (McCormack, Dorin,
and Innocent, 2004; Pocock-Williams, 1992; Rebelo, Bicker,
and Machado, 2020), we propose studying creative tools
as a mighty force in bridging the perceptual qualities of
visual and auditory languages. On the one hand, we han-
dle the challenge of abstract representation and transposi-
tion of knowledge between multiple domains. On the other
hand, we handle the challenge of balancing freedom with
rigid rules, a topic long discussed in the ﬁeld of computa-
tional creativity (CC) (Boden, 2009).
rq .3 How can we systematise theoretical knowledge for the compu-
tational production of audio-visual relationships?
– Crossing the knowledge from several domains is essen-
tial for developing multidisciplinary design artefacts. How-
ever, there is no standard way to formalise the knowledge
from several domains (Colton, Charnley , and Pease, 2011).
To this end, we propose designing a model to clarify the
components of our problem and the interactions between
these components. By delineating a set of principles and
rules, we enable the integration of knowledge from vari-
ous domains and provide a structured methodology for
designing and creating audio-visual language mediated by
emotions.
rq .4 Can evolutionary processes be used to explore audio-visual re-
lationships that ﬁt speciﬁc criteria?
– The plasticity and efﬁcacy of Evolutionary Algorithms (EA)
have been recognised as useful in solving various prob-
lems, including those with large solution spaces and a more
subjective nature (Bentley and Corne, 2002). Speciﬁcally ,
they have proven suitable for solving creative and interdis-
ciplinary problems, such as music composition and art gen-
eration. As described in (Bentley and Corne, 2002; Koza,
2003), given the complexity and subjectivity of creative
problems, Evolutionary Algorithms (EA) offer a way to in-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

4i n t r o d u c t i o n
put a high-level problem statement and generate a satisfac-
tory solution, regardless of the domain.
Hence, we propose to study computational expressions in-
spired by natural algorithms to explore the relationships
that may emerge from computationally crossing visual and
auditory languages. By iteratively generating and evaluat-
ing new relationships between music and image character-
istics, EA shall be able to identify meaningful connections
and generate new solutions that meet speciﬁc design crite-
ria.
rq .5 Is it possible to give users the capacity to intervene in the
process and approximate the artefact outcomes to their prefer-
ences?
– We assemble a set of user tests in two phases to test the
system’s expressive performance. For the ﬁrst phase, we as-
semble a test to assess the user’s emotion perception of mu-
sic and image generator. In the second phase, we propose
an appreciation of the users’ relationship/experience with
the system by providing an interaction (Sims, 1992; Yan-
nakakis, Liapis, and Alexopoulos, 2014) that allows them
to approximate the representation of music-image pairs
based on their perception. We rely on CC to improve the
efﬁciency and effectiveness of the user testing process, as
well as to generate solutions that support human creativ-
ity and help us further evaluate their decision-making pro-
cesses in the context of A VR (Kantosalo, Linkola, and Män-
nistö, 2022; Maher et al., 2018).
1.3 research goals and methodology
During the course of this investigation, we have deﬁned a set of goals
and principles to guide our work.
We constructed a solid theoretical basis by analysing the history
of cross-modal associations (CMA), or how authors and artists have
been exploring mappings between sound and image. Likewise, have
created our design tools to transform and manipulate multidimen-
sional data and investigate it in creative ways (Grill and Flexer, 2012;
Moroni et al., 2014; Whitney ,1994). This has two advantages over tra-
ditional graphic design processes. On one side, this approach proves
to be robust when testing a set of design prototype variations, as we
can perform a wide range of tests in a short time frame. On the other
side, these personalised digital systems complement processes of hu-
man creativity (Yannakakis, Liapis, and Alexopoulos, 2014).
Combining the principles of design with computational processes
of creativity , we generate an abstract language to explore relation-
ships between the domains of Music and Image. This exploration
seeks to provide new possibilities in the expression of audiovisual
mappings at a perceptual-affective level. Furthermore, to rule out
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

1.3r e s e a r c h g o a l s a n d m e t h o d o l o g y 5
bias induced by a semantic approach (e.g., a "red" apple), we limit
our study to an abstract expression of images and music and restrain
it to Western culture.
1.3.1 Objectives and Principles
To systematically conduct our hypothesis and ensure that the devel-
oped prototypes do not interfere negatively with our research experi-
ences, we outline the following objectives and principles.
Objectives
• Characterise the elements that compose visual and auditory lan-
guages and identify a common vocabulary among these;
• Identify the Visual and Auditory features that have greater rele-
vance in the human perception of music or image;
• Demonstrate how to map visual and auditory stimuli compu-
tationally; in particular, develop a computational approach for
the generation of images and music based on PE;
• Conduct experiments on computational methods to explore the
audio-visual interplay/relationship, with emphasis on evolu-
tionary processes;
• Draw a framework that systematises theoretical ﬁndings from
multiple domains into a computational approach;
• Develop a system that can perform translation in both direc-
tions — Music to Image (MI) and vice-versa;
• Demonstrate that our system can adapt to the problem in differ-
ent contexts, for example, to different instances of emotions;
• Create pairs of music-image that approximate to the user’s pref-
erences through a co-creative approach;
• Analyse the user interactions to gain a better understanding of
the user’s perceptual motivations. In addition, assess the user’s
satisfaction with the process.
Principles
• The scope of this work is limited to Western cultural ﬁndings;
• Music and Image expressions should convey similar emotions;
• The generation of images should not be focused on a direct
translation of the temporal dimension of music but instead on
the global perceived expression, which by nature is subjective
and cumulative;
• Artefacts should maintain consistency between the aesthetic prop-
erties of image and music, preventing music and image from
interfering negatively in the experience;
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6i n t r o d u c t i o n
• A balance between the rigid rules of an algorithm and the free-
dom of creativity should be fostered;
• In the interactive prototypes, a natural and intuitive interaction
should be promoted;
• The audio-visual language should be deﬁned from two points
of view: informal and formal. The informal regards the creative
and aesthetic representation of the languages, or in other words,
its formalisation. The formal perspective regards the program-
ming language used for its materialisation.
To meet the identiﬁed objectives and guidelines, we have adopted
a Design Science Research (DSR) methodology , which offers speciﬁc
guidelines for evaluation and iteration within research projects (Hevner
et al., 2004). This is particularly suitable for this project, as we aim to
achieve new knowledge and better understand the problem through
the iterative development of prototypes and the analysis of their per-
formance. The various stages of the process were validated through
the development of computational tools and user tests.
1.4 contributions
This investigation contributes to a better understanding of the cre-
ative processes behind the computational exploration of Audio-Visual
Associations (A V A) while providing the scientiﬁc foundations for re-
solving cross-modal context problems in a novel way . Furthermore,
results suggest the artefact’s plasticity expresses and evolves an inter-
disciplinary language, which can be used in multiple design contexts.
This results in key contributions to the domains of Creativity Sup-
port Tools (CST) and Evolutionary Computation (EC) and enhances
future developments in Digital Media Design and other multidisci-
plinary contexts. We hereby describe the main contributions of this
thesis: 1.
rq .1a ) literature review We conducted multidisciplinary research
where we gathered several works and processes performed in
the areas of Abstract Art (see Section 3.2), Cross-Modal Associa-
tions (see Section 3.3), audio-visual language and Relationships
(see Chapters 2, 3, and 4), Emotions Perception (see Sections 4,
6.2, and 6.4), Computer Music (see Section 5.1), Computer Art
(see Section 5.1), Computational Creativity (see Section 5.2) and
Nature-Inspired Computing (see Sections 5.3 and Chapter 8).
rq .1b ) cross -modal dataset To the best of our knowledge, lim-
ited research has been performed previously to study the rela-
tionship among three areas: music, image and emotions. We
have extended the depth of our research, compared to other
1 The contributions items (e.g. RQ. 1 a) and RQ. 1 b)) have been organised according to
research questions’ groups (RQ.1; RQ.2; etc.)

ies into an extensive dataset of relationships between Emotions-

vided a solid basis for future cross-modal experiments involv-
ing visual and auditory expressions grounded in affective-perceptual
knowledge. Moreover, we have made the resulting dataset avail-

We better understood the practical application of emotion per-
ception to cross-media design problems in media-art installa-

rq .3f r a m e w o r kWe conceptualised a computational framework
to support the A V language. Given the complexity of crossing
domain knowledge, we divided the problem into several com-
ponents, clarifying which theoretical elements should be part
of the solution and formalising the interactions between com-
ponents of the framework (see Chapter 7). The overall system
was based on four key modules: vocabulary set, music genera-
tor, image generator, and evolutionary engine. While the ﬁrst al-
lowed us to deﬁne the problem, the second and third were used
to generate a ﬂexible visual and musical language dynamically .
Finally , the fourth was used to explore associations through evo-
lutionary techniques. This model conceptualisation resulted in
the following communication:
• Rodrigues, A., Cardoso, A., & Machado, P . ( 2020). T owards
a computational framework for cross-modal associations: Image,
Music, and Emotions . European Conference on Artiﬁcial In-
telligence (ECAI), Art and Artiﬁcial Intelligence Workshop.
rq .4a ) evolutionary processes for the audio -visual interplay
We studied several evolutionary processes to uncover those that
would allow interactions between the domains’ elements and
enhance the expression of Audio-Visual (A V) languages. In par-
ticular, we explored Artiﬁcial Life environments and Genetic
Algorithms (GA), contributing to a better understanding of in-
teractive guided algorithms for A V artefacts (see Chapter8). The
following publications resulted from this research:

Cham.

monic constellation: An audiovisual environment of living or-

rq .4b ) artefact for adaptive av relationships Through the
capacity of EA to transform and manipulate multidimensional
data, we investigated the computational exploration of Cross-
Modal Associations founded on Genetic Programming (GP) (see
Chapters 9 and 10). Here, we achieved a solution that would
evolve a set of associations, departing from theoretical ﬁndings
and steered by the perceived emotions of A V relationships. This
work, together with the previous contribution, resulted in the

rq .5c o -creative tool We developed an interactive artefact with
a ﬁtness function that adapts dynamically by combining user in-
puts based on their perception and theoretical ﬁndings. In par-
ticular, we focused on developing partially-guided EA for cre-
ative purposes. This way , the user could redeﬁne or reorganise
the corresponding weight of each feature, allowing the system
to learn with the user and interactively evolve the audiovisual
language. This had two contributions: ﬁrst, the generation of
mappings that suit the preferences of the user; second, the anal-
ysis of interactions towards a better understanding of the user’s
perceptual motivations (see Chapters 7, 9 and 10).
1.5 document outline
The present document is divided into three major parts: (i) a litera-
ture study on visual and auditory relationships mediated by affective-
perceptual ﬁndings; (ii) computational generation and abstract repre-
sentation of associations between the domains of image and music;
and development of a framework that systematises the correspon-
dences between these domains and their integration into a compu-
tational approach; (iii) evolution, exploration, and assessment of the
resulting A VL.
In Part i, we conduct multidisciplinary research to gather theoret-
ical foundations on cross-modal associations, A V relationships, and
emotion perception. By analysing the history of cross-modal associ-
ations and how several authors and artists have explored mappings
between music and image elements, we established a basis for devel-
oping perceptual-affective relationships.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

10 introduction
In Part ii, we describe a preliminary investigation of computational
approaches towards the expression of A V environments and some in-
teractive approaches for this interplay . This resulted in a series of ex-
perimental prototypes in which we explored the translation of music
properties into visual ones, namely computer techniques for music or
image generation driven by perceptual ﬁndings. Both the theoretical
and practical knowledge developed at this stage had an essential role
in future iterations of this work.
At the end of Part ii, we discuss the design of a computational
framework to support a systematised development of the audio-visual
language. Findings from Part i constituted the vocabulary of three ma-
jor alphabets: visual elements, auditory elements, and basic emotions.
In Part iii, we discuss the use of nature-inspired algorithms to trans-
form and manipulate multidimensional data, and evolve cross-modal
associations co-creatively . Here, we applied the knowledge of previ-
ous explorations to build an Evolutionary Engine based on Genetic
Programming (GP) and achieved a computational solution for cross-
domain exploration.
By following this structured approach, we aim to contribute to the
understanding and advancement of audiovisual language, bridging
the gap between multiple disciplines and fostering advances in CC.

2THE ELEMENTS OF MUSIC AND IMAGE
Noam Chomsky , an important ﬁgure in social and cognitive sciences,
deﬁned the verbal language as "an alphabet (i.e. a ﬁnite set of sym-
bols out of which its sentences are constructed) and its grammatical
sentences" (Chomsky , 1956). When considering Auditory and Visual
languages, which are central to this thesis, the complexity of under-
standing these forms can sometimes surpass that of verbal languages.
However, when adequately conveyed, they can be rich sources for
communication and hold the capacity to achieve more expressive and
comprehensive communication (Marriott and Meyer, 1998). To better
understand these languages 1, we begin by analysing the elements
that compose music and image and discuss some essential aspects of
how humans interpret and understand them 2.
2.1 the language of music
Music, by deﬁnition, is a complex and abstract form of communica-
tion. However, it can achieve several purposes given speciﬁc rules and
structure. Thomas et al. deﬁne music as a set of "codes with rules gov-
erning the combination of its elements" (Thomas et al., 1995). Adorno
et al. state that "no music exists without expressive elements, and
in music, even expressionless become an expression" (Adorno and
Gillespie, 1993). This statement reinforces the shared idea that music
goes beyond a logical organisation and combination of sounds as it
can mediate and communicate a speciﬁc intention. Examples of this
throughout history are the Egyptian scale system directly associated
with the days of the week and the planets and the Greek modes to
reﬂect the ethical characteristics of a tribe (Thomas et al., 1995).
Similarities of music with verbal language have been associated
with the temporal organisation of sounds and the individual expres-
sion of the auditory elements (Adorno and Gillespie, 1993). For ex-
ample, in Western culture, a musical language can be characterised
by a structured succession of sentences or phrases, periods and punc-
tuation (see Figure 2). Each phrase comprises musical intervals with
a "semantic" and contextual "meaning".
Whether music is composed or improvised, it can be conceived as a
hierarchy of parameters or organisational levels. Here, some proper-
ties (such as pitch, duration and timbre) are considered fundamental,

The second one is formal and regards the software implementation and computer
languages used to generate and display music or images. The following chapters ( 2,
3, and 4) focus on the informal perspective.
2 This chapter aims to contribute to research question 1: What sort of aesthetic and
affective-perceptual correspondences may exist between image and music?

and others (such as melody , harmony , rhythm, and instrumentation)
are regarded as conceptual or historically/culturally speciﬁc (Black-
well, Granulator, Raidl, et al., 2004).
Through simple combinations of melodic and harmonic intervals,
rhythms, and tonal modes, music is thriving at communicating emo-
tions (anger, sadness, etc.) or sensations (motion, tension, balance,
etc.) (Thomas et al., 1995). For example, it is known in Western cul-
tures that certain intervals ( 2nds, 7ths, augmented 4ths) provoke ten-
sion in the listener, while others ( 3rds, 6ths, 4ths, 5ths, and octaves)
provide a sense of resolution. This leads to the following questions:
What are the elements of music? What is the perceptual impact of
the elements that compose a piece of music? Are there elements that,
when combined, transmit speciﬁc sensations? In the following sub-

2.1.1 Music Elements
At the physical level, a sound can be physically characterised by
four main parameters: frequency , amplitude, duration and timbre.
Frequency determines the pitch of the sound (Platzer and Almeida,
2001), where tones become higher as it increases (Collopy , 2000). Am-
plitude determines how loud the sound is. Duration is a relation be-
tween sound and silence. It begins as soon as we hear a sound until
it goes silent again. Timbre allows us to distinguish different kinds of
sound emitters, e.g., to determine a ﬂute from a piano (Platzer and
Almeida, 2001).
If we consider more abstract levels, psychoacoustic features 3 can re-
gard Tempo, Dynamics, Articulation, Timbre, Pitch, Interval, Melody ,
Harmony , Tonality , Rhythm, and Form (Platzer and Almeida, 2001).
Notes, or pitched sounds, are the essential elements of most music.
Three of the most critical perceptual features characterise them are
pitch, duration and volume. Pitch is a perceptual property of sound
that determines its highness or lowness. Duration refers to how long
or short a musical note is. Volume relates to the loudness or intensity
of a tone. When melody and harmony are organised under a promi-
nent tonal centre, it is called a tonal music (predominant in current
Western cultures). By combining particular pitches and hierarchies
we are able to favour speciﬁc states of stability/relaxation or instabil-
ity/tension in music (McDermott and Oxenham, 2008).
Even though the concepts of harmony and progression do not ap-
ply in an atonal context, the quality of the sounding of two or more
tones usually strongly depends on the formal and harmonic musi-
cal contexts in which it occurs. This quality is generally classiﬁed as
consonance. Consonance is a context-dependent concept that refers to
two or more simultaneous sounds combined in a pleasant/agreeable
unity of sound. On the other side, dissonance describes tension in
the sound, as if sounds or pitches did not blend and remain sepa-
rate auditory entities (Dillen, 2020). Anyway , consonance is a relative
3 While physical sound characteristics are objective, psychoacoustic tend to have a
subjective nature.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

16 the elements of music and image
concept with several levels of consonance/dissonance. Although con-
sonance refers to simultaneous sounds, it may also be applied to two
successive sounds due to the memory retention of the ﬁrst sound
while the second is heard.
The difference between two pitches is called interval. Intervals may
be harmonic (two simultaneous tones) or melodic (two successive
tones). In tonal music theory , intervals are classiﬁed as perfect con-
sonants (perfect unison and perfect 4th, 5th and 8th intervals), im-
perfect consonants (major and minor 3rd and 6th) and dissonances
(all the others) (Dillen, 2020).
According to (Cariani, 2019), the musical attributes for musical
composition and improvisation can be divided into three main group-
ings, as illustrate in Figure 5:
• Basic variables: texture, dynamics (intensity), range (pitch), tim-
bre (tone colour), rhythm (duration, tempo, meter), harmony
(consonance/dissonance), melody , form, mood;
• Melodic aspects: shape, motive, phrase, manipulations (para-
phrase/extend, invert/reverse/sequence/pattern, augment/di-
minish), approaches (scalar/modal, chordal, intervallic);
• Structural aspects: background/foreground activity , similar/com-
plementary/contrary activity , tension/release, silence/sound, im-
itation, repetition, variation, density .
Figure 5: Overview of the elements and structure of auditory languages.
Chapters 4, 6, and 9 further explore and detail the music language
elements within the scope of this thesis.
2.1.2 Principles of Auditory Perception
Auditory perception can be deﬁned as "the organisation and interpre-
tation of sensory information received through the ear" (APA, 2008).
Our interpretation of these sounds depends on a combination of fac-
tors such as an individual’s culture, social context, and personality
(Juslin, 2013) but also on how the auditory system encodes the acous-
tic information (McDermott and Oxenham, 2008).
At the physical level, when an object vibrates, its movements “make
both the basilar membrane and the tectorial membrane ﬂex up and
down. These waves will stimulate receptor cells in our ears that will
be perceived as sounds” (see Figure 6) (Carlson, 2013).
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

2.1 the language of music 17
Figure 6: Auditory information processing. Sound waves coming from a
sound source triggers changes in the air pressure. (a) These waves
are funnelled via the external ear (pinna) (b), and then the tym-
panic member or eardrum transmits the sound from the air to the
(c) ossicles inside the middle ear, which converts and ampliﬁes
vibration in air to vibration in ﬂuid. (d) Next, the sound passes
through the inner ear where the hair cells of the cochlea form
synapses to (e) bring auditory information into the brain (Cari-
ani, 2019; Carlson, 2013).
We describe the most relevant dimensions of auditory feature per-
ception: Melody , Harmony , Dynamics, Texture, Temporal Organisa-
tion, and Rhythm.
• Melody: When adjacent notes played in a sequence are too short
(less than 100ms), they fuse, whereas long notes (more than
2s) hardly form a uniﬁed pattern (Cariani, 2019). As for pitch
height, higher frequencies and higher musical melodic lines are
our ﬁrst level of sound attention, and next in our attention, low
frequencies (bass notes) (Sonnenschein, 2001). The human brain
encodes and remembers the relationships between the pitches,
being particularly sensitive to whether the pitch increases or
decreases from one note to another.
• Harmony: Concerns the simultaneous combination of notes, shap-
ing the interplay of chords and how music is expressed (McDer-
mott and Oxenham, 2008), having the potential to evoke emo-
tions or deepen the listener’s engagement with the music. To
perceive a consonant chord, it is agreed among authors and mu-
sicians that two notes with simple frequency ratios (consonant
intervals) — 2:1 octave, 3:2 ﬁfth, 4:3 fourth — produce the sensa-
tion of blending smoothly and harmoniously and therefore they
induce relaxation, while those with more complex ratios (disso-
nant intervals) — 16:15 minor 2nd, tritone — sound rougher,
discordant and thus tense (Cariani, 2019; Carlson, 2013). How-
ever, cultural expectations may also affect consonance percep-
tion (Cariani, 2019).
• Dynamics: The perception of loudness can be impacted by the
duration of a sound (Sonnenschein, 2001). From a physics per-
spective, the human ear can detect changes as small as 1 dB
(12%) in amplitude (Cariani, 2019).
• Timbre: Relates to the information on the nature of a particular
sound (Cariani, 2019). “The auditory system can discriminate
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

18 the elements of music and image
between sounds with different timbres by detecting the individ-
ual overtones of each instrument and produce unique patterns
of neural ﬁring in the auditory system” (Carlson, 2013).
• T emporal Organisation: The auditory system is constantly build-
ing up a representation of sound patterns by comparison with
patterns of incoming auditory events. The time dimension in
music reﬂects the properties of the auditory short-term mem-
ory . More speciﬁcally , a set of expectations can be conﬁrmed or
violated, i.e., if the patterns are consistent with the current repre-
sentation, the auditory image is reinforced, if incoming patterns
diverge from expectations, these patterns are ampliﬁed and new
expectations are created from the violations. Manipulation of ex-
pectations is an efﬁcient way of achieving the tension-relaxation
effects (Cariani, 2019). 4.
Rhythm involves the perception of temporal patterns of events;
it allows time intervals to be subdivided into shorter durations
without breaking higher-level temporal relations (Cariani, 2019).
We may apply the gestalt principles (usually applied to visual per-
ception) (Attneave, 1954) to music concepts to better clarify the mech-
anisms handled by our brain to analyze auditory information. We
hereby describe some of the gestalt principles applied to music.
• Proximity: The law of proximity explains how the brain clus-
ters nearby objects. Applying this law to music means that adja-
cent sound elements tend to be grouped as single sound objects.
However, the aggregation of time-close texture sounds is not a
simple sum of the individual sounds (Sonnenschein, 2001).
• Similarity: The law of similarity states that similar sounds are
grouped even when separated by time. In music, similarities are
more likely to have stronger bonds with timbre than with pitch.
If a distinction is desired, it’s better to avoid similar timbres
(Sonnenschein, 2001). For a sequence of identical notes, group-
ings can be created by changes in any perceptual attribute: loud-
ness, timbre, pitch, and duration (Cariani, 2019). In turn, repeti-
tion is a powerful way of establishing expectations.
• Continuity: In music, the principle of continuity relates to detect-
ing changes in some musical aspects. Smooth and continuous
changes in the frequency , intensity , location, or spectrum will
be perceived as being part of the same trajectory . If the change
is abrupt, a new trajectory is activated. The principle of continu-
ity provides then the sense of integration. However, there is an
additional difﬁculty in perceiving changes in sound increases
at very low loudness levels since they are harder to detect (Son-
nenschein, 2001).
4 The apparent passage of time can be affected by music by slowing down or speeding
up a scene. Nonetheless, we can only interpret acoustic information to a certain
speed. To perceive different sound qualities, we need at least 0.013 seconds for pitch,
0.05 seconds for loudness, and 0.1 seconds for timbre (Sonnenschein, 2001)
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

2.2 the language of image 19
• Symmetry: It is more challenging to perceive acoustic symmetry
than visual symmetry . For this reason, it is rarely encountered
in the acoustic world. Nonetheless, some experimentation can
be found with melodies (Mongoven and Carbon, 2017).
• Closure: The law of closure states that the mind will tend to
unite two disconnected lines lying along the same trajectory .
The mind likes to complete patterns, so if a melody or cyclic
sound has paused before completing its intended trajectory , we
will feel tension and conﬂict (Sonnenschein, 2001).
• Figure and Ground : This law relates to our ability to separate
a ﬁgure from the background. For example, in music, it can
happen when a frequency or timbre pops out differently from
the ground, as, for example, “a soft, quirky sound of a buzzing
insect inside a quiet building” (Sonnenschein, 2001).
2.1.2.1 Cultural aspects
Western tonal music’s most common chord trajectories begin by es-
tablishing a tonal centre or base (tonic) and then stepping away from
the stability using more dissonant chords to build tension to return
to the tonic to create relaxation. Stepping away from the tonic creates
tension, and those towards the tonal centre create relaxation. As a
result, the tonic is often the most frequently played note or the note
with the most prolonged duration (Cariani, 2019).
The major triad is considered the most stable chord, followed by mi-
nor triads, and then by diminished and augmented chords” (Cariani,
2019). In addition, natural intervals ( 5:3 major third, 4:3 major fourth)
are considered to have a more relevant role when compared to unnat-
ural intervals (e.g. 13:10 semitones). Yet, the octave is an exception to
this (McDermott and Oxenham, 2008). Music melodies played using
notes within the major mode tend to be interpreted as happy , while
those played with notes of the minor mode usually sound sad (Carl-
son, 2013).
2.2 the language of image
Similarly to music, the visual language can be deﬁned as a set of
elements and combinations - that, when arranged in a speciﬁc way ,
express a "style". Eubanks has suggested that visual language also
shares common elements with verbal language. In this case, the shared
attributes with verbal language rely on "signals or symbols with a con-
ventional meaning and a code system that organises the set of sym-
bols" (Eubanks, 1997). When these symbols and codes are combined,

As Marriott et al. have stated, "one of the main motivations for
visual language research has been to facilitate multi-modal commu-
nication and interaction between humans and computers" (Marriott
and Meyer, 1998). Consequently , research on visual languages has be-
come pivotal when generating visual compositions as they support
the design of these processes and the transposition of the visual lan-
guage into computational artefacts.

Humans naturally tend to translate non-visual ideas into visual
codes [Giannakis and Smith, 2000]. Examples of visual language em-
ployment can be found in pre-history paintings from Mesopotamia
(see Figure 7), the traditional paintings of central Australian aborig-
inean (see Figure 8 and 9), sign languages from deaf communities,
and maps.
Figure 9: Example of Aboriginal Art Symbols. Most of the symbols are rel-
atively simple, but to tell a more complex story , they are used in
more elaborate combinations. Image from the Central Art of Abo-
riginal Art ( Aboriginal Arts in Australia n.d.).
Generally , auditory and visual languages share three components:
form, content and use. The form may include sounds, symbols and
design principles (vocabulary). Content regards the meaning or how
symbols or sequences of sounds are arranged (grammar). As for the
use, it relates to the purpose of communication, the context in which
music or images are expressed (Eubanks, 1997).
2.2.1 Image Elements
In this document, the term “image” is used to refer to a set of ele-
ments (shape, color, texture, etc.), that when placed together, these
elements combine to create the overall composition and contribute to
its perceived meaning.
According to Paul Rand (Rand, 2014), a visual representation in-
cludes formal and psychological materials. The ﬁrst concerns the fol-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

2.2 the language of image 21
lowing attributes: space, contrast, proportion, harmony , rhythm, rep-
etition, line, mass, shape, colour, weight, volume, value, texture, and
order. The second regards aspects regards visual perception, the spec-
tator’s instincts, intuitions, emotions, and the designer’s needs (Rand,
2014). By combining discussions of Paul Rand (Rand, 2014), Christian
Leborg (Leborg, 2006), and Wassily Kandinsky (Kandinsky and Re-
bay ,1979) on the visual elements that compose an image, we suggest
that a visual language can be decomposed into the following groups:
• Basic variables: point, line, size, colour, transparency/opacity ,
shape, thickness;
• Visual aspects or manipulations over the elements: repetition,
rotation, frequency , mirror, direction, augment/diminish, angle
movement, path;
• Structural aspects: groups, direction, position, space/weight/-
mass, balance, quantity , symmetry , attract, neutrality , depth (back-
ground/foreground), variation, distance, parallelism/angle, neg-
ative/positive, overlap, inﬂuence, diffusion, proportion/harmony ,
rhythm, complexity , crowdedness, density , visual distribution.

2.2.2 Principles of Visual Perception
Vision is a naturally rich source of information about the world —
about “ 25% of our cerebral cortex is devoted to the visual system”
(Carlson, 2013). In this sense, visual perception can be deﬁned as "the
awareness of visual sensations that arises from the interplay between
the physiology of the visual system and the internal and external
environments of the observer" (APA, 2018b). In this process of col-
lection and processing, a search for patterns is conducted to identify
meaningful structures (Pettersson, 1993).
Several authors have described a simpliﬁed explanation of how the
brain processes visual information (Carlson, 2013; Ware, 2019): there
are three main stages of processing (see Figure 11): In Stage 1, we ex-
tract basic features from the visual environment simultaneously (or
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

22 the elements of music and image
low-level properties) — such as orientation, colour, texture and move-
ment patterns. In Stage 2, “rapid processes of pattern perception seg-
ment the visual ﬁeld into regions of the same colour, texture, or mo-
tion patterns”. This processing involves both working memory and
long-term memory . In Stage 3, “the information is reduced to only
a few objects held in visual working memory by active mechanisms
of attention to form the basis of visual thinking”, or in other words,
we perceive visual information as having certain aesthetic qualities
(high-level) (Ware, 2019).
Figure 11: Visual information processing. We highlight four key stages of
visual information processing, where different brain areas will
process different tasks. (a) Visual receptors (light wavelength, in-
tensity) (b) Stage 1: Features (c) Stage 2: Patterns; (d) Stage 3:
Visual working memory .
From the neuroscience point of view, it is known that the visual
system is sensitive to a limited number of visual features. Features
such as contours (lines), shapes, colours, depth, texture, movement,
and composition are the "building blocks of our perceptual experi-
ence" (Melcher and Bacci, 2013; Ramachandran and Hubbard, 2001).
Supported by these ﬁndings, we provide details on how the human
brain processes these characteristics at a perceptual level.
For this thesis, we group the visual elements into four perceptual
dimensions: (i) primitive or atomic features (low-level), (ii) composite
or context-depending features (high-level), (iii) manipulations, and
(iv) relationships.
• Low-level features are the basis of our perception. They are easily
perceived and distinguished.
Color: Color perception is determined by an intersection be-
tween three dimensions: hue, saturation, and brightness. These
perceptual dimensions correspond to the physical dimensions
of wavelength, purity , and intensity (see Figure 12) (Carlson,
2013; Ware, 2019). Hue results from the information provided
by three types of cones, which are sensitive to particular wave-
lengths: long, medium, or short. “The peak sensitivities of the

value (lightness).

system.
three types of cones are approximately at 420 nm (blue-violet),
560 nm (green-yellow), and 610 nm (orange-red)” (Carlson, 2013).
Although it has been argued that monochromatic (black-and-
white) vision allows us to create or emphasise the feeling of
tension-relaxation, hue vision gives us the ability to distinguish
and group information more easily (Arnheim, 1965; Carlson,
2013; Ware, 2019). Saturation refers to the “relative purity of the
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

2.2 the language of image 23
light that is being perceived” - if the radiation contains all vis-
ible wavelengths, it will be perceived as white (Carlson, 2013).
Brightness or luminosity refers to the perceived amount of light
coming from a source. “Brightness is a constant property of any
surface” (Arnheim, 1965).
Form: The boundaries determine the shape of objects derived
from the eye’s capacity to distinguish between areas of differ-
ent brightness and colour. It is also known that the “neurons
in the primary visual cortex detecting lines and edges respond
best to sine-wave gratings”, meaning that we experience an ad-
ditional difﬁculty in perceiving shapes when low-frequency in-
formation is removed (Carlson, 2013). The shape is considered
a better way of identifying or distinguishing several distinct ob-
jects than colour. This is due to its capacity to offer more kinds
of qualitative difference (see Figure 13), but also because the
characteristics of shape are hardly affected by environmental
variations opposite to colour brightness and colour hue (Arn-
heim, 1965; Ware, 2019).

• High-level features may have dynamic interpretations, i.e., de-
pending on the surrounding context or environment, they can
have different interpretations. They refer to a visual composi-
tion’s complex and abstract attributes. They can create a speciﬁc
mood or tone.
• Manipulations: This can involve changing the colour, contrast,
or texture of an image, as well as distorting or warping it. Vi-
sual elements can be manipulated for various purposes in a
visual composition, such as creating a sense of balance, empha-
sising certain elements, or creating contrast. By manipulating
such elements, we can express different visual styles and con-
cepts (Leborg, 2006).
• Relationships: Relationships between elements are, in part, ex-
plained by the Gestalt theory . Gestalt theory emphasises that
the whole is greater than the isolated sum of its parts through
a set of principles. These concepts are brieﬂy described below.
A perspective worth mentioning is Gestalt principles or the laws
of perceptual organisation. They are based on the idea that the hu-
man brain naturally seeks to organise information into patterns rather
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

24 the elements of music and image
than perceiving individual elements in isolation. In the ﬁeld of visual
perception, Gestalt principles such as proximity , similarity , continuity ,
closure, and symmetry (see Figure 14) guide the way elements are
grouped, inﬂuencing how we interpret complex images and scenes.
Equally translating these principles to auditory perception, similar
laws can be observed as described in sub-section 2.1.2.
Figure 14: Visual principles of Gestalt illustrated. (a-b) proximity , (c-f) simi-
larity , (g-j) connectedness, (k - l) continuity , (m-l) symmetry , (o-p)
closure, (q) ﬁgure and ground. Adapted from Ware (Ware, 2019).
• Proximity: Spatial proximity is an efﬁcient way of creating per-
ceptual organisation. This type of grouping principle produces
visual clusters. If there is not enough proximity among ele-
ments, the sensation of the group isn’t perceived (Ware, 2019).
• Similarity: Similar elements tend to be grouped. Each aspect of
visual perception — shape, size, brightness, colour, texture, spa-
tial location, movement, etc. — can cause grouping sensation
through the similarity of visual features (Arnheim, 1965).
• Connectedness: Connecting elements is a powerful grouping prin-
ciple, and it has been suggested that it is stronger than proxim-
ity , size, colour, or shape. It can be a powerful way to express
relationships between graphical objects than features of proxim-
ity , colour, size, or shape (Ware, 2019).
• Continuity: The principle of continuity states that “we perceive
two distinct visual elements as one when they contain a smooth
and continuous transition”, as opposed to the ones that contain
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

2.2 the language of image 25
abrupt changes (Ware, 2019). A smooth continuity will provide
a sense of unity among elements, while abrupt changes will
result in a disconnection between elements.
• Symmetry: Symmetry is considered aesthetically pleasing (Ra-
machandran and Hirstein, 1999). It can provide a powerful or-
ganisation of elements in a visual composition when compared
to asymmetrically positioned shapes. However, it is important
for patterns to remain small (the number of visual objects should
not be higher than 20). Otherwise, symmetry perception may be
compromised (Ware, 2019).
• Closure: It has been suggested that closed contours are more
likely to be perceived as objects. This can be explained by the
human brain’s natural “perceptual tendency” to close contours
with gaps in them (Ware, 2019). A closed contour will be seen
as an object, and when there are gaps in the contours, the eye
interprets them as the contours of a shape.
• Figure and Ground : “A surrounded surface tends to be seen as
a Figure and the unbounded surrounding one as ground. If we
try to reverse the spatial situation, we may experience a strong
resistance” (Arnheim, 1965; Ramachandran and Hirstein, 1999).
Figure 15:
Spectrum of
abstraction,
McCloud (1994).
We would also like to introduce the concept of abstraction (Figure

abstract can be seen as a way to focus on speciﬁc details rather than
eliminating details – i.e. “stripping down an image to its essential
‘meaning” (McCloud and Martin, 1993). Speciﬁcally , we ﬁnd this pro-
cess useful to represent the subjective states of emotions (Melcher and
Bacci, 2013).

In essence, abstract forms of visual communication have been widely

derlying processes behind abstract expressions were explored in the
ﬁrst instance by multidisciplinary artists like Larry Poons and later
by artists who built customised mechanical tools like the ones men-
tioned in the next Chapter 3, and also later by computer artists (see
Chapter 5). For a detailed approach and process for generating ab-
stract images, please consult Chapter 6.
2.2.2.1 Cultural aspects
It has been proven that cultural aspects have a certain inﬂuence on
the way a person appreciates aesthetics (Leder et al., 2004). Multiple
cultural evidence suggests that certain colour hues — speciﬁcally , red,
green, yellow, and blue — have different interpretations depending
on the cultural beliefs/connotations. That is, color can be used a mean
of communication. As an example, Eskimos have different words in
their vocabulary to refer to different tonalities of white — in this

2.3 abstract forms of audio -visual communication
Abstract forms of A V communication can be found in different con-
texts (e.g. social and artistic). For example, the Golden Record - car-
ried by V oyager - can be considered an informal A V language, al-
though it is not a conventional language with grammatical rules and
syntax. In this artefact, various sounds, music, and images were se-
lected to portray Earth’s diversity of life and culture. This suggests
that audio and visual representations can communicate meaning or
emotions without a speciﬁc linguistic structure (see Figure 18).

In a creative tools context, an example of A V communication can
be experienced in "Patatap" from Brandel ( 2014) and Lullatone, an
instrument that transforms touch into expressive abstract visuals and
melodic compositions (see Figure 19). Several other Visual Music
(VM) instruments have been created, such as "The Manual Input
Workstation" by Golan Levin or "Mycenae Alpha" by Iannis Xenakis
(Levin and Brain, 2021).
2.4 summary
Understanding how to represent and express music or image is cru-
cial for developing cross-modal artefacts. However, the expression of
music or image is not a mere sum of its characteristics. It depends
on many factors, such as culture, personal interests, physical aspects,
and how these properties are processed at cognitive levels. As we fo-
cused on generating perceptually relevant A V artefacts, we provide
an overview of the different types of auditory-visual relationships
found in the literature in the next chapter.

3AUDIO-VISUAL CORRESPONDENCES
Our brains are wired to make connections between what we see and
what we hear. It’s even common for humans to associate certain types
of images with speciﬁc types of music and vice versa. For example,
the rhythm and tempo of a piece of music might suggest quick move-
ments in an image, and the colours and textures of the images might
suggest the timbre and tone of the music 1.
3.1 two perspectives : metaphor and cognition
The relationship between image and music is complex and varied
and can be inﬂuenced by various factors, including the individual
viewer’s personal experiences and preferences.
Two perspectives stand out in creating mappings between audi-
tory and visual modalities: metaphor and cognition. The ﬁrst follows
from theories of metaphor, connotation, and association. In this per-
spective, an individual’s experience is conveyed without conscious
or empirical reasoning (Arnheim, 1965). The second, a more recent
process, employs scientiﬁc approaches to follow theories of physical,
neurological and cognitive processes (Day, 1996). While metaphorical
approaches have a more signiﬁcant underlying expressive power, cog-
nitive correspondences provide essential cues for developing suitable
artefacts compatible with our perception. We argue that both are im-
portant for developing and exploring auditory-visual relationships.
3.2 abstract art and visual music
3.2.1 Color, Shape, Sound
The ﬁrst experiments and artworks exploring correspondences be-
tween music and image were proposed by Pythagoras, Aristotle, New-
ton, Castel, and Ernst Chladni. Pythagoras studied the analogy be-
tween tone and light-led vibrations, leading him to imagine the "Mu-
sic of the Spheres" (Snibbe and Levin, 2000).
Figure 20:
Newton’s wheel of
sound and colour
(1704).
Aristotle tried to draw correspondences between the harmony of
sounds and the harmony of compound colours. Newton proposed
a connection of sound and vision through the wave properties of
colour (red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo, and violet) in light
and pitch in music (C, D, E, F, G, A, B) (see Figure 20) (Evans, 2005).
Castel constructed an instrument that played visual and auditory mu-
sic simultaneously , "Clavecin" (Mason, 1958). Scriabin wrote a piece
with a cross-modal interrelation, "Prometheus" (Marks, 1996), which
1 This chapter aims to contribute to research question 1: What sort of aesthetic and
affective-perceptual correspondences may exist between image and music?
27
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

28 audio -visual correspondences
was to be played along with a visual display produced by a colour
keyboard (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007). Lastly , Ernst Chladni’s
most known achievement was the invention of Cymatics. It geometri-
cally showed the various types of vibration on a rigid surface (Monoskop,
n.d.).
By the early twentieth century , abstract painters such as Klee and
Kandinsky tried to express the relationship between music and image
in abstract paintings. These artists believed that certain combinations
of colour, form, and movement emphasised the A V experience. They
performed several experiments, generally consisting of a direct trans-
lation from Music to Image (MI), i.e., a direct mapping between a set
of auditory and visual properties.
Kandinsky’s paintings strongly explored the connection between
form, sound and colour (Grill and Flexer, 2012). As Snibbe says, he
believed that certain combinations of colour and light could bring
spiritually transcendent experiences (Snibbe and Levin, 2000). His
two-dimensional paintings often characterized music visually into a
series of colour combinations and object transformations like permu-
tation, expansion and contraction (Kandinsky and Rebay , 1979). For
example, in his book "Concerning the Spiritual in Art" he wrote some
of his mappings: "A light blue is like a ﬂute, a darker blue a cello;
still darker a thunderous double bass; and the darkest blue of all is
an organ" (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007).
Kandinsky was interested in the analogous relationship between
music and painting. His artwork can be described as an interplay
of form, colour and expression. He defended that while form can
stand alone as a representation of an object or as an abstract limit
to a space or surface, colour cannot stand alone (Kandinsky and Re-
bay ,1979). His work was inﬂuenced by the atonal music of composer
Arnold Schoenberg (McDonnell, 2007). , who produced a musical
drama, "The Yellow Sound", in which colours and sounds were com-
bined(Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007). An explanation for Kandin-
sky’s focus on colours is that he was synaesthetic; he saw colours
corresponding to the sounds of different instruments and used those
correspondences in his paintings.
Figure 21: "Fugue
in Red", Paul Klee
(1921).
Paul Klee often used music as a framework to explore his artistic
ideas. Unlike Kandinsky , he was interested in the traditional harmony
and counterpoint of 18th-century music composition and in formulat-
ing a vocabulary of abstract art comparable to the rules and structures
of music composition. He used colour to create harmony in his work.
One of his methods was to overlay colours and intermingle shapes
and forms to create a rhythm and direction with pictorial elements
(see Figure 21) (McDonnell, 2007). However, Paul Klee was cautious
about making mappings between colour and music that were too lit-
eral or direct (Collopy , 2000).
Besides paintings, another form of visual expression was explored
within contemporary music, graphic notation . Graphic notation did
emerge as a form of music representation and visualisation, which
can be considered an extension of traditional music notation. The
musical score’s graphic effectiveness was central to Western classical
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

3.2 abstract art and visual music 29
music’s development. The primary function of graphic notation was
to serve as a diagram or map in the performance of a musical piece
(see Figure 22). This required composers to translate non-visual ideas
into visual symbolism, making them link ears, eyes and hands (Wal-
ters, 1997).
The ﬁrst rumblings of graphic notation were in early twentieth-
century scores incorporating percussion—non-pitched instruments
required only single-line staves. For example, Edgar Varèse Ionisa-
tion’s (1931) score for percussion looks "as graphically sharp as the
music sounds" (Walters, 1997). Another essential reference is John
Cage, who worked on simple pieces. This piece would combine graphic
directness with a decorative charm that may be inspired by Erik
Satie, whose hand-written scores contained decorative illustrations
and imaginative use of lettering and white space (Walters, 1997).
Figure 22: Graphic Notation examples: (left) Franco Donatoni, "Babai";
(right) Udo Kasemets, "Timepiece for a Solo Performer".
Oskar Fischinger transformed the cinematographic animation world
by focusing on the temporal dimension of sound (Evans, 2005). He
pioneered the animation of abstract lines and shapes to music. For in-
stance, he made the ﬁrst color-on-ﬁlm movie in Europe, called "Kreise"
(see Figure 23). This intends to express music in a time-based se-
quence of graphic patterns leading to the emergence of the VM era.
Figure 23: "Kreise"
(circles) by Oskar
Fischinger (1983).
VM takes many forms, including abstract animations, geometric
patterns, or interactive instruments. Its main goal is to create a unique
sensory experience by combining the senses of music and visual art.
3.2.2 The Time Dimension
Walter Ruttman, Norman McLaren, Mary Ellen Blute and Oskar
Fischinger were pioneer artists in abstract animation movies.
Walter Ruttmann used colour as an element in the choreography
that would help structure the ﬁlm, differentiate speciﬁc shapes, move-
ments or repetitions, and establish certain general moods or atmo-
spheres (McDonnell, 2007)
In the 1940s, Oskar Fischinger made cinematographic explorations
between images and sounds, employing traditional animation tech-
niques and creating each artwork frame-by-frame. His ﬁlms relied on
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

30 audio -visual correspondences
an intuitive connection to musical form (Alves, 2005). He would syn-
chronize non-representational graphics and music that appealed di-
rectly to the viewer’s feelings (McDonnell, 2007). One of Fischinger’s
most remarkable works is a series of ﬁlms in which he represented
music in delicately ﬂuid forms. This series explored studies (see Fig-
ure 24) with the speciﬁc task of focusing on the rhythm and dynamics
of the music to enhance the experience of the abstract elements and
fuse with music expressively (McDonnell, 2007).
Figure 24: "Studies", by Oskar Fischinger ( 1930).
Parallel to Fischinger’s work, in1940, Disney created a movie called
Fantasia, which intended to reﬂect abstract music with abstract im-
ages. With clear and simple animations, Walt Disney hoped people
could see the music and the pictures. Fantasia was built upon Ab-
stract Expressionism principles to express feelings and emotions con-
sonant with music through colour and form.
As Collopy suggests, colour changes can be used to draw atten-
tion to the rhythmic structure of the music. For example, a colour
change can signal each beat, each downbeat, the beginnings of bars,
or accents. Furthermore, they can also be associated with particular
percussive elements (Collopy ,2000).
Another artist worth mentioning is Mary Ellen Bute, known for
her pioneering work in abstract animation and VM. Her ﬁlms were
often described as VM because they aimed to create a synesthetic
experience by combining sound and image to evoke emotions and
sensations in the viewer. She used various techniques, from animation
to painting and photogrammetry , to create her abstract imagery and
synchronize it with music (Moritz, 1996).
In short, each VM artist would create an individual abstract lan-
guage based on their interpretation of musical concepts to explore
this relationship without further experimentation. Moreover, some
artists like Bute or Fischinger expressed the common desire to ex-
pand the simple translation and connect the two domains through
perception. However, they did not have the technological means to
achieve it.
Several techniques were approached for this end, ranging from
hand-drawn animations in metric paper to creating custom-built in-
struments such as the "Clavilux" by Thomas Wilfred, the sound spec-
trograph by M. Ellen Bute, and computer programs by John Whitney .
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

3.3c r o s s -modal associations 31
3.2.3 Metaphor-based instruments and computer algorithms
As early as 1944, John Whitney reﬂected on the difﬁculty of achieving
a formalism for colour (DeWitt, 1987). To him, such a direct, synes-
thetic mapping of music’s basic parameters (pitch, loudness, and so
forth), as others had done, wasn’t enough to capture the expressive-
ness of music. He then developed theories that hypothesized that
Pythagorean harmony could be matched in visual art. His anima-
tions would math these theories and result in harmonious patterns
of symmetry at points corresponding to the same ratios that deﬁne
musical consonances (see Figure 25) (Alves, 2005).
Figure 25: "Permu-
tations", John Whit-
ney (1968).
Artists like John Whitney began to make use of computers. Cross-
ing three broad domains (optical, analogue, digital), they believed the
computer was the only instrument for creating music interplaying
with colour and graphic design (Whitney , 1994). On another perspec-
tive, Larry Cuba tried a geometric approach, also aided by digital
tools, performing a series of precisely choreographed rhythmic trans-
formations (Cuba, 1978).
By 1950, Norman McLaren used oscilloscope patterns to generate
abstract images for his animation "Around is Around" (Moritz, 1996)
(see Figure 26). He was also one of the pioneers who explored the art
of making rhythm visible in ﬁlm. By painting directly in the frame
and the sound strip of the ﬁlm, he would create a synthetic synchro-
nised sound with the image and generate sound in real-time.
Figure 26: "Around is Around", by Norman McLaren ( 1953).
The history of VM makes clear the strong inﬂuence that abstract,
surrealist and expressionist art movements had on bringing new ideas
to the relationship between sound and image. Visual artists used
colour-tone analogy and concepts of motion, shape, rhythm, and time.
3.3 cross -modal associations
Cross-modal Associations, according to the deﬁnition from the APA Dic-
tionary of Psychology , has two possible deﬁnitions (APA, 2018a):
1. "The coordination of sensory inputs involving different brain
regions. It is usually required in tasks that involve matching
auditory and visual inputs, tactile and visual inputs, or a similar
combination of cognitive functions."
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

32 audio -visual correspondences
2. "A phenomenon in which the input to one sense reliably gener-
ates an additional sensory output that is usually generated by
the input to a separate sense."
For the purpose of our research, Cross-Modal Associations (CMA)
are interpreted as strong associations between 2 or more sensory in-
puts (Cuskley et al., 2019).
Previous analyses on the perceptual and cognitive association be-
tween auditory and visual modalities predominantly analysed the
relationships between tones and colours. Although tones and colour
attributes have been studied to a greater extent, few attempts to asso-
ciate other visual and Auditory Properties (AP) have been made em-
pirically or documented in little detail (Giannakis and Smith, 2000; Ra-
machandran and Hubbard, 2001). More recently , the relationship be-
tween music and shape has been analysed regarding the role played
by emotions (Albertazzi, Canal, and Micciolo, 2015).
We highlight some concepts and ﬁndings proven so far to clarify
the existing relationships between visual and auditory stimuli from a
cognitive point of view.
3.3.1 The physiological phenomenon of Synesthesia
When stroked by an auditory sensation, some individuals automat-
ically draw a visual experience (Ward, Huckstep, and Tsakanikos,
2006). This is called synesthesia, a sensory-perceptual phenomenon
in which a person experiences sensations in one modality when a
second modality is stimulated (Ramachandran and Hubbard, 2001).
Synaesthesia literally means "experiencing together" (Baron-Cohen,
Wyke, and Binnie, 1987). There are many kinds of Synesthesia. How-
ever, one of the most commonly experienced is sound-colour Synes-
thesia (Ramachandran and Hubbard, 2001). There is some evidence
that people without Synesthesia also report correspondences between
music and colour (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007).
What precisely differentiates a synesthete physically from a non-
synesthete has yet to be fully understood. Some studies have proven
that non-synesthetes and synesthetes use common cognitive and neu-
ral mechanisms when building relations between the auditory and
visual domains (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007; Ward, Huckstep,
and Tsakanikos, 2006). Ward suggested that this kind of Synesthesia
might be present in us all from a young age, which could explain
why most people can generate on-demand visual correspondence to
music (Ward, Huckstep, and Tsakanikos, 2006). However, many open
questions remain open (Hubbard and Ramachandran, 2005).
While Synesthesia is an involuntary , automatic sensory experience,
CMA are not involuntary or automatic. In theory , however, they share
the power of unveiling strong relationships that may exist between
different sensory modalities. Furthermore, "CMA are often shared
across a population, though some may vary cross-culturally ." (Cusk-
ley et al., 2019)
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

3.3c r o s s -modal associations 33
3.3.2 T one (pitch) - Colour Associations
Colour has been regarded as an important attribute in our perception
(Albertazzi, Canal, and Micciolo, 2015). Mudge (Mudge, 1920) con-
ducted a study to generalise relations between tone and colour. The
resulting associations made by the majority of people were as follows:
(i) low tone - dark colours or greys; (ii) medium pitch tone - medium
brightness; (iii) high tone - bright colours or light greys; (iv) C key -
medium brightness and maybe blue; (vi) A key - blue to yellow, (vii)
D minor key - dull or soft colours as pale green, lavender, violet or
greys.
Later, Marks (Marks, 1974) investigated the psychological corre-
spondences between light and sound and the parallels between vi-
sual and auditory dimensions of sensory perception. He claims that
the two fundamental attributes of sense perception are quality and
intensity: The quality in the visual manifestation is related to the
colour (hue), and the auditory manifestation is associated with the
pitch. The intensity of vision corresponds to brightness, and hearing
corresponds to loudness. He proved the existence of some systematic
relation between hue and pitch with an experiment where subjects
had to match the brightness of grey surfaces to pitch. Analysing the
results, he concluded that individuals mapped in creasing pitch into
increasing brightness. Individuals also matched increasing loudness
to increasing brightness, although this last correspondence was not
the more apparent and natural for all the participants (Marks, 1974).
Comparing synesthetes with non-synesthetes, Marks concluded that
most people associate high-pitched sounds with light and low-pitched
sounds with darker colours. He found these same trends in non-
synesthetes when asked to select colours for a pitch or rate some
speciﬁc audiovisual relationships (Marks, 1974).
More recently , Albertazzi (Albertazzi, Canal, and Micciolo, 2015)
referenced other studies where results suggested that music in the
major mode matches lighter colours than in the minor mode. How-
ever, faster music in the major mode matches more saturated and
lighter colours than music in the minor mode. Even when tested with
different cultures, there is generally concordance in associating the
major mode with more saturated, lighter, and yellower colours. In
contrast, in the minor mode, the opposite pattern was expressed.
3.3.3 Sound - Shape Associations
