In 1987, DeWitt (DeWitt, 1987) referenced a study by Schneider, Engel,
and Debener ( 2008), where participants were presented with visual
pictures of objects and recordings of different object sounds. They
had to judge whether the presented sound was appropriate to the
object. Results revealed that responses were faster when the sound
was meant to correspond to the pictured object than when the sound
was not suitable to the pictured object.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

34 audio -visual correspondences
In another study by Helmholtz, he investigated the relationship be-
tween musical harmony and human perception. He concluded that
we appreciate geometric progression in sound frequencies because
our ears seem to produce these overtones without their physical pres-
ence (DeWitt, 1987). Furthermore, sound-shape associations have been
explored by Ramachandran and Hubbard ( 2003). In this experiment,
two images were presented, and participants had to make a corre-
spondence between those images and two words, "kiki" and "bouba".
The results of their study suggest that 98 per cent of all respon-
dents attributed the name Kiki to the sharp image and bouba to
the round image (Figure 27). Furthermore, they concluded that most
participants would make a consistent association even though they
had never seen these stimuli before. The reason for this was that the
sharp changes in the visual direction of the lines in the ﬁgure would
mimic the sharp phonemic inﬂexions of the sound kiki. In contrast,
the rounded contours of the other ﬁgure would make it more like the
rounded auditory inﬂexion of bouba.
3.3.4 Emotion Associations
From a very young age, we can distinguish between a "happy" song
and a "sad" song. Even when we listen to a piece of music from an-
other culture or in another language, we can decode its emotional
meaning (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007). Recent studies on CMA
between music, image, and emotions have emphasised the impor-
tance of a multidisciplinary approach to studying the relationships be-
tween these domains (Cavanaugh, MacInnis, and Weiss, 2015; Melcher
and Bacci, 2013). Speciﬁcally , they suggest certain auditory and vi-
sual combinations of features can inﬂuence expressing speciﬁc emo-
tions. For instance, Albertazzi concluded that musically trained par-
ticipants did focus more on musical characteristics (such as theme,
mode, changes in pitch, etc.), while untrained participants had a
higher focus on emotions (Albertazzi, Canal, and Micciolo, 2015).
While we can ﬁnd many experiments associating musical aspects
with emotions, examples relating emotions to visual elements are
more limited to studying colour (hue, brightness, saturation) and
shape. In general, brighter colours are most likely to be associated
with positive emotions, whereas darker colours are linked to nega-
tive emotions (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007; Marks, 1996). Round
shapes, for instance, have been primarily associated with positive
emotions like calm and pleasure, whereas sharp shapes have been
linked to negative emotions like fear and anger (Cavanaugh, MacIn-
nis, and Weiss, 2015; Ramachandran and Hubbard, 2001).
Other studies have related high loudness and fast tempo with anger
or slow tempo and low loudness with sadness. Positive emotions
such as happiness have been associated with musical features of fast
tempo and major modes, whereas negative emotions have been linked
to minor modes. Perceptual harmonic aspects such as dissonance
have been related to feelings of sadness, excitement, anger and sad-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

3.3c r o s s -modal associations 35
ness. In contrast, consonance has been associated with feelings of hap-
piness, calm, and tenderness, among others (Gabrielsson and Lind-
ström, 2010; Juslin, 2013).
In 2015, Lindborg and Friberg aimed to explore associations be-
tween sound and image at a perceptual level, where they studied
colour and size association with music mediated by emotions. Their
experiments suggested that low energy (perceived as calm or boring)
was expressed through the object’s size with smaller patches and as-
sociated with darker colours towards blue. In contrast, low-tension
music (perceived as easy-going) was associated with lighter colours
(Lindborg and Friberg, 2015).
It is also noteworthy to mention (Cavanaugh, MacInnis, and Weiss,
2015) study on using multiple perceptual dimensions to differenti-
ate emotions. Overall, we can ﬁnd consistent associations between
auditory elements, visual elements and emotions. However, Barbiere
notes that not all sensory correspondences are necessarily emotion-
ally based (Barbiere, Vidal, and Zellner, 2007).
Figure 28: Example of cross-modal associations mediated by emotions.
3.3.5 Cognition-based computer artefacts
In 2012, Grill and Flexer (Grill and Flexer, 2012) investigated percep-
tual relationships between sound and image and then applied it to
a digital media tool. Their goal was to develop an intuitive screen-
based interface representing large collections of sounds, where sound
manipulation would be facilitated by the exploitation of cross-modal
mechanisms of human perception, similar to what we aimed to do in
this work. They used sensory properties shared between sounds and
graphics and constructed a meaningful mapping of auditory to visual
dimensions. However, they restricted their focus to textural sounds,
i.e., those appearing as static events instead of evolving over time.
Speciﬁcally , the visual features of shape, stroke, grid position, and
colour were linked to textural sounds classiﬁed into the following
perceptual categories: smooth/coarse, tonal/noisy , ordered / chaotic,
high/low, and homogeneous/heterogeneous (Grill and Flexer, 2012).
Although they focused on the perceptual expression of sound tex-
tures, they also aimed at building an intuitive and meaningful in-
terface. User tests were conducted, and subjects could successfully
associate sounds with the corresponding graphical representations.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

36 audio -visual correspondences
Other examples of perceptually based tools that relate auditory and
visual dimensions can be found in the work of Lindborg (Lindborg
and Friberg, 2015) and Hamasaki et al. (Hamasaki and Goto, 2013),
who developed interactive visualizations with several layers of infor-
mation but do not visually represent the perceptual relevance of the
musical characteristics.
3.4 discussion
In this chapter, we analysed the history of A V artworks and, sub-
sequently , the types of mappings performed between these two lan-
guages to analyse their existing relationships.
In short, A V computer artefacts have a rich history dating back to
the mid- 20th century . By the 1970s, the development of video tech-
nology and personal computers led to an explosion in the creation of
A V works. The growth of digital media led to further developments,
including creating interactive installations. Here, we discussed early
artworks from Castel, Kandinsky , Fischinger and Whitney , who have
connected music and image metaphorically . Overall, these authors
shared the belief that certain combinations of colour and form could
emphasise the audiovisual experience.
On the other hand, on the cognition side, we discussed several mu-
sical and visual aspects connected through emotions. For example,
happiness has been associated with auditory characteristics such as
fast tempo, major modes, simple harmonies and high pitches (Mudge,
1920). Likewise, bright colours are visual characteristics likely to be
associated with positive emotions (Lindborg and Friberg, 2015).
Analysing the history of A V artworks, we ﬁrst classify the relation-
ship between music and image from two perspectives:
• Metaphoric: In this approach, the relationships are founded on
each author’s interpretation of the elements. They have high ex-
pressive power as they performed several studies on the abstrac-
tion of these languages. However, they often end in straightfor-
ward correspondences between the elements of these two di-
mensions, leading to non-representative mappings from a per-
ceptual point of view.
• Cognitive: The second approach is more concerned with the
development of associations between the two domains from a
cognitive point of view2, which allows the development of more
complex and perceptually signiﬁcant relationships between the
two domains, however in this approach they tend to disregard
design principles.
In a second instance, we group these relationships into three sub-
categories (see Figure 29):
• 1:1 Here, one property of one domain is mapped into one prop-
erty of another domain.
2 we restrain the research scope of this thesis to the study of emotion perception
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

3.5 summary 37
Figure 29: Examples of relationships ( 1:1, m:1, and m:n) between music and
image elements.
For example, pitch octave → color hue or timbre → form (shape)
(examples from the works of M. Ellen Bute and McLaren respec-
tively) (Evans, 2005; Moritz, 1996).
• 1:n / m: 1 Here, several properties of one domain are mapped
into a single property of another domain.
For example, happiness → pitch level + brightness (Lindborg
and Friberg, 2015; Marks, 1996) or loudness + pitch → bright-
ness (Marks, 1996).
• m:n Here, several properties of one domain are mapped into
several properties of another.
For example, sound duration + pitch → length + movement
direction (Evans, 2005; Pocock-Williams, 1992)
Parallel to the second point, we can infer that these relationships
can be explored in different directions: from music to image (M ! I),
image to music (I ! M), or in both directions (M $ I).
3.5 summary
By investigating the possible correspondences between visual and
auditory domains and decomposing them into their fundamental el-
ements (e.g. melody , harmony , rhythm, colour, form, etc.), we could
categorise this relationship into metaphorical and cognitive approaches.
While the metaphorical approach explores A V connections founded
on intuitiveness and cultural context, the cognitive approach seeks to
understand these mappings through a cognitive science lens.
We hypothesise that combining these two approaches could enrich
our comprehension of auditory and visual relationships. As a result,
in the following chapter, we further review the literature on associa-
tions between music, image, and emotions.

4AD A T A S E TO FA U D I O - V I S U A LA S S O C I A T I O N S
MEDIATED BY EMOTIONS
Understanding how to arouse or generate a particular emotion through
music and image (Cavanaugh, MacInnis, and Weiss, 2015; Juslin, 2013;
Lindborg and Friberg, 2015) is crucial for developing multidisciplinary
artefacts. However, the establishment of perceptually relevant map-
pings between music and image is still largely unexplored by design
sciences (Caiola, 2022). To this end, we assemble a set of relationships
between these domain’s properties. Constrained by other scientists’
and artists’ work, we collect a comprehensive set of ﬁndings regard-
ing auditory and visual languages and propose a dataset of associa-
tions mediated by emotions 1.
Overall, in this study , we aim to bring additional knowledge on
cross-modal associations of a creative nature. Additionally , this dataset
would aid in the computational exploration of interdisciplinary dia-
logues (see Part iii). It is noteworthy to clarify that we are working
towards emotion perception - which is different from emotion induc-
tion (Gabrielsson and Juslin, 2003; Schindler et al., 2017).
4.1 method , materials and data preparation
Deﬁning search criteria was fundamental to determining the inclu-
sion of speciﬁc literature ﬁndings and ﬁltering them according to our
objectives. Additionally , we deﬁned how to organise and interpret
the collected materials in the dataset to draw signiﬁcant conclusions.
Hence, we detail the methodology performed to build the proposed
dataset.
1. Context deﬁnition
Firstly , we have identiﬁed the research question and related do-
mains. To this end, we have focused on a theoretical exploration
of relationships between auditory and visual characteristics me-
diated by perception and emotion.
2. Research and data collection
This phase encompassed an extensive search of studies related
to the following keywords selection: "visual music," "color-sound
associations," "sound-image associations," "audio-visual metaphors,"
"audio-visual relationships," "audio-visual perception," "music-
emotions," "image-emotions," "cross-modal associations," "per-
ceived emotions," "emotional expression," and "aesthetic emo-
tions."
1 This chapter aims to contribute to research question 1: What sort of aesthetic and
affective-perceptual correspondences may exist between image and music?
39
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

40 a dataset of audio -visual associations mediated by emotions
3. Studies selection (inclusion/exclusion criteria)
All the included studies primarily focused on Western culture.
The collected references belong to a broad historical period, as
we wanted a broad perspective. To avoid the repetition of ﬁnd-
ings, the articles containing original experiments were favoured,
and, as such, a limit on the year of publication was not imposed.
We also crossed the articles referencing other articles to elimi-
nate duplicate information during this process.
We did not include references with an unconﬁrmed hypothe-
sis in the present study , as we were looking for experimentally
conﬁrmed relationships. We also excluded references not con-
templated on the list of common emotions to music and images
(see sub-section 4.2.1.1). Moreover, references relating to whole
pieces of music/images instead of their auditory/VP were not
included.
After this selection process, we wrote down the ﬁnal associ-
ations with their bibliographic reference and double-checked
them.
4. Data organisation
As a few studies were related to the three areas, we divided the
collected studies into two separate tables: (i) emotions-music
and (ii) emotions-image. We ﬁrst ensured that music and image
vocabulary shared the same emotions to draw the dataset.
Secondly , we ensured that they had the same levels of depth
(we deﬁned three levels of information: perceptual category ,
property , and value). For example, music vocabulary can be un-
folded in this way: ( 1) Perceptual category: temporal organisa-
tion, ( 2) Property: tempo, ( 3) Values: fast, moderate, and slow.
As for image vocabulary , it can be unfolded into the following
example: ( 1) Perceptual category: low-level attributes, ( 2) Prop-
erty: size, ( 3) Values: big, medium, small.
Regarding the Table entries, they were created each time an as-
sociation was found (according to our methodology rules). For
each entry or association, we marked a cross along with the bib-
liographic reference of the corresponding research study . For
example, if three authors - Auth 1, Auth 2, Auth 3 - associated
"Anger" to "dissonant" music and two authors - Auth 1, Auth4 -
"Calm" to "consonant" music, we would place these associations
in the "music-emotions" Tableas exempliﬁed by Figure 30.
5. Findings summary
We synthesized the dataset through summary tables to help
communicate results. As it was essential to limit the scope of our
experiments to extract signiﬁcant conclusions, we focused on a
smaller dataset of visual and auditory associations regarding
four emotional states: angry , calm, happy , and sad. This smaller
set of emotions was chosen for the amount of information avail-
able and their position in the circumplex model of emotions.

6. Interpretation and analysis
We interpreted the literature review ﬁndings in agreement with
our research questions (see Chapter 1 and Section 4.3) by analysing
the perceptual relevance of the collected A V associations. We
have also identiﬁed gaps in the current state of the art and pro-
vided some recommendations for ﬁeld practitioners.
In the following sections, we present and analyse the resulting as-
sociations explored between music, image, and emotions.
4.2 the dataset
The resulting dataset comprised over 857 associations between mu-
sic characteristics and emotions and over 424 associations between
image characteristics and emotions. The Supplementary Material sec-
tion provides a complete overview of these tables’ contents.
The tables’ contents were structured based on three key vocabular-
ies: emotions, AP, and VP. Each vocabulary was reﬁned and iterated
according to our research objectives. We detail this process below.
4.2.1 Emotions Vocabulary
Emotions are a fundamental aspect of the human experience as they
are essential in navigating and making sense of the world around
us. Besides our individual experiences, cultural background, and cur-
rent mood, it is known that certain music or image expressions can
inﬂuence our perception of emotions (Juslin, 2013; Matsumoto, 1989;
Ramachandran and Hubbard, 2001).
The choice for the emotions vocabulary to be included in the dataset
relied on ﬁnding a set of emotions common to the visual and au-
ditory domains. To assess the maximum of the emotions commonly
perceived in both visual and auditory domains, we made a list of
the emotion-based studies containing them. Several authors often
presented similar terminologies during this process, so we merged
the synonymous emotion terms. We looked for a dictionary deﬁni-
tion to ensure that the words were synonyms. For example, dignity
and solemn were all in the same category (emotion nº 4 of the list
enumerated above). When the same pairs of emotion-feature associa-
tions were present in several articles by the same author, we collected
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

42 a dataset of audio -visual associations mediated by emotions
the emotion nomenclature of the most recently published reference.
The identiﬁed and assembled emotions fall into 16 distinct groups
to limit the extensive categorisation of emotions. This ended in a set
of 16 emotion labels: ( 1) anger; ( 2) boredom; ( 3) calm; ( 4) dignity; ( 5)
disgust; ( 6) energy; ( 7) excitement; ( 8) expectation; ( 9) fear; ( 10) hap-
piness; ( 11) masterful; ( 12) pleasantness; ( 13) sadness ( 14) sleepy ( 15)
surprise ( 16) tenderness (Hevner et al., 2004; Russell, 1980).
4.2.2 Music and Image Vocabularies
Both music and image vocabularies were built in two phases. First,
we collected a list of characteristics from the literature experiments
relating speciﬁc auditory/visual characteristics to emotions. In a sec-
ond instance, we completed the characteristics list by composing ele-
ments of music or an image, even if no studies had mentioned it. In
the following step, we merged this information and grouped these
characteristics into perceptual levels of information. In both cases, we
carefully selected the information that these auditory and visual el-
ements would later use to create expressive but abstract forms of
communication.
The music vocabulary was grouped into four perpetually distinct
sections: ( 1) harmony and melody , ( 2) dynamics, ( 3) texture, and ( 4)
temporal organisation. These parameters were structured based on
the perceptual organisation suggested by several authors (Bregman,
1994; Livingstone et al., 2007; Temperley, 2004).
Below, we present some music samples to exemplify how they ﬁt
into several perceptual categories. We shall note that they are not pre-
sented in the full detail found in literature as this is a mere demon-
stration example.
A. Harmony and Melody ; e.g. consonance and dissonance:
https://freesound.org/people/Lemoncreme/sounds/186942
https://freesound.org/people/ZeritFreeman/sounds/472315
B. Dynamics; e.g. loudness:
https://freesound.org/people/NoiseCollector/sounds/15705
C. T exture; e.g. instrument’s timbres
https://freesound.org/people/Angel_Perez_Grandi/sounds/47382
D. T emporal Organisation; e.g. rhythm:
https://freesound.org/people/iut_Paris8/sounds/466770
As for the image vocabulary , we grouped visual attributes into four
distinct sections: ( 1) high-level attributes, ( 2) low-level attributes, ( 3)
object manipulations and (4) relationships among elements. Our choice
for organising visual attributes is justiﬁed by Christian Leborg’s pro-
posed visual grammar (Leborg, 2006). Additionally , we completed
the list of attributes with references from literature studies featuring
abstract visual alphabets (Arnheim, 1965; Gerstner and Stephenson,
1964; Markovi´ c and Radonji´ c, 2008).

exemplify a representation of the following visual properties: (a,

(d1,d 2,d 3) shape contours, closure, and type; (e, f) elements over-

of elements; (i) repetition of elements; (j) texture; (k, l, m) visual

As exempliﬁed in Figure 31 through a Kandinsky painting, a set
of visual characteristics can be used to produce an abstract image

lines to emphasise the use Low-level or isolated properties, and the
green dashed lines to note the expression of High-level or composite
properties as well as Object Manipulations and Relationships.
It is important to note that reaching a list of emotions capable of
being recognised in both auditory and visual expressions was essen-
tial. Part of this process was also to ensure that auditory and visual
vocabularies would depart from the same principles of organization
(perceptual category levels and properties) to allow later the devel-
opment of a modular an adaptive A V language (see Part iii for more
details).
4.2.3 An approach to connect Music and Image
Emotions, music, and images can be understood and studied from
many perspectives, and different approaches may emphasise differ-
ent dimensions or aspects. Even though auditory and visual domains
have not been directly connected so far, as they share the same PE
and vocabulary organization, we suggest connecting them from a
cognitive perspective (see Chapter 3 and Part iii). As such, our ﬁrst
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

44 a dataset of audio -visual associations mediated by emotions
approach to studying these domains focused on developing two com-
prehensive tables: one connecting emotions and AP and the other con-
necting emotions and VP. Due to the length of the resulting "music-
emotions" and "image-emotions" tables, we have placed the complete
tables in supplementary materials. Therefore, we next outline an anal-
ysis of these datasets.
4.3 analysis and discussion
We analyse this literature review from three perspectives: (i) frequency
of auditory/VP associations to emotions, (ii) perceptual relevance of
properties associations to emotions, and (iii) selection of A V associa-
tions mediated by emotions. The following analysis will allow us to
explore which emotions are most commonly associated with auditory
or VP and which are more likely to be Perceptually Relevant (PR) in
the representation of emotions. Moreover, we can identify limitations
in the current state of the art as well as provide some suggestions for
ﬁeld practitioners.
4.3.1 Music and image properties across emotions
It has been argued that when listening to music or observing an im-
age, some emotions are more prone to occur than others (Brattico
and Pearce, 2013; Juslin, 2013; Plutchik, 2001; Vink, 2001). Analysing
the frequency of agreement among authors on the established associ-
ations, we intended to extract a set of preferences.
To accomplish this analysis we counted how many times each pa-
rameter was associated with a speciﬁc emotion. Then, we added the
total values of all the parameters in the dataset for that emotion (see
Table 1 for an example of this process).

In sum, these reports suggest that similar emotions can be expressed
through a shared set of auditory and visual qualities. For example,
happiness and pleasantness share the auditory property values of
consonant harmonies, large pitch variation, regular rhythm, and tonal
melodies, and the visual property values of round edges, bright colours,
large element sizes, and simple and clear compositions.
Observing these data sets in Figures 32 and 33, we can also con-
clude that the associations between auditory elements and emotions
have been studied in more depth than associations between visual
elements and emotions 5.
The next step of this process consisted of performing a property
sum process for each emotion. This sum resulted in Table 2. Analysing
this table, we can conclude that “happiness”, “sadness”, “anger”, and
“fear” are the emotions most commonly associated with visual and
AP (followed by “tenderness", "pleasantness", "energy", and “calm").
In contrast, the least studied emotions were "disgust", "boredom",
"sleepy", and "expectation".

number of times that each property was associated with an emotion.

4.3 analysis and discussion 51
Generally speaking, results were similar when comparing the most
relevant properties associated with the entire set of emotions to those
associated only with the four selected emotions (see Table 2). Nev-
ertheless, it is essential to highlight some differences: for example,
regarding VP, “thickness” and “regularity” have higher relevance in
the limited group of emotions; regarding musical properties, the dif-
ferences are more expressive, with properties such as “tone attack”
and “articulation variation” gaining more relevance, and “pitch level”
losing relevance.
4.3.3 Audio-visual associations mediated by emotions
Working with a smaller set of information was essential to limit the
scope of our experiments and extract signiﬁcant conclusions. To this
end, we have created a smaller dataset and focused on the relation-
ships regarding four emotional states: angry , calm, happy , and sad.
As previously explained, this set of emotions was chosen for two rea-
sons: the amount of information available and their position in the
circumplex model of emotions (Russell, 1980). Additionally , we have
ﬁltered these contents into a set of relationships where three or more
emotions are associated with each property (The remaining dataset
can be consulted in the supplementary ﬁles section). Following this
method, the following properties were excluded from Table 5:
• Music: harmonic intervals, melodic motion, vibrato, pause, tempo
variation, note density , rhythmic pattern.
• Image: regularity , closure, opacity , balance, density , visual dis-
tribution, augment/diminish, movement, symmetry , repetition,
rotation, cluster, depth, distance, variation, volume, overlap.
Table 5 presents the resulting dataset. Every relationship mentioned
in the literature is marked with a dot " •". Whenever there are concur-
rent ﬁndings, two or more property values assigned to the same emo-
tion, we mark with a star " ?" the relationship found to have stronger
evidence support. In the remaining concurrent cases, the ones with
equal weight, we limited to the bullet. 7
Provided this analysis, it is also worth mentioning some other as-
pects that should be further studied. For example, it is common to
ﬁnd in literature theories about the importance of tension-relaxation
sensation in music. Because this seems such a prominent aspect, re-
searchers have yet to focus on experimenting with this sensation.
Nonetheless, it could be essential to understand better these attributes
and their impact on our emotional perception of a particular piece of
music.

4.4 the dataset as a tool 53
4.3.4 Foundations of an Audio-Visual Language
In the context of computational exploration of creative domains, the
A V language refers to a system of communication comprising a vo-
cabulary and grammar. This will enable the representation and gener-
ation of associations between multiple sets of visual elements, music
elements, and basic general emotions.
The vocabulary encompasses a perceptual organisation of visual
and AP and a set of emotions common to both domains. For instance,
vocabulary elements can include visual attributes such as shape, colour,
and size, as well as musical attributes such as rhythm, melody , and
harmony . On another note, emotions serve as a bridge between the
auditory and visual domains, enabling the association of speciﬁc emo-
tions with particular sound and visual elements. The grammar of the
A V language establishes rules and patterns for combining elements
from the vocabulary . It is later used to generate and express music
or image. This is, the grammar provides a structure for generating
and expressing music or images based on the selected elements and
guides the arrangement of a given composition. By better understand-
ing a domain’s emotional contents and perceptual characteristics, we
aim to draw relevant associations in the computational exploration of
A V languages. In this sense, the A V language proposed serves as a
transpositional tool, allowing to convey relationships between differ-
ent domains’ elements and facilitate the exploration of PE.
4.4 the dataset as a tool
Creative Emotions (https://creativeemotions.dei.uc.pt/) is a digi-
tal tool to aid in the exploration of relationships between Emotions,
Music, and Image. This tool provides a quick way of revealing the
associations between these domains and enhances the possibility of
interrelating distinct creative domains.
Through the development of a website and an API, we aimed that
researchers, designers, and curious minds could easily access, inter-
act, and use our research results (see Figure 34) to create emotion-
sensitive design or music applications.
4.4.1 The Web page
The developed web page contains all the associations presented in the
cross-modal dataset of Chapter 3. These associations can be navigated
from a “top down” or “bottom up” approaches. The ﬁrst approach
is more focused on a exploration of associations through auditory
or VP. As a result, the “Top-down” search begins with the proper-
ties that compose a piece of music or image, followed by the values
of each property and its associated emotion. The second approach,
“Bottom-up”, is focused in providing an overview of all the proper-
ties and values associated to a speciﬁc emotion. As such, this search
begins with a list of emotions common to music and image domains,

followed by the values associated to each emotion present in the list.

4.4.2 Information as an API
In addition to the web page, we provided a JSON ﬁle ready to be used
in upcoming projects. Similarly to the web page, it can be navigated
in tree up (bottom-up) or down (top-down) approaches. The structure
inside the JSON ﬁle can be accessed by typing the “ed” variable in
the browser console. The information is organised as follows: ed >
domain > levels > properties > emotions. Generally speaking, the
JSON ﬁle allows us to access the desired level of information directly .
For instance, we can access the property and see all associated emo-
tions or access emotion and see all associated properties. For example,
in the ﬁle, within the Music domain, there is a perceptual level called
Harmony Melody . This level has a property named Harmony Sonance,
which contains a value called Consonant. This value is associated with
several Emotions, one of which is Happiness (see Figure 36).
In other words, performing this search path "Music > Harmony
Sonance > Consonant" would result in a list of emotions - "Calm, Dig-
nity , Happiness, Masterful, Pleasantness, Sleepy , Tenderness" - which
in turn are associated with the musical value of "consonance". If,
for example, we wanted to see all the properties and values associ-
ated with the emotion of "Happiness" we could perform a bottom-up
search (see Figure 37).

4.5 summary
In this chapter, we investigated a dataset of A V associations, i.e., map-
pings of AP like tempo and harmony and VP like colour and shape
mediated by emotions, providing a foundation for co-creative appli-
cations. The methodology involved reviewing and selecting works
from multiple authors to ensure we captured a comprehensive and
focused on PR associations.
Through this dataset, we concluded that certain emotions, such as
happiness, sadness, anger, and calm, had stronger associations with
speciﬁc audio and visual properties. For example, happiness was of-
ten linked with bright colours and major musical modes, while sad-
ness correlated to darker colours and minor modes. In addition, the
dataset highlighted the perceptual relevance of various properties in
conveying emotions — for instance, tempo and loudness regarding
music and colour, hue and shape regarding image.
To validate the dataset’s potential application in creative tools, we
developed an artefact, "Face Emotions," in which facial expressions
were classiﬁed into an emotion category through computer vision
and then translated into a corresponding visual expression. The next
chapters further describe techniques for the computational genera-
tion of A V relationships.

5CONTEXT AND RELATED WORK
5.1 computer music and computer art
Since the advent of computer science, art has taken a signiﬁcant in-
terest in using computers for the automated generation of images
and music. However, the generation of artworks has not always in-
volved using computers. Pre-computer era examples of generative
art include the works of Kenneth Martin, whose abstract paintings
relied on basic geometrical ﬁgures (squares, circles, diagrams) and
rules of proportion (see Figure 39). Other examples include the dice
music written by Haydn and Mozart, where the exact order of the
pre-composed music phrases would be decided by throwing a dice
(see Figure 40) (Boden and Edmonds, 2009) 1.
Figure 39: Samples of Kenneth Martin Work. (a) "Abstract" 1952, (b) "Chance
and Order" 1971, (c) "Rotation Frankfurt I" 1977.
Figure 40: Mozart’s
Rolling Dice Game
(1787).
With the aid of computational tools for creating works in audio
and visual domains, new possibilities and forms of creative processes
have emerged. By 1843, Ada Lovelace wrote the ﬁrst algorithm in his-
tory . In fact, she showed the underlying potential of computers by
using a musical metaphor: "Supposing, for instance, that the funda-
mental relations of pitched sounds in the science of harmony and
musical composition are susceptible of such expression and adapta-
tions, the Engine might compose elaborate and scientiﬁc pieces of
music of any degree of complexity or extent" (Hutzler, Gortais, and
Drogoul, 2000). The speed and modularity of technology would then
allow new forms of creation and an ability to interpret and evolve
abstract concepts with higher degrees of autonomy and complexity
(Boden, 2009; Boden and Edmonds, 2009; Noll, 1966; Sims, 1992).
1 This chapter aims to contribute to the research question 2: How can we map image
and music computationally?
59
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

60 context and related work
5.1.1 Computer Music
Computer music has historically fallen into two categories. On the
one side, computer-aided composition provides algorithmic tools for com-
posers to interactively build new works based on personal, musical
or intuitive factors (Gerhard and Hepting, 2004). Conversely , in algo-
rithmic composition, the algorithms are set free to compose new works
independent of a human, and the ﬁnal work is then judged for aes-
thetic appeal. Overall, this ﬁeld relies on the use of a "formal process
to make music with minimal human intervention" (Edwards, 2011).
Inﬂuential computer music artists include names such as Iannis
Xenakis and Brian Eno. Iannis Xenakis soon realised the great po-
tential of using computers to make music by using mathematical
and scientiﬁc concepts to create complex and abstract musical struc-
tures. He developed several techniques and notation systems, includ-
ing stochastic music, in which chance operations determined the ele-
ments of the composition (Giannakis and Smith, 2000).
Also, within generative music methods, Brian Eno (Boden and Ed-
monds, 2009) conducted several composition experiments where a
set of rules deﬁned music. Discreet Music LP ( 1975) is an example of
this process: a tape loop feedback system created a musical piece. A
synthesised melody was recorded onto a tape machine, the output of
which was fed into a second tape machine. The output of the second
machine was then fed back into the ﬁrst machine, and the overlap-
ping signals were recorded (see Figure 41) (Pearson, 2011).
Figure 41: Brian Eno’s operational diagram for "Discreet Music" LP — the
black line indicates the signal path, 1975.
Most of the methodologies in the literature for the automated gen-
eration of music, range from grammar-based systems, knowledge-
based systems, and logical conditions to Genetic Algorithm (GA) (Bernardes
et al., 2015). David Cope described key models (Cope, 2005) for the
generation of computer music:
• Rule-Based Programming: Typically consists of a series of if-
then-else clauses, in which a condition is posed, a consequent
action is given for when that condition proves true, and an al-
ternative is indicated for when that condition proves untrue.
Markov Chains can be included in this kind of conditional be-
haviour (Cope, 2005). This results in declarative and logical pro-
grams that describe what is required in terms of rules, and it
is left to the computer system to work out the order in which
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

5.1 computer music and computer art 61
things should be done. The sequence can potentially go on for-
ever without loops, depending on the rules (Edmonds, 2003).
Other techniques that departed from a grammar-based system
are works performed with L-Systems. In one of the ﬁrst works
on the ﬁeld of music generation and L-systems, Prusinkiewicz
(Lourenço, Ralha, and Brandao, 2009) described a technique to
extract music from a graphical interpretation of an L-system
string. The length of each note was interpreted as the length of
the branch, and the note pitch was interpreted as the vertical co-
ordinate of the note. Graphical and musical interpretation were
synchronized (Prusinkiewicz, 1986).
• Data-Driven Programming: Uses analysis of data rather than
programmed rules to create new instances of music.
• Evolutionary Algorithms: This process relies on natural selec-
tion (survival of the best ﬁt). Typically , a series of constraints
tells whether individuals have a better chance of surviving, re-
producing or being selected (Bentley and Corne, 2002). For ex-
ample, GA and cellular automata are included in this topic.
This technique has been effectively used for music composition
(Cope, 2015).
• Neural Networks: A received input triggers neuron substitutes,
which turn to trigger an initially random output. They then cy-
cle through a series of forward or back propagations that com-
pare output with input and alter hidden unit values to match or
approximate the training values set (V ogels and Abbott, 2005).
• Fuzzy Logic: Ranks output possibilities according to the solu-
tion’s potential of being true or false. This has proven particu-
larly valuable for analysing musical data with more than one
potential interpretation (Cope, 2005).
• Mathematical Modeling: Mathematical series can be used in
alternative formalisms for quasi-randomness. Chaotic genera-
tors and resultant attractors generate aimless-sounding music
or drivel at worst. Examples of these are fractals, Brownian mo-
tion, and random walks, among others (Cope, 2005).
• Soniﬁcation: Music or sound is produced by translating tradi-
tionally non-audible data into sound. It is commonly used to
search for patterns that otherwise may go unnoticed by the hu-
man eye or computational analysis. It allows the user to obtain a
deeper understanding of the data or processes under investiga-
tion by listening. A central soniﬁcation technique is parameter
mapping, where data (or data-driven) features are mapped to at-
tributes such as pitch, timbre, etc. The high number of acoustic
attributes makes soniﬁcation a high-dimensional data display
(Hermann and Hunt, 2005; Nees and Walker, 2009)
Overall, music has a substantial structural diversity and complexity .
In the scope of this thesis, we consider rule-based programming, GA,
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

62 context and related work
and mathematical modelling to be the most inspiring approaches for
music generation.
5.1.2 Computer Art
The terms computer art and generative art have been used to describe
art-making processes by computers with and without the artist’s di-
rect control. A set of rules or instructions are typically input into
a computer program. The computer program then uses these rules
to generate an image or animation. With these computational tech-
niques, we can produce varied artworks with unpredictable results
without wasting too much time experimenting consciously (Boden,
2009; Noll, 1966).
Early computer art can be traced back to the work of Ben F. La-
posky , V era Molnar, Michael Noll, Frieder Nake, Manfred Mohr, and
Harold Cohen, among others. Ben Laposky , for example, used a cath-
ode ray oscilloscope with sine wave generators and other electrical
circuits to create abstract art. The resulting images were called Os-
cillons, and they displayed various aesthetic combinations of basic
electronic waveforms, playing a sort of VM. V era Molnar, another pi-
oneer computer artist, created a series of geometric shapes arranged
in a grid pattern inspired by the abstract art of Piet Mondrian. These
works were generated using a set of rules deﬁned by the artists (Com-
part, n.d. Dietrich, 1985; Noll, 1966).
In Graphic Design, Karl Gerstner proposed a series of grammar to
build languages of typography in the early days of the computer era
(Gerstner and Stephenson, 1964). By the 90s, Harold Cohen’s project
AARON was an early functioning example of a harmonic relation
between man and machine. With this, he wanted to automate the
drawing process by teaching the computer his style of drawing (Diet-
rich, 1985). Free from physics, mechanics, and optics limitations, dig-
ital computer graphics promised an unprecedented palette of colour,
light, form, and animation (Snibbe and Levin, 2000).
In general, computational techniques to generate visual art (exam-
ple in Figure 42) commonly include:
• Rule-Based and Grammar-Based Systems: They involve deﬁn-
ing a set of rules or constraints that govern the generation of
an art piece. These rules can be as simple or complex as de-
sired and create various outputs. It can include anything from
generating abstract shapes (Brown, 1992; Levin and Brain, 2021;
Pocock-Williams, 1992) to creating complex landscapes and struc-
tures like shape grammars (Stiny and Gips, 1971) and l-systems
(Prusinkiewicz, 1986).
An example of the application of generative art methodologies
are shape grammars. This ﬁeld emerged as an algorithmic pro-
cess of “machine-manipulable form of stylistic description” (Stiny
and Gips, 1971) allowing the exploration of essential visual ele-
ments’ interrelations according to speciﬁc rules. They iterate the
growth of visual elements into a uniﬁed structure of forms. This
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

5.1 computer music and computer art 63
Figure 42: Study of different generative design techniques (e.g. combining
shape grammars and cellular automata). Figure and study from
Gu, Singh, and Merrick ( 2010).
has been known as one of the most efﬁcient tools to simulate
abstract art structures. For example, Knight presents a series of
relationships between forms or lines and rules that deﬁne rela-
tionships between colours based on Vantongerloo and Glarner
paintings (Brown, 1992; Knight, 1989).
• Neural Networks: These techniques have become increasingly
popular with the emergence of more powerful computing re-
sources to generate computer art (Li et al., 2020). They include
techniques such as Neural Style Transfer, GANs, and Recurrent
Neural Networks (RNNs). The ﬁrst uses convolutional neural
networks to apply the style of one image (typically an artwork)
or music to the content of another (Briot, Hadjeres, and Pachet,
2020). The second consists of two neural networks where the
generator and a discriminator compete against each other. The
discriminator evaluates the generator outputs against a training
data set, learning to distinguish between generated and base-
line images. Over time, the generator improves its ability to cre-
ate images that fool the discriminator (Goodfellow et al., 2014).
In the third approach, RNNs learn patterns from sequences of
frames or strokes and generate new sequences that mimic the
learned style or continue a given visual narrative (Roberts et
al., 2019). Examples of recent works in this ﬁeld are Dall-E and
Midjourney models (Borji, 2022).
• Nature-Inspired Computation: These processes have allowed
artists to create works that learn from their environment and
produce unique outputs. In particular, the ﬁeld of evolutionary
art (Bentley and Corne, 2002) has been generating images and
music through evolutionary processes (Machado, Romero, and
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

64 context and related work
Manaris, 2008; Moroni et al., 2006; Weiss-Cohen, Cherchiglia,
and Costa, 2017). In this process, a set of rules or parameters is
given to the algorithm, generating different versions of the art
piece based on these parameters. The best versions are then se-
lected and used as input for the next generation, and the process
repeats until the desired result is achieved (Sims, 1992). Latham
and Todd ( 1992) was one of the pioneers in using EA to gen-
erate art. He combined organic visual concepts with computer
graphics, using evolutionary principles to create complex forms
that evolved over time based on certain aesthetic ﬁtness criteria.
Other computer art generation techniques worth mentioning
include Artiﬁcial Life Systems, which simulate the behaviours
and characteristics of living organisms to create dynamic and
adaptive artworks. By mimicking biological processes such as
growth, reproduction, and evolution, artists can create art/de-
sign that not only evolves visually over time but also responds
to environmental stimuli, originating artworks that can change
and develop in unexpected ways, reﬂecting the unpredictable
nature of life itself. Such systems often explore the concept of
emergence (Reynolds, 1999), where simple rules at a local level
lead to complex behaviours at a global level, like the example
of swarm art (Blackwell, Granulator, Raidl, et al., 2004).
Two examples of systems built to explore music through nature-
inspired computing were developed by Blackwell or Beyls (Beyls,
Bernardes, and Caetano, 2015; Blackwell, Granulator, Raidl, et
al., 2004). Blackwell et al. explored the mapping of textile im-
ages into sound by simulating swarms ﬂying over images and
producing sound through granular synthesis. In another ap-
proach, Beyls et al. created an artefact that explored nature-
inspired computing to visually organise sounds according to
their perceptual qualities.
• Sound and Music Visualisation: Information Visualisation tech-
niques have the ability to map auditory and visual domains,
transforming data (sounds) into compelling visual expressions
(Lima, Santos, and Meiguins, 2022). They usually employ algo-
rithms to interpret sound data and identify auditory patterns
(e.g. waveforms, spectrograms, real-time frequency analysis), con-
verting them into visual elements such as colours, shapes, and
movements (Ferguson, Moere, and Cabrera, 2005; Giannakis and
Smith, 2000). For instance, sound visualisation is commonly
used in music videos and live performances to create engaging
A V experiences or to compose music in real-time.
For this thesis’s objectives, we outline rule-based programming,
grammar-based systems, and nature-inspired computation as the most
suitable approaches for image generation. We have described possi-
ble approaches and techniques for computationally generating music
and images. However, how may these approaches inﬂuence the per-

ception of these forms of expression? Or, in other words, can compu-
tational systems produce music and images based on emotions?
5.1.3 Computer Generation of Music and Image based on Emotions
Only a few computational explorations linking music and images to
emotions were found, as this is a relatively complex interaction. How-
ever, some inspiring explorations performed with generative art and
evolutionary techniques are worth mentioning. Although some of the
following works we describe aim to induce emotions, we note that
our goal focuses on the representation of emotion perception.
In the ﬁeld of computational music and emotions Livingstone et al.
(2007) developed a system that employed a set of rules for score struc-
ture and performative expression based on the arousal-valence model.
By varying musical parameters like the tempo, mode, loudness, artic-
ulation, pitch and others to produce and induce speciﬁc emotional
effects in the listener. On another approach, Scirea et al. (Scirea et al.,
2017) explored an evolutionary music system where they aimed to
create music that could express different moods in a dynamic envi-
ronment. The system generated compositions with a chord sequence,
a melody , and an accompaniment (Scirea et al., 2017), dealing with a
set of musical properties such as harmony , melody , pitch, scale, inten-
sity , timbre, and rhythm.
In the realm of generative art, computational expressionism emerged
as a way to create artworks capable of conveying solid emotional
content. Artists working in this domain used algorithms to produce
pieces that were not necessarily representational but could convey
emotional content. For example, vibrant colours, bold lines, and dy-
namic shapes are known to convey joy and excitement. In contrast,
grayscale images with soft edges and a blurry effect can evoke a sense
of sadness and melancholy , or even distorted shapes and dark colours
can create a feeling of fear and unease.
Artists like Rafael Lozano-Hemmer and Golan Levin have created
installations that engage with the audience’s emotions (Lozano-Hemmer,
2008; Snibbe and Levin, 2000). Lozano-Hemmer’s "Pulse Room" in-
vited visitors to put their ﬁngers on a sensor that detected their pulse,
which was then displayed as a blinking light in a room ﬁlled with
hundreds of other lights. This work has been described as both calm-
ing and unsettling.
In another instance, Golan Levin created a series of pieces known
for their emotional impact. For example, his work "Dialtones: A Telesym-
phony" was a performance that involved many people dialling into a
telephone system and creating music by pressing the buttons on their
phones. The piece is described as "a kind of symphony of phone lines"
and has been described as both joyful and melancholic.
More recently , Reﬁk Anadol’s artworks (Anadol, 2020) used Artiﬁ-
cial Intelligence (AI) to create data paintings representing meditative,
negative, and positive states of the mind. These paintings demon-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

66 context and related work
strate how recent computational techniques can be employed to ex-
plore the representation of emotions in visual artworks.
In the area of sound visualization, Grill and Flexer (Grill and Flexer,
2012) proposed a strategy that used metaphoric sensory properties to
build meaningful associations between auditory and visual dimen-
sions. They linked visual features such as shape, stroke, grid posi-
tion, and colour to different perceptual categories of textural sounds
(smooth/coarse, tonal/noisy , ordered/chaotic, high/low, and homo-
geneous/heterogeneous), allowing interaction with the visualization
of an auditory dataset through visual representations.
To summarise, by using generative and EA and other computa-
tional tools to create dynamic, interactive works of art, artists can
explore the relationship between technology and human emotions.
5.2 computational creativity
Computational creativity (CC) has witnessed the development of vari-
ous systems and techniques to simulate human creativity processes.
From rule-based systems to interactive algorithms, a wide range of
computational approaches has been adopted to achieve expressive
computational representations in creative domains like music and im-
age. This research ﬁeld has its roots in the early days of AI research,
when researchers sought to develop computer programs capable of
generating novel ideas. This led to the development of systems that
could generate music, art, and even new scientiﬁc theories.
Margaret Boden, an early Figure in the ﬁeld, has contributed to un-
derstanding creativity as she argued that creativity could be under-
stood as a cognitive process and could be modelled computationally .
She has proposed three main types of CC: combinatorial, exploratory ,
and transformational, offering distinct approaches to generating cre-
ative outputs (Boden, 2009; Boden and Edmonds, 2009).
Combinatorial creativity combines existing ideas or elements uniquely
based on predeﬁned rules or patterns. It is based on the idea that
creativity arises from the rearrangement and combination of exist-
ing components rather than the generation of entirely new elements.
Exploratory creativity focuses on exploring and searching for new
ideas, concepts, or possibilities by exploring different problem spaces
through randomisation and variation. Lastly , transformational cre-
ativity transforms existing elements using analogy , abstraction, or
adaptation to create novel outputs. While each type has its emphasis,
they can also intersect and complement one another in the creative
process, providing a rich landscape for CC.
Although most CC systems have not focused on aesthetic rules or
perception principles to guide the generation of images or music, re-
cently , there has been a shift towards incorporating perceptual quali-
ties into computational tools that support these processes as they have
the potential to enhance the aesthetic appeal and creativity of the gen-
erated outputs. For example, this can include considering visual and
auditory perception principles, such as colour harmony , visual bal-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

5.2 computational creativity 67
ance, texture, spatial arrangement, musical structure, and rhythm. In
turn, these perceptual qualities will help to create visual or auditory
outcomes that resonate with human observers.
Other issues discussed in the CC ﬁeld regard evaluating CC sys-
tems and balancing rigid algorithmic rules and creative freedom. Some-
times, these processes involve the combination of "familiar ideas in
unfamiliar ways" (Moroni et al., 2006), and, in other cases, the explo-
ration and transformation of conceptual spaces (Boden, 2009). Either
way , the development of such systems is usually intended to achieve a
certain degree of unpredictability to have more exciting or surprising
outputs (McCormack, 2001).
The computational techniques adopted in CC systems have been
diverse. Some of the forms they took were rule-based systems (e.g.,
Aaron from Harold Cohen as illustrated in Figure ??) (Cohen, 1988)
while others relied on interactive evolutionary systems as presented
by Karl Sims (Sims, 1992). Using such computational techniques to ex-
plore CMA (relationships between several domains) has been a grow-
ing topic - which can be partly justiﬁed by the computer’s capacity to
interpret and evolve abstract concepts with a certain degree of auton-
omy and abstraction (Boden and Edmonds, 2009; Moroni et al., 2014;
Noll, 1966; Sims, 1992). For example, in "ArtV ox", Moroni et al. ( 2006)

proposed an interactive evolutionary environment to evolve images
inspired by Kandinsky’s artworks. This resulted in the evolution of a
visual composition built upon a series of geometric primitives, guid-
ing sound production in another evolutionary environment. At the
same time, this system aimed to contribute to exploring automatic
processes of human intelligence and creativity .

Evaluating CC systems requires careful consideration of various
factors, including the system’s intentions, the creativity criteria used,
and human evaluators’ expectations and interpretations. Developing
robust evaluation methodologies is crucial for advancing the ﬁeld and
ensuring the effectiveness of CC systems (Kantosalo, Linkola, and
Männistö, 2022; Maher et al., 2018).
5.3 nature -inspired computation
It is a truism to say that we live in an increasingly complex world.
This is not because the natural world (physical, biological) has changed
but because our comprehension of that world is deeper. Complex and
self-organized systems have a great appeal for A V exploration since
they can continuously change, adapt and evolve. Over the years, com-
puter artefacts promoting emergent systems behaviours have been
explored (Barszczewski et al., 2013; Uozumi et al., 2013).
5.3.1 Artiﬁcial Life Environments
The overlap between biology and art has been used by several artists
to manipulate the processes of life — such as survival and reproduc-
tion, among others — as well as to achieve a series of organic visual
expressions with a certain degree of autonomy and unpredictability
(Dorin, 2004).
Additionally , these algorithmic interpretations can generate the com-
plexity usually present in nature and thus mediate a human-machine
symbiosis. Popular computational expressions of these behaviours
range from simulations of “movements of a bird ﬂocking (see Fig-
ure 44), school of ﬁsh, pattern formation in bacteria, slime moulds or
virus’s infection" (Reynolds, 1999).
Figure 44: Craig Reynolds boids’ rules: (a) separation, (b) alignment and (c)
cohesion.
As a matter of fact, outputs from these nature-inspired systems will
not be precisely reproducible, creating a value through its partially
non-deterministic outcomes (Eck and Lamers, 2013; Tero et al., 2010).

game of life.

(Whalley ,2009). We highlight “earGRAM”, developed by Beyls et al.
(Beyls, Bernardes, and Caetano, 2015). This was an interactive audio-
visual installation based on a society of autonomous artiﬁcial agents
sensitive to each other and to the physical human inﬂuence. They
intended to bridge an artiﬁcial life system and physical world be-
haviours in a way that had a mutual inﬂuence. The connection to the
physical world was achieved through computer vision, which, in turn,

Other authors have explored how simple rules and algorithms could
create complex and emergent behaviours, where we highlight Con-

45), "Eden" by McCormack ( 2001) - an interactive artiﬁcial life system,
where virtual creatures evolve in a simulated environment - and "You
Pretty Little Flocker" by eldridge:2009.
On a bio-art approach, “Physarum Polycephalum”, a unicellular
slime mould, inspired an experiment where Toshiyuki Nakagaki used
this organism to mimic the pattern of the way cities are scattered
around Tokyo (see Figure 46). To reinforce the path, nutrients were
supplied at these key points, leading to the emergence of a line that
connects locations (Eck and Lamers, 2013; Tero et al., 2010).
Overall, Artiﬁcial Life Environments (ALE) provide a platform to
study and dynamically understand a response to changing environ-
ments or stimuli. We next describe evolutionary algorithmic approaches,
which can adapt and scale problems of varying complexities and di-
mensions.
5.3.2 Evolutionary Algorithms
Evolutionary algorithms are stochastic search procedures inspired by
natural selection and genetics principles. They are widely used for
optimisation, design, and learning problems due to their ﬂexibility
and ability to handle complex problems with large solution spaces.
The main idea behind EAs is to iteratively improve a population of
candidate solutions by applying genetic operators such as selection,
reproduction, and variation.
EA processes (Figure 47) typically start with initialising a popula-
tion of individuals representing candidate solutions. These individu-
als are generated randomly or based on domain-speciﬁc knowledge.
In each generation, a subset of individuals, known as parents, is se-
lected based on their ﬁtness values, which evaluate their performance
in solving the problem. Next, the selected parents undergo genetic
operators, such as mutation and crossover, to produce offspring. The
offspring, together with the parents, form the next generation. A new
population is then created by selecting a subset of survivors based
on their ﬁtness values. Finally , the survivors replace the individuals
in the current population, and the process continues for a ﬁxed num-
ber of generations or until a termination criterion is met (Bentley and
Corne, 2002; Fogel, 1999).
In the context of CC, Interactive Evolutionary Algorithm (IEA)s
have been used to incorporate human judgment and preferences into
the creative process. IEAs allow users to actively participate and con-
tribute their subjective aesthetic or functional criteria, bridging the
gap between computational systems and human creativity (Sims, 1992;

Yannakakis, Liapis, and Alexopoulos, 2014). By incorporating human
judgment and preferences, IEA enable users to actively participate
in the creative process, contributing to their subjective aesthetic or
functional criteria. In a way , they aim to bridge the gap between com-
putational systems and human creativity , allowing for the generation
of outputs aligned with human sensibilities. In this context, IEAs can
provide a valuable framework for CC by integrating computational
techniques with human input, fostering collaboration between hu-
mans and machines.
In the domain of music composition, there have been some efforts
to create evolutionary systems for the automatic generation of music.
A strong example is the EA proposed by Biles ( 1994). In this work,
he used a GA to produce jazz solos over a given chord progression
(Biles, 1994; Guéret, Monmarché, and Slimane, 2004; Moroni et al.,
2000; Todd and Werner, 1999; Wiggins et al., 1998).
Some of these methods have also been used to aid in evolutionary
image generation tools. NEvAr (Neuro-Evolutionary Art), a system
proposed by Machado et al. (Machado and Cardoso, 2000) combined
neuro-evolutionary algorithms with user guidance to generate artistic
images. In NEvAr, the user takes an active role in the evolution of the
artwork by providing feedback and guiding the evolutionary algo-
rithm towards desired aesthetics. Hence, the user’s preferences and
artistic input served as the guiding force for the system to explore its
solution space.
5.3.2.1 Genetic programming
GP, a sub-ﬁeld of EA, tries to solve the computational challenge of
creating an automatic system whose input is a high-level statement of
a problem’s requirements and whose output is a satisfactory solution
to the given problem (Bentley and Corne, 2002).
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

5.4 preliminary work 71
Although there are some challenges in handling GP algorithms,
such as premature convergence, scalability , and diversity maintenance,
they have proven to be very efﬁcient in discovering complex relation-
ships and patterns that may not be apparent in the original feature
space. Following this logic, GP seemed to be a good ﬁt to help explore
our Multi-dimensional (MD) problem, as it could attend to the design
concern of being able to navigate the dialogue in two directions (from
M!I or I !M).
Several implementations of genetic programmes can be found. How-
ever, there is one particular that captures our attention - Tiny GP .
TinyGP is a simple GP system for symbolic regression proposed by
Poli et al. (Poli, Langdon, and McPhee, 2008). This is, Given a range
of input values x, the algorithm’s objective is to ﬁnd a function f so
that f(x) equals a deﬁned target value y . TinyGP ﬁnds the function
f through evolving equations represented as trees composed of ter-
minals and primitives. This representation is known as a program.
The terminal set comprises a range of ﬂoating point variables, and
the function set comprises addition, multiplication, protected divi-
sion and subtraction (Poli, Langdon, and McPhee, 2008).
5.4 preliminary work
In the context of audiovisual explorations, nature-inspired computa-
tion techniques and sound visualisation offer the possibility of ex-
ploring complex associations between auditory and visual elements
while responding to user input preferences. To assess the potential of
state-of-the-art approaches, we conducted preliminary experiments.
These prototypes resulted in interactive sound visualisation and
digital art installations created within real-world contexts. This led to
a better understanding of the practical implications and potential of
computational approaches for exploring the A V interplay steered by
human perception. We next summarise these experiments. 2
nº 1. an interactive tool to assist music creation Expanding
on Bernardes, Davies, and Guedes ( 2017) previous work, we ex-
plored a MD tool to support mashup creation through an in-
teractive sound visualisation, guiding users intuitively in the
music mixing process (Maçãs et al., 2019).
Mashup creation is a music composition practice strongly linked
to various Electronic Dance Music (EDM) sub-genres and en-
tails the recombination of existing (pre-recorded) musical audio
as a means for creative endeavour.
In particular, we studied the use of a force-directed graph to
enhance the clarity and ﬂuidity of a visualisation (see tool in-
terface in Figure 48). We expressed the hierarchical harmonic
compatibility among tracks through linked nodes on a canvas
and a set of visual variables to characterise rhythmic, spectral

and timbral track attributes. Additionally , our interface allowed

5.4.1 Audio-visual Mappings
The development of the visualisation model was guided by prin-
ciples ensuring efﬁciency and interactivity . For instance, each
track’s visual representation is anchored in perception theories.
Additionally , to enable effective visualisation and facilitate user
navigation, we incorporate various techniques for representing
musical concepts. For example, users can modify the organisa-
tion of the graph, adjust connection thresholds, and highlight
speciﬁc track characteristics through an interactive panel.
The musical attributes of onset density , spectral region, and tim-
bre were mapped to visual variables:
• Spectral region and onset density are divided into three
levels, each represented by a speciﬁc shape. High-frequency
regions are represented by sharp contours (triangles), low
frequencies by rounded contours (circles), and medium
frequencies by neutral contours (rectangles). Colour rein-
forces these associations, with higher frequencies repre-
sented by a cold colour (blue) and low frequencies by a
warm colour (orange).
• Onset density is categorized into high, medium, and low
levels, visually represented by shape-ﬁlling. Low density
is depicted as an empty shape, medium density as a half-
ﬁlled shape, and high density as a ﬁlled shape.

5.4.2 Perceptual relatedness metrics
The harmonic compatibility metrics employed in MixMash en-
compassed both small-scale and large-scale indicators based
on the perceptually-motivated Tonal Interval Space. Small-scale
harmonic compatibility combined perceptual relatedness and
dissonance, represented as distance metrics. A smaller distance
indicated a greater afﬁnity between the two tracks regarding
perceptual relatedness. Large-scale harmonic compatibility was
expressed as the distance from the major and minor key loca-
tions, indicating the association of a sample with a musical key .
Furthermore, MixMash computed descriptions for each audio
track’s rhythmic, spectral, and timbral attributes. Rhythmic con-
tent was characterised by the track note onset density (repre-
senting the ratio of the number of onsets to the track’s total
duration), spectral content was described using a 24-band per-
ceptual Bark frequency scale, and timbral content similarity was
computed using the cosine distance between frequency spec-
trum coefﬁcients.
Overall, this work aimed to expand the state-of-the-art by cre-
ating a visualisation that enabled a user to analyse and relate
a large collection of music tracks for mashup creation, with the
long-term goal of positively impacting user creativity .

Each lichen had unique characteristics and behaviours resem-

In this artefact, there were two ways of interacting with Glow-

5.4.3 Emotions
Glowing lichens make the invisible visible by absorbing, pro-
cessing, and reﬂecting the characteristics of the surrounding so-
cial communities. Using light as an interface, they gave form
to feelings and their transformations, reinforcing the emotional
qualities of the public space.
3 This digital artefact was exhibited at Reboot Fest 2019, a media-art festival.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

5.4 preliminary work 75
Although it could be perceived subtly to passers-by that Glow-
ing Lichen was a result of its surroundings, its visual charac-
teristics (colour + movement) established a mood intended to
reﬂect four behaviours: calm, happy , nervous, and aggressive:
• Calm: prefers environments with little movement. When
approaching an audience, the lichen collapses by decreas-
ing its size. It is disturbed by direct interaction, and posi-
tions are changed to avoid it.
• Happy: prefers hectic environments and direct interaction.
Its personality is demonstrated by frequent movements and
constant random changes in the colour of each of its LEDs,
thus provoking a joyful, festive atmosphere.
• Nervous: prefers well-lit spaces with little movement. It is
uncomfortable when people approach and highly unstable
when they interact with each other. The loss of colour and
fast and frantic random movements demonstrate this dis-
comfort and instability .
• Aggressive: behaves conﬁdently and seeks confrontation.
His personality becomes more evident in noisy and agi-
tated environments. Approaching people causes a colour
change of the lichen to red, and direct interaction causes
sudden and frantic changes in light intensity , thus alerting
the public to its aggressiveness.
As the lichens are contaminated by several causes — due to
their environment and direct interactions with human subjects
— they react and try to adapt to these changes by changing their
personality traits.
5.4.4 Life, Death, and Visual Expression
An important characteristic of these luminous organisms was
their life span. If the ecosystem reaches such an imbalance, they
could die. When such happens, a new organism is reborn, how-
ever, this time with another personality trait or predisposition.
As this process was dynamic, the ecosystem could begin with
one nervous and one calm organism.
Visually , the life of a lichen is represented by changes in shape,
colour, and movement (see Figure 54). A lower life span will
cause lower colour saturation/brightness and slower movements.
• A calm organism will have its life decreased if the audience
remains close to the artefact, touches it, or the environment
is noisy and has low luminosity .
• A happy organism will decrease its lifespan if the audience
is far from the artefact, not touching it, or the environment
is quiet and has a low luminosity .

5.4.4.1 Discussion
In future, to enhance public engagement with the organism, we
suggest incorporating auditory expressions, allowing them to
emit sound or respond to external stimuli. These auditory ele-
ments could create a more immersive and engaging experience.
Additionally , it would be interesting to expand the project’s in-
stallation to different locations, as this could reveal which per-
sonalities are more likely to thrive in speciﬁc public spaces.
On a computational note, the system could be improved with an
evolutionary algorithm so that lichens could adapt to their en-
vironment or even allow the ecosystem to create new personali-
ties based on their surroundings. Speciﬁcally , we see potential in
exploring interactions between organisms as a co-evolutionary
system between organisms and the environment.
5.5 summary
This Chapter described several techniques and methods for the com-
puter generation of music and images. We highlighted the role and
potential of rule-based programs and nature-inspired computation
for the intersection of multiple creative domains. To evaluate the ef-
fectiveness of the highlighted state-of-the-art approaches in address-
ing multidisciplinary problems, we began by exploring an interactive
sound visualisation for composing sound in mashup creation. Next,

The preliminary work showcased the computational techniques’

tic settings. However, further investigation is required to achieve a
more structured approach to the computational interplay of music,
images, and perceptual qualities within co-creative processes.

6APPROACHES FOR MUSIC AND IMAGE
GENERATION
In this chapter, we discuss a set of algorithmic explorations for mu-
sic and image generation and the incorporation of visual perceptual
qualities and emotions into these computational artefacts. We support
our hypothesis by conducting preliminary user tests 1.
6.1 experiments on music generation
6.1.1 Harmonic Chords
In a ﬁrst prototype, we created a Music Generator (MG) that used
harmonic chords and probabilistic rules to determine the placement
of notes to the root interval. By incorporating simple sound elements
such as notes’ frequency , volume, and duration, along with the char-
acteristics of harmonic chords, we were able to generate more intri-
cate and structured musical sequences. This approach allowed us to
explore the interplay between harmonies and individual notes, result-
ing in compositions with enhanced complexity and structure.
In our MG, chords were formed in the root position. Then, notes
would be positioned according to the root intervals and probabilistic
rules 2. For this purpose, we considered the upward interval they
form relative to the chord’s root. In brief, we would allow chords to
be formed as sequences of 3rds counting from the root up to the 13th
(see Table 6).
note interval 3rd 5th 7th 9th 11th 13th

to form a chord.
For example, if a note formed an upward 3rd interval with the root,
there would be a high chance (of 90 percent) for the note to be placed.
Notes that formed intervals of 7th, 9th, 11th and 13th could also be
placed on the chord, but with decreasing probabilities. 5ths were not
favoured but could also exist in the chords (lower probability). In sum,
when a note formed an interval with the root that did not meet that
condition, it could still be included in the chord, but only after being
transposed up or down one or more octaves to meet the condition. As
for a note forming a 2nd with the root may be transposed one octave
to form a 9th and thus be included in the chord. We should note that
1 This chapter aims to contribute to the research question 2: How can we map image
and music computationally?
2 These probabilistic rules try roughly to reﬂect the musical practice in current western
tonal music styles.

80 approaches for music and image generation
these rules do not distinguish major from minor intervals nor perfect
from diminished/augmented ﬁfth intervals, as we did not want to
impose additional constraints on the chord space.
By not including other musical qualities such as major, minor, di-
minished, or augmented chords, the music resulted in a restricted
harmonic palette and did not fully capture the variety of harmonic
progressions found in music. Also, this approach did not explicitly
address the management of musical dynamics and other relevant mu-
sical elements essential for creating expressive musical narratives.
Please consult Sections 8.1.2 and 8.1.3 for an example of this music
generation system employment.
6.1.2 Expressive Music
To assess the importance of music expressiveness, we conducted pre-
liminary work exploring the potential use of Deep Learning (DL) al-
gorithms in the context of artiﬁcial music generation (Briot, Hadjeres,
and Pachet, 2020). During our research, we observed that most artiﬁ-
cial music compositions generated by DL models lacked the desired
level of expressiveness when compared to compositions crafted by
human musicians (Carnovalini and Rodà, 2019). In turn, this high-
lighted the need to address the issue and motivated our hypothesis
that by incorporating expressive variations, particularly in note veloc-
ity and duration , to enhance the quality and perceived expressiveness
of the artiﬁcial compositions 3.
We conducted user tests to evaluate this practical approach„ in
which participants listened to and evaluated both human and arti-
ﬁcial music excerpts. The results of our experiments indicated that
aspects such as note velocity and duration played a crucial role in
conveying expressiveness in music compositions, as participants con-
sistently preferred compositions that exhibited these expressive ele-
ments over those that lacked them. Based on these ﬁndings, we con-
cluded that incorporating expressive components, speciﬁcally through
variations in note velocity and duration, is essential when generating
artiﬁcial music to achieve a certain level of depth (Simões, Machado,
and Rodrigues, 2019).
6.1.3 Discussion
Although we could advance our understanding of computer music
generation and important expressive qualities, these approaches did
not explicitly model or consider emotions in the generation process.
As emotions in music are complex and multidimensional, they can
be inﬂuenced by various musical elements such as harmony , melody ,
rhythm, and dynamics. Furthermore, ﬁner control over musical pa-
rameters is needed to generate abstract and ﬂexible music based on
PE. To this end, we designed and tested a second approach for music
3 As this was not the centre of our research, please read (Simões, Machado, and Ro-
drigues, 2019) paper for full detail on this experiment.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.2e m o t i o n-based music 81
generation based on PE. Here, we attempted to incorporate a broader
range of music elements while considering their expressive and per-
ceptual qualities regarding a set of emotions. We next describe this
experiment.
6.2 emotion -based music
Representing emotions with a computer dates from Picard’s concept
of Affective Computing (Picard, 1999). He defended that emotions are
an important part of human cognition and perception and thus have
a prominent role in assisting people with computational systems.
Historically , affective music experiments have been based on di-
mensional and discrete models of emotions. Dimensional models at-
tempt to identify a series of dimensions capable of representing all
possible emotional states (Brattico and Pearce, 2013; Buechel and Hahn,
2017; Russell, 1980; Scherer et al., 2000). However, a common criti-
cism of dimensional models is that they present limited dimensions
for modelling the complex world of human emotions. To overcome
this limitation, several authors have proposed discrete models of emo-
tion that use other categories to classify them and additional cate-
gories on the dimensional model of emotions. As we are trying to
limit the complexity of our experiments, we will rely on the two-
dimensional model proposed by Russell, where emotions are dis-
tributed by their arousal and valence, where we chose a sub-set of
four emotions evenly distributed in this 2D space (See Figure 55).
