Despite the potential of using emotions to compose music algorith-
mically , research on this subject has been limited to studying several
auditory parameters. They have mostly focused on studying the AP
of loudness, tempo, and harmony sonance (Gabrielsson and Lind-
ström, 2010; Juslin, Sloboda, et al., 2001). For example, consonant har-
monies have been associated with positive emotions, and loudness
and fast tempo to high arousal emotions (Dillen, 2020; Juslin, Sloboda,
et al., 2001).
To overcome this limitation, we have extended our research by in-
cluding more auditory characteristics as presented in Table 7 and
used it as baseline for generating music driven by four instances of PE
4. In this generator, we approached the music properties of harmony ,
tempo, pitch, melody direction, articulation, melody rhythm, and
loudness. To conﬁrm and validate the relationships between these
music-emotion associations, we performed a pilot user study , com-
paring the preferences of each participant to the literature ﬁndings
on the subject. This way , we better understood the computational rep-
resentation of music-emotion associations.
We note that this computational artefact, resulted from an iteration
of a system previously developed by Seiça et al. ( 2017), whose focus
was to musically represent emotions retrieved from Twitter. Built as a
probabilistic rule-based system, it produced a melodic line and a har-
monic progression according to the intended emotions. The melody

56 A): the one originally deﬁned for Anger, and the one for Happiness

[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.2e m o t i o n-based music 83
to just one set for all emotions (Figure 56 C), as it was a parameter

melodic lines.
Regarding harmony , the progressions were maintained (See exam-

harmony (Figure 57 A), playing either a more consonant or dissonant
sound. For the positive emotions (Happiness and Calm), consonance
keeps the chords intact, and the dissonance adds notes to the estab-
lished chords: for example, in a major chord, a minor 2nd, minor 3rd,
augmented 4th, augmented 5th or minor 7th can be added to the
chord structure, all with equal probability . For the negative emotions
(Sadness and Anger), dissonance retains the chord structure, and the
consonance transforms the dissonant notes of the chords (augmented
fourths, augmented ﬁfths and minor ﬁfths) in their consonant coun-
terparts, as perfect fourths and ﬁfths.

The tone quality was simpliﬁed, opting for a piano and a violin,
the ﬁrst to play both the melody and the harmony and the second
for the melody . The choice to reduce the number of timbres to just
two instruments, whose sound is familiar and well-recognized, was
to balance the tone inﬂuence in the emotion association. Therefore,
we could focus on evaluating the perceptual weight of the chosen
musical parameters with the minimum inﬂuence of other characteris-
tics.
6.2.2 Experiment Setup and User T ests
User tests were performed to evaluate the perceptual importance of
each musical parameter in the computational generation of these four
emotions - anger, calm, happy , and sad. Seven music properties were
tested: harmony , tempo, pitch, melody direction, melody articulation,
melody rhythm, and loudness. The melody rhythm was an excep-
tion we chose to test, despite little mention in the literature review,
as it was already implemented in Seiça et al. ( 2017) system, and we
considered it a relevant feature to evaluate. For each parameter, and
based on the literature ﬁndings, we established the expected values
for each emotion (as in Table 7), which would then be compared to
the participant’s choices. All the music parameters necessary for the
generation but not the user tests were kept immutable to isolate the
ones we wanted to evaluate.
Twenty participants performed the tests, with 12 male subjects and
8 female. Ages spawned from 22 to 45 years old, with an average of
27.8 years and a standard deviation of 4.88. We gathered a balanced
set of subjects regarding musical background since this could add
bias to the experience. The tests were performed in person to ensure
that the environmental conditions were consistent for all.
We designed an interface to provide a natural and easy way for par-
ticipants to interact with our system. This allowed users to explore a
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.2e m o t i o n-based music 85
Figure 60: Interface created for the user tests.
set of possible music parameter combinations. In sum, participants
had to aurally interpret the impact of the values of each music prop-
erty that best represented a given emotion. They could interact with
the MG in real time, listen to different combinations, and choose the
one most resembling a perceived emotion. If a participant considered
a particular musical parameter irrelevant in representing the emotion,
they could mark it as “indifferent” through a button.
Speciﬁcally , the user test would begin with a random combination
of musical parameters chosen by the system. This choice ensured that
the system would not inﬂuence the participants and avoid possible
fatigue during the test. The participants also had the possibility , at
any point, to listen to the starting point of the system and compare it
to their preferences or even restart the values to the initial set. Once
a participant reached the best possible combination, it would save
the chosen set and evaluate it from 1 to 5 (Likert scale) according
to its perception of the emotion representation. In this interval of
feedback exchange, no music was played so the participant could
have a moment to refocus and return to a neutral state of mind before
the next emotion. As the initial set of parameters for each emotion
was always random, the music could start close or distant from the
expected combination of parameters, ensuring a non-biased user’s
choice.
6.2.3 Analysis of Results
We have analysed user test results from the following perspectives: (i)
the time each participant took to reach a preferred combination; (ii)
the satisfaction/resemblance of his/her combination with the repre-
sented emotion; and (iii) the relationship between the participant’s an-
swers to literature ﬁndings. In addition, we divided the results of each
analysis among participants with and without musical backgrounds.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

86 approaches for music and image generation
Regarding time, people with no musical background took longer to
grasp the musical parameters and the changes caused by each value.
This difference was more prominent in happiness, with an average
of people with musical backgrounds taking 3:37 minutes to reach a
desired combination and those without taking 5 minutes. Happiness
was followed by Calm, with 1:05 minutes distinguishing the partici-
pants with and without musical background, Anger with 30 seconds,
and Sadness being the most balanced, with just 3 seconds.
The participants evaluation/resemblance of each emotion were mea-
sured with a Likert scale. According to this analysis, we consider sad-
ness to be the emotion with the best musical representation, with 11
participants choosing the highest classiﬁcation ( 5) of the Likert scale.
The three remaining emotions had their most popular classiﬁcation in
the fourth value. Above all, people with musical backgrounds tended
to give a higher classiﬁcation, which can be justiﬁed by a more accu-
rate understanding of music ﬂuctuations.
Concerning the relationship/distance between participant’s preferences
and literature ﬁndings, we performed two types of analysis: (i) dis-
tance to baseline; and (ii) the percentage of correct answers of each
music parameter.
The distance between the sets of values chosen and the baseline can
be seen in Figure 61. There we can see that despite Happiness having
less positive results in the Likert evaluation, it had the lowest vari-
ation, maintaining the distance between 0 and 3 parameters, which
shows a higher correlation with literature ﬁndings. On the other hand,
sadness and Anger did not have a chosen set equal to the expected,
with answers diverging along six parameters. Overall, most answers
are placed between the distances of 1 and 4, which shows a percep-
tual tendency and correlation to the ﬁndings of other authors on the
subject.

Regarding the perceptual relevance of musical parameters, melody
direction had the least impact on emotion representation, as reported
by seven out of twenty participants. Melody rhythm and harmony
were moderately relevant, while tempo and articulation received vary-
ing degrees of signiﬁcance.
6.2.4 Discussion
This study explored music-emotion associations through a generative
system, producing parameter-based music representing the emotions
of happiness, sadness, calm, and anger. Comparing empirical results
with ﬁndings from the literature provided a foundation for evaluating
associations and uncovering new connections.
Users with a musical background demonstrated faster test comple-
tion by accurately grasping musical ﬂuctuations, distinguishing them
from participants without a musical background. As suggested by
Juslin (Juslin, 2013; Juslin, Sloboda, et al., 2001) external factors can
also inﬂuence music perception. For instance, participants frequently
compared the system’s output to familiar songs or contexts, demon-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

88 approaches for music and image generation
strating evaluative conditioning. Additionally , some participants asso-
ciated the Algorithmic Composition (AC) with cinematic scenarios.
As for the perceived connections between music and emotions, re-
sults suggest multifaceted perspectives in the interpretation of emo-
tions such as calm and sadness. A preference for low pitch in repre-
senting Anger was found and a few participants suggested that Hap-
piness should have a faster rhythm and marked pacing for a deeper
perception of the emotion.
Future work may include expanding the musical features and in-
corporating timbre and rhythm to enrich the musical scenario. Also,
an extended user test with a larger sample size will strengthen sta-
tistical analysis, including pairwise comparisons, to assess variations
and further validate these ﬁndings.
6.3 experiments on image generation
In this section a series of artefacts were designed to enable the gener-
ation of abstract, two-dimensional visual compositions. Furthermore,
we investigate the incorporation of perceptual and affection qualities
in generating expressive images. The choice of working with abstract
images is motivated by a study of Melcher and Bacci ( 2013), which
suggests that abstract artworks can effectively convey emotions.
When working in computer art / generative design ﬁeld, it is es-
sential to recognise the importance of providing ﬂexibility in creative
explorations while imposing certain constraints to ensure coherence
and meaningful outcomes (Boden, 2009). Most literature studies on
generating visual artefacts tend to look at the image as the result
of the sum of isolated VP instead of a perceptual sum of its expres-
sive qualities. They often focus on a visual composition’s low-level
(e.g., colour, size, thickness ) and high-level properties (e.g., balance,
density , structure). This leads to an important question: How can we
transform a set of VP into a visual language without neglecting PR
principles?
6.3.1 Shape-Grammars
We ﬁrst proposed a shape-grammar (Stiny and Gips, 1971) inspired
implementation with two components: (i) a generator of visual sen-
tences to to deal with forms and promote a certain degree of plastic-
ity/creativity in a visual composition and (ii) an interpreter to treat
each visual sentence with a distinct expression, empowered by some
aesthetic perceptual principles (see Figure 63) (Arnheim, 1965; Ca-
vanaugh, MacInnis, and Weiss, 2015).
General rule, the system decisions were stochastic to promote a
natural expression without falling into the rigidity of a ruled-based
program. Additionally , we introduced some aesthetic notions to the
system (e.g. overlap limits) so that it could autonomously evaluate the
decisions of its creative process. To develop a computational visual
language, we relied on two essential aspects highlighted by Eubanks
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.3 experiments on image generation 89
Figure 63: "Shape Grammar" interface overview.
(1997): a set of symbols and a rules system to organise this set of
symbols. By carefully deﬁning these aspects, we can communicate a
visual language with a particular expression.
Similarly to most natural language approaches, we created a mod-
ule with a set of symbols or syntax and their corresponding organ-
isation (the generator) and a second module responsible for visual
communication, that is, the semantics of the visual language (the in-
terpreter) (see Figure 64). Each one has a distinct function in the pro-
cess. By adopting this modular approach, we could iterate over the
foundations of the language without compromising its ﬂexibility and
scalability .
Figure 64: Overview of the system architecture of two modules.
The ﬁrst module, generator, there are instances of a formal language
loosely inspired by shape grammars (SG) (Knight, 1989). Shape gram-
mars are known to be capable of producing an inﬁnite number of
forms, due to their ﬂexible organization of the grammar’s rules. Start-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

90 approaches for music and image generation
ing from an initial form, the rules are recursively applied to generate
a sentence of the language associated with the grammar, in this case,
a composition of different forms.
The visual grammar of the Image Generator (IG) is deﬁned by the
tuple VG = (VN,VT, S, P) where:
• VN, is a nonempty ﬁnite set of nonterminal shape elements (NF,
CH, FA, C, ST, OP)
• VT, is a nonempty ﬁnite set of terminal shape elements (br, ﬂ,
sw, cl, cv , ct, ol, op p, op r, op s, e)
• S, is the initial sentence or start symbol (F)
• P , is a nonempty ﬁnite set of pairs or transformation rules (LHD
> RHD) meaning the replacement of the left hand side in a sen-
tence by the corresponding right and hand side, e.g., F > CH;
The language associated with the grammar is deﬁned by the set of
visual sentences {v 2 T ⇤ : S ⇤! v} - e.g. (br, ﬂ, sw, cl, cv , ct, ol) The set
of production rules was deﬁned as follows in Table 8.
Table 8: Description of the grammar syntax and the production rules in
which the grammar relies to perform a sequence of decisions.

NF -F A CH - FA | OP | e FA - C, SW, ST, OL OP - OP_R | S | P e -F

A possible outcome of this grammar for one iteration could be the
following: [br, ﬂ, sw, cl, cv , ct, ol] | [op r]. The grammar can be iterated
as many times as needed. Nonetheless, as stated, the grammar does
not decide the semantics of its output which is an exclusive task of the
interpreter. The approach for the visual expression of this language
is depicted next.
The second module, interpreter, is responsible for ensuring a visual
expression that agrees with the generator. Building a composition on
top of a vocabulary of simple or geometric elements makes it eas-
ier to achieve and play with tensions, harmonies, and other percep-
tual effects (Collopy , Fuhrer, and Jameson, 1999). In this module, we
divided visual attributes in two categories: low-level and high-level
characteristics. While the ﬁrst is concerned with a direct interpreta-
tion of isolated properties of any form, the second deals with the
relationship between all of the forms present in a composition. This
is because low-level features contribute to the individual expression
of each visual element. This may include representing the VP of size
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.3 experiments on image generation 91
and thickness for example. On the other hand, high-level features en-
hance the overall perception of the composition by considering the
relationships between all the forms present in the image. The inter-
pretation of these features for the visual composition is as follows:
1. Low-level features
• Size: mapped to the size of forms. Ranges from small to
big size.
• Thickness: mapped as the stroke weight of forms. Ranges
from thin to thick contours. Both size and thick have the
ability to inﬂuence the perceived weight of a form.
• Fill: mapped to ﬁll the shape color content or remain just
as a stroke line color.
• Closure: open or close shape boundaries.
• Contours: interpreted as regular/irregular shape contours.
Regular will give rise to geometric and symmetric forms
while irregular give rise to geometrically undeﬁned and
asymmetric forms. This will inﬂuence the sensation of har-
mony , balance and tension.
• Curvature: round or sharp shape edges. This will inﬂuence
the sensation of softness/roughness as well as tension.
• Shape type: the ﬁnal form that a shape will acquire results
in the collective decisions of closure, contours and curva-
ture properties. In Figure 5 an example is given of this
process.
2. High-level features
• Balance: achieved through levels of choice, stability of val-
ues and symmetry .
• Complexity: using overlap of shapes allows not only to
create new forms but also to inﬂuence the complexity per-
ception of a piece. We limited the overlap to a maximum
intersection of two forms and a condition of distinct clo-
sures (open+close) - as Arnheim once suggested, “a subtle
complexity can be obtained by combining simple shapes”
(Arnheim, 1965).
• Contrast: contrast can be achieved by allowing opposite
expressions for the same perceptual property . In this case,
each visual element attribute can assume distinct values
- shapes (sharp/round), sizes (small/big), number of ele-
ments (one, many), stroke weight (thin, thick).
• Density: density is visually interpreted as an agglomera-
tion of points in a speciﬁc area of the composition. It can
be airy (more spaced) or dense (more condensed).
• Proportion: this regards the organization of all the elements
in the space of the composition. It can be based on a ge-
ometric, equally spaced grid or a chaotic and randomly
spaced set of points.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

92 approaches for music and image generation
• Spaciousness / Quantity: number of visual elements in
the composition. Ranges from clear (fewer elements) to
crowded (bigger number of elements).
• Rhythm: rhythm is mostly achieved through the use of geo-
metric operations of rotation, resize and translation. How-
ever it is also inﬂuenced by a set of predeﬁned levels of
representation, e.g., for size we consider the variation be-
tween three levels: small, medium and big. This latter divi-
sion also aids us to achieve a certain control of contrast.
• Variety: we achieve variety through a stochastic approach
in the selection of values for the attributes within the same
category , e.g., a sharp attribute can give form to a square
or to a triangle as they both ﬁt into the same perceptual
description.
6.3.2 Experiment Setup
The computational generation of an abstract visual language based
on a set of VP (Leborg, 2006) and visual principles of composition
(see 2.2). We shall note that we had to constrain our interpretation of
a composition to a limited set of both properties and principles as we
needed to break the complexity of the problem into small parts.
The generation of every composition (see Figure 63) begins in the
same way , with the generation of a grid of points. In turn, this process
has three main sub-steps: grid distribution (organized, random), grid
density(airy , dense), and grid crowdedness (clear, crowded). After-
wards, forms/shapes are placed on the top of generated grid points.
Once the grid is generated, forms and shapes are placed on top of the
grid points.
Then, the interpreter has the plasticity to provide a speciﬁc expres-
sion based on a global sensation. As we can see in Figure 65, even
though “round” has a 70 percent chance of being chosen, other less
likely characteristics can also be selected (such as sharp shapes). This
results in a static yet cumulative representation of elements. Such an
approach favors the representation of emotions computationally as a
cross-modal association between VP with emotions, and has been a
matter of study and interest in the CC research area.
To test this approach, we started by isolating the properties to iden-
tify perceptual oppositions. We then increased the complexity of the
generator by introducing variations in VP as Figure 66 illustrates.
Analysing the ﬁgure mentioned above, a-d illustrated the variation
in the spatial organisation within a grid. The second sequence e-f de-
picts contrasts of round and sharp forms, representing softness and
roughness. Here, we focused on working with the elements’ closure,
contours, and curvature. The third sequence g-j explores regular and
irregular contours for round and sharp forms. Regular forms (Figure
66g and 66i) exhibit balance, while irregular forms (Figure 66h and
66j) show tension. In the last sequence of the ﬁgure, k-p, we demon-

6.4e m o t i o n-based images 93
Figure 65: Decision tree of shapes’ characteristics.
strate variations of size, ﬁll, thickness, and overlap. We note that ﬁll
adds dynamics to the composition.
6.3.3 Discussion
This approach demonstrated the versatility and expressiveness nature
of an abstract visual language. The recursive grammar allowed for
content generation and modiﬁcation of visual elements’ composition.
While our system has its own aesthetic style due to limited forms, we
able to depict a certain degree of plasticity .
In future iterations, we intend to further investigate the boundaries
between the generator and interpreter, focusing on aspects that can
transition between the two. Other improvements may involve incor-
porating additional attributes in the interpreter, such as element spac-
ing, motion, colour, and opacity , to achieve a more distinct visual
expression. Ultimately , we aim to create conditions that allow for a
deeper complexity in representing A V relationships while keeping a
variety of PE.
6.4 emotion -based images
6.4.1 Abstract Faces and Dynamic Lines
Understanding how to evoke speciﬁc emotions through non-verbal
communication is crucial for developing design artefacts. However,
this area is still largely unexplored in design sciences. Various visual
aspects have been found to inﬂuence the perception of emotions. For
example, brighter colours have been associated with positive emo-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

94 approaches for music and image generation
e f

Figure 66: Illustration of visual compositions that can be generated, playing
with different system settings.
tions, whereas darker colors have been linked to negative emotions
(Lindborg and Friberg, 2015).
Inspired by Chernoff faces (Chernoff, 1973) modularity and nature,
we generate several abstract faces. To address the challenges men-
tioned earlier, we combined speciﬁc visual features with correspond-
ing emotions - for example associating red color or sharp lines with
the emotion of anger. These visual characteristics were then applied
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.4e m o t i o n-based images 95
to different facial elements such as the eyes, mouth, hair, and nose.
However, each visual feature value (e.g. blue colour or green colour)
was associated to weight (probability of occurring) depending on a
given emotion.
The visual alphabet consisted of both low-level features, including
line, shape, size, and color, and high-level features such as complexity ,
density , and texture. We also incorporated manipulations such as mo-
tion, repetition, and symmetry into the visual representations. Each
visual feature was assigned a weight, representing the probability of
its occurrence, based on the speciﬁc emotion it was associated with.
Figure 67 demonstrates the results of this approach, illustrating a set
abstract faces generated using image-emotion associations.
Figure 67: Algorithmic attempt at generating emotional faces. From left
to right: surprise, neutral, anger, happy , sad, surprise, joy ,
sad/anger.
Despite the expressive outputs and the potential of combining vi-
sual features to represent speciﬁc emotions, we soon abandoned this
approach. Faces inherently carry strong semantic meanings, such as
associating a smiling expression with happiness and a frowning ex-
pression with sadness. This semantic bias (e.g. happy smile vs sad
smile) could inﬂuence our perception of emotions, regardless of the
graphic variations introduced. To overcome this limitation, we pur-
sued a second approach.
This new approach aimed to capture visual emotions through line
segments. Line segments have long been regarded as the fundamen-
tal element of visual composition expression, and this notion ﬁnds
support in the works of renowned artists such as Paul Klee and Wass-
ily Kandinsky . For instance, Klee believed that lines possessed an in-
herent expressive quality , allowing them to represent a composition’s
movement, rhythm, and emotional states. Similarly , Kandinsky con-
sidered the line the "spiritual element" in his artworks, capable of
conveying profound emotions and spiritual concepts.

tion in this direct representation as the drawn line segments tended
to appear predominantly straight. This restricted the expressive pos-
sibilities, as we could not capture the dynamics and subtle perceptual
qualities associated with different emotions.

6.4.2 A Physics Perspective
To address the challenge of capturing the dynamics of emotions through
line segments, we approached the problem from a physics perspec-
tive. By applying a physics-based approach, we aimed to modulate
the motion of the lines, thereby achieving a visual expression that res-
onated with the intended emotional qualities. As a result, we focused
on representing associations in the vectorial domain, allowing us to
incorporate the principles of motion and dynamics into our graphic
representations. It is important to clarify that our primary objective
was not to isolate and test individual visual features but to explore
the combined effects of various features and visual aspects on the
perception of emotions.
To support this hypothsis we compiled a dataset of relevant fea-
tures that could be used in compositions centred around line seg-
ments (Cavanaugh, MacInnis, and Weiss, 2015; Lindborg and Friberg,
2015; Scherer and Oshinsky , 1977). The resulting dataset can be found
in Table 9. The movement of line was determined by (i) a set of visual
characteristics and (ii) time (duration and repetition of events). This
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.4e m o t i o n-based images 97
is, time determined whether the current visual conﬁguration was re-
peated or not. A visual demonstration of these can be found in the
Appendix, Section .2. By incorporating physics principles into the vi-
sual expression, we aimed to add a dynamic dimension to the repre-
sentation of emotions through line segments. In particular, variations
in angular velocity enhanced the overall expressiveness of the visual
language.

Figure 70: Visual morphology overview.
The visual grammar was interpreted as follows:
• A “regular" characteristic means that the visual motif would re-
peat itself in regular periods of time, and “irregular" means that
the visual motif would be inconsistent and change within these
time intervals. General rule, when an irregular conﬁguration
is applied, the visual conﬁguration does not follow a strict pat-
tern or predictable sequence (e.g. size variation, stroke variation,
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

98 approaches for music and image generation
among others). This is obtained through a randomization pro-
cess within a limited range of values.
• The terms "straight" and "wavy" were used to describe different
types of line movement. "Straight" implied a constant angular
velocity , meaning that there were no variations in the speed or
direction of the line’s movement, except for irregular continuity .
On the other hand, "wavy" implied line movement with varia-
tions in angular velocity . The angular velocity could range from
soft changes to achieve a smooth or rounded path, to abrupt
changes to achieve a sharp path.
• The characteristic of "density" referred to the distance between
the current location of the line and the next location. A smaller
distance resulted in a higher density , while a larger distance
resulted in a lower density . This allowed for variations in the
spacing and concentration of the line segments.
• Lastly ,"stroke" was used to vary the thickness of the line. By
adjusting the stroke thickness, the visual weight and emphasis
of the line could be modiﬁed, adding another dimension to the
visual representation of emotions through line segments.
Figure 71 presents an example of the employment of the proposed
visual grammar.

6.4.3 Experiment Setup
To validate the associations and combinations of visual features for
line motion, an experiment was conducted involving a total of 45
participants. The participants had an average age of 31.8 years with a
standard deviation of 10.3. Among the participants, 51.1% identiﬁed
as female and 48.9% as male. In addition to demographic information,
the participants were also asked whether they had a background in
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.4e m o t i o n-based images 99
visual communication. Among the participants, 33.3% indicated that
they had a background in visual communication.
The user study was divided into two parts. In the ﬁrst part, partic-
ipants were presented with an image that was considered to be the
most representative of a speciﬁc emotion. They were then asked to
identify the emotion depicted in the image. This allowed us to assess
how well the visual representation conveyed the intended emotion,
as perceived by the participants.
In the second part of the study , participants were shown four differ-
ent visual animated expressions for each emotion. They were asked
to choose the animation that they deemed most representative of
the given emotion. Additionally , participants had the option to se-
lect "none of the above" if they felt that none of the provided options
accurately represented the intended emotion.
By including the option of "none of the above," we acknowledged
the possibility that participants may not ﬁnd any of the presented
visual expressions to be a suitable representation of the emotion.
Additionally , the visual conﬁgurations used in the ﬁrst part of the
experiment can be consulted in Table 10).

6.4.4 Analysis and Discussion
In Part 1, the visual conﬁgurations that succeeded the most, were
those related with the following emotions: “calm" ( 77.8%), “anger"
(71.1%), and “happy" (68.9%) respectively . Surprisingly , the same com-
bination of features in the representation of “anger" in the second part
of the test was chosen by a slightly smaller number of participants
(46.7%). “Sad" ( 35.6%) was the emotion where participants had the
biggest difﬁculty to associate with the presented animation. This is
congruent with results obtained in the second part of the experiment
where this same representation of sadness was chosen by 26.7% of
participants (see Table 11 and Table 12).
Additionally , associations were made to other emotions than the
expected. For instance, “Anger" and “Calm" visual representations
were associated with “happy" and “sad" emotions. “Happy" visual
representation was associated to “calm" and “sad" emotions. “Sad"
was associated to “calm" and “anger" emotions. In spite of this, we
should note that the associations to other emotions than the desired
ones were made by a small amount of participants (no more than
17.8%, see Table 11). Overall, all the other emotion classiﬁcation by

Comparing the results obtained in our experiments and the associ-
ations established in the literature, we have conﬁrmed some relevant
relationships between visual characteristcs and emotions:
• For the emotion of "anger," our ﬁndings align with the liter-
ature regarding the associations with wavy lines, sharp lines,
high density , big size, and medium stroke. This indicates a con-
sistency between our experimental results and the established
knowledge regarding the visual representation of anger.
• In the case of "calm," we found associations with regular con-
tour, round edges, and thick strokes. While these features align
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.4e m o t i o n-based images 101
with the literature, our experiments also revealed the relevance
of additional visual features such as irregular continuity , wavy
curves, low density , and medium size.
• Regarding the emotion "happy ," our ﬁndings correspond to round
edges, big size, and thin strokes, which align with the literature.
However, our experiments also highlighted the importance of
irregular contours, wavy curves, and medium density in the vi-
sual representation of happiness.
• In the case of "sad", our ﬁndings exhibited some discrepancies
with the established associations in the literature. While the lit-
erature emphasises regular contours and thick strokes for sad-
ness, our experiments identiﬁed the relevance of straight curves,
low density , and irregular contours in the visual representation
of sadness.
Based on these tests, we conclude that the associations that best
matched the literature ﬁndings on emotions and visual features cor-
responded to the feeling of "anger." In contrast, the associations for
"sadness" showed the weakest alignment with the established knowl-
edge. As these experiments suggest, a certain degree of complexity
and subjectivity is involved in the visual perception of emotions. Fur-
thermore, a challenge in these approaches was transposing the asso-
ciations between visual features and emotions into an abstract visual
language while maintaining the desired expressiveness and modular-
ity . Thus, this suggests the need for further exploration and reﬁne-
ment to generate a visual language of emotions.
6.4.5 A Visual Language mediated by Emotions
Based on the language developed in Chapter 4 and the experiments
described in the previous sections of this chapter, we have iterated
to reﬁne the solution for generating abstract compositions mediated
by emotions. Similarly to the work present in Sub-Section 6.3.1, this
artefact was composed of a generator and an interpreter. While the
ﬁrst incorporates speciﬁc algorithm rules, the second acts as a bridge
between the computational output and the human perception of the
artwork expression. Speciﬁcally , the generator provides the ﬂexibil-
ity and creativity to generate diverse visual elements, and the inter-
preter ensures that these elements effectively convey the desired ex-
pressive qualities (whether they are motivated by emotions or other
constraints). Figure 72 presents an overview of the artefact architec-
ture.
6.4.6 The generator
In its current form, the generator of images can be used without a
context (e.g. randomly) or given a set of properties and values as-
sociated to a speciﬁc emotion, for example, by providing different

in Figure 73.

Figure 73: Image generation process exempliﬁed for the emotion of "happy".
As for the interpreter, one of the main differences compared to pre-
vious ones is that we have structured the visual composition in two
layers: foreground and background. In this solution, the foreground
layer did draw low-level attributes, focusing on the details and local
objects’ expressions in the composition, such as line, shape, size, or
colour. On the other hand, the background layer addressed high-level
attributes that inﬂuence the overall composition and context, such as
complexity , density , or texture. The background layer’s primary func-
tion was to enhance the overall visual expression/sensation, comple-
menting the foreground elements. The interaction between these two
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

6.4e m o t i o n-based images 103
layers allowed us to capture the complexity and richness of emotions
in an abstract form.
The shared visual elements between the layers, such as "edge", "reg-
ularity", "shape type", "size", "colour", and "visual distribution", en-
sure continuity and coherence between the foreground objects and
the background context. Additionally , the background layer intro-
duces speciﬁc attributes limited to its domain, such as "crowdedness",
"complexity", and "orientation", which will further contribute to the
overall emotional expression. Figure 76 illustrates the interpreter.
We note that all images focused on a two-dimensional abstract
expression to keep the subjectivity as suggested by Melcher et al.
(Melcher and Bacci, 2013) and enhance the potential to represent dis-
tinct emotions. This resulted in a static but cumulative representation
of sensations instead of a direct and time-based approach, as it is
commonly found in the state-of-the-art. As Figure 74 illustrates, this
approach brought the ability to generate a broad set of expressive
abstract images, preserving the delicate balance between algorithmic
rules and creative expression.

6.5 summary
Inspired by the works of abstract painters and computer art artists, we
recognised the potential of line segments to evoke and communicate
visual emotions and explore their expressive power to create a unique
language of visual expression.
By investigating the computational interplay of auditory and visual
elements, we aimed to better understand human perception while
gaining an overview of the most suitable techniques to navigate this
multi-dimensional problem. We next explore a framework to formalise
the computational representation of CMA.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

7AC R O S S - M O D A LF R A M E W O R K
During the course of this work, we handled the following key chal-
lenges: the abstract representation of knowledge between multiple
domains and its computational expression. One of the challenges we
faced was the act of balancing freedom with rigid rules, a topic long
discussed in the ﬁeld of CC (Computational Creativity) (Boden, 2009)
in the evolution of cross-modal relationships. Additionally , we han-
dled the challenge of generating a dynamic/adaptive solution that
allowed the interplay of multiple dimensions.
To address this, we proposed a framework with guidelines for the
computational exploration of CMA between music, image, and CS.
This framework not only opens the possibility to expand our work
but also to improve the different modules iteratively as new research
and developments emerge 1.
7.1 principles and components
Designing a framework for the computational exploration of A V re-
lationships required identifying theoretical concepts and principles
relevant to these ﬁelds, so we could organise these concepts to guide
the research and development of CMA.
• Audience: We designed a tool to support researchers and prac-
titioners investigating the interplay between music, image, and
emotions.
• Emotion and Perception Coherence: By capturing and express-
ing emotions coherently in both domains, we aimed to create
a synchronised emotional experience for the audience. More-
over, we recognise that emotions are subjective and cumulative.
Therefore, we prioritised capturing the overall perceived expres-
sion rather than a direct translation of the temporal dimension
of music.
• Consistency: It was important to maintain the consistency be-
tween the properties of image and music, ensuring that the au-
ditory and visual elements do not interfere negatively .
• Algorithmic Rules and Creativity: The framework aimed to fos-
ter a balance between the structured rules of algorithms and
freedom of creativity , enabling innovative and engaging A V out-
comes while maintaining a coherent framework.

• Constraints: The present framework operates within the con-

Given the domains’ complexity , abstract nature, and interdisciplinary ,
it was essential to accommodate ﬂexibility and adaptability in the
framework’s architecture. To manage this challenge, we structured
the framework into four modules (I-IV). These modules work in an in-
tegrated manner, forming a cohesive framework for exploring A V re-
lationships. This is, each module addresses a speciﬁc aspect and con-
tributes to the overall functionality of a system — Figure 78 presents
an overview of the framework modules. They were designed as fol-
lows:
module i A vocabulary of music, image, and emotions;
module ii Deﬁnition of grammar rules for computational expres-
sion of the language music;
module iii Deﬁnition of grammar rules for the computational ex-
pression of the language image;

7.1 principles and components 109
module iv Implementation of an evolutionary process. This last
point enabled an adaptive and dynamic exploration of possi-
bilities within the A V grammar.
In this framework, the MG and IG are integral components of the
A V language and are designed to support the expression of emotions
and sensations. However, they are open to more than this context and
offer a ﬂexible solution for other possibilities.
It is important to note that grammar does not explicitly deﬁne spe-
ciﬁc interconnections between the auditory and visual elements. In-
stead, we propose an Evolutionary Engine (EE) to explore and en-
hance the A V relationship. The EE should allow for an adaptive and
dynamic exploration of combinations within the A V grammar. In ad-
dition, the EE will enable the system to generate music and images
autonomously or to incorporate user input through an interactive ap-
proach, helping uncover preferences and generate creative mappings.

The grammar proposed in Modules II and III includes aspects such
as the relationship between musical elements and emotions or visual
elements and emotion, in addition to the composition’s overall aes-

7.1.3 Music Generator (Module II)
Module II refers to the MG, which is an instance of a system previ-
ously developed by Seiça et al. (Seiça et al., 2019) (see Section 6.2).
This artefact was built as a rule-based system through probabilities
and guided by two signiﬁcant musical aspects: harmony and melody .

The generator shapes the music composition according to the prob-

termined as follows:
• Melody Intervals have a 5% for a 1st, 35% for a 2nd, 30% for a
3rd, 5% for a 4th, 10% for 5th, 5% for a 6th, 5% for a 7th, 5% for
an 8th.
• Melody Direction has 50% probability to be ascending, and 50%
to be descending.
• T onalityalways follows the western tonal system rules.
• Articulation has 50% probability to be legato, and 50% to be stac-

to play both the melody and the harmony , and the second for
the melody . The choice to reduce the number of timbres to just
two instruments, whose sound is familiar and well-recognized,
was to balance the tone inﬂuence in the emotion association.
• Rhythm in melody was deﬁned by whole notes with 10% of
occurrence, half notes with 20%, quarter notes with 50%, and
eighth with 20%.
• Patterns occur in melody sequences and harmony progressions
within an emotional state.

114 a cross -modal framework
7.1.4 Image Generator(Module III)
Module III refers to image generation. This is an instance of the work
previously developed in Section 6.4 where we built a composition
with a vocabulary of simple or geometric elements.
As we wanted the resulting compositions to be more expressive
than the sum of isolated properties, we have drawn upon a central
concept to developing these images: depth layers (foreground and
background). The ﬁrst layer is concerned with directly interpreting
isolated properties of any form (low-level features), while the second
deals with the relationship between all forms present in a composi-
tion (high-level features).
For the properties aimed only at composition (C) purposes, we as-
signed a neutral state as follows:
• Thickness: medium-thin stroke value so it doesn’t impact the em-
phasis of the visual elements.
• Closure closed shape for foreground and overlapping objects
and open shape for background objects.
• Shape Fill: overlapping objects are submitted to a 50% chance of
being ﬁlled with colour.
• Opacity: we introduce a small opacity to the composition objects
so we can view foreground and background layers.
• Movement: although these images are static, the placement of ob-
jects in a diagonal is desired to achieve the sensation of motion.
• Depth: images feature types (low-level and high-level) are placed
in two layers - foreground and background, respectively .
• Variation: variation is achieved through generativity in some
properties. For example, we vary the selection of shapes and
colour tones within their levels.
The emotion associations with image properties can be consulted
in the following Table 17.

7.1.5 Module IV: Evolution
The last module of the framework, the Evolutionary Engine (EE), il-
lustrated in Figure 83, presents a solution for the computational ex-
ploration and expansion of the auditory-visual language. Generally
speaking, EA work with populations of solutions to help determine
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

116 a cross -modal framework
Figure 83: Module IV . Evolutionary Engine.
where to go next in the space of possibilities. In this process, the com-
puter is instructed to keep the best solutions and let the worst die. The
better solutions are then allowed to reproduce, causing an evolution
to occur. Additionally , a ﬁtness function is typically used to measure
the solution’s quality; this is a score of how well the solution ﬁts the
objective (Bentley and Corne, 2002).
A design concern when developing this module was the possibility
of navigating the dialogue in two directions, that is, from MI or Im-
age to Music (IM). Given this problem’s abstract and multi-domain
nature, we opted for evolutionary programming methods to explore
the set of associations. The adaptability inherent to Module IV allows
for a broad application of techniques. For instance, it can address dif-
ferent evolutionary strategies based on the requirements of the task at
hand, from optimising mappings to exploring emergent behaviours
in more complex systems as the next Chapter 8 exempliﬁes.
7.2 summary
To effectively systematise the computational generation of A V lan-
guages, we developed a framework based on evolutionary processes,
which in turn, facilitated the exploration of creative domains through
a common language. Crafting and formalizing this approach was
challenging but essential to achieve consistent experimentation and
signiﬁcant analysis of results.
The presented framework aims to the expansion of the A V lan-
guage in two ways: (i) automatically evolving a system of relation-
ships according to a predeﬁned theoretical target; (ii) evolving and
adapting the A V grammar according to user preferences. This dual
approach offered the following advantages:
• Explainable Evolution: By evaluating the evolutionry system
with previously studied A V relationships connections, we are
able to extract insights on the perceptual motivations of users
and the be able to interpret the evolutionary process outcomes
in a coherent and logic way .
• User-Driven Adaption: Adapting the relationships based on user
preferences by allowing them to personalise the relationships,
we can foster a more responsive interaction between the user
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

7.2 summary 117
and the system. This increases the expressive capabilities of the
outputs and deepens the insights of cognitive processes associ-
ated with perception of A V Languages.
• Iterative/Evolutionary Process: By relying on an iterative/evo-
lutionary process we allowed the system to learn and adapt,
contributing to research of Creativity Support Tools (CST) and
techniques.
• Scalability and Flexibility: This adaptability ensures that our
framework can be applied across various scenarios and user
preferences, making it a robust tool for exploring CMA.
Although this provided a foundation to computationally express
and explore relationships between the domains of music, image and
emotions its utility can be extend beyond these areas due to its in-
herent modularity and ﬂexibility . Chapters 9 and 10 demonstrate the
application of this framework within the scope of this thesis.

8THE AUDIO-VISUAL INTERPLA Y
The present chapter describes a series of experiments where we ex-
plored computational techniques for the interplay between music and
image elements. Following the framework presented in Chapter 7,
our primary focus was to investigate whether Evolutionary Compu-
tation (EC) methods could facilitate the interaction and integration of
music and images to bridge underlying connections and form percep-
tually relevant relationships between these two domains. To address
this computational challenge, we ﬁrst employ artiﬁcial life environ-
ments based on agent-based modelling and genetic algorithms, and
later employ GP techniques 1.
8.1 exploration n º 1: artificial life systems
Although we departed from the previously mention cross-modal frame-
work principles, we chose to simplify the expression of music and im-
ages to basic elements, narrowing the scope of this set of explorations
and allowing for a more focused analysis of the computational capa-
bilities in handling A V interplay .
8.1.1 A Swarm of Fireﬂies
Inspired by the visual nature of ﬁreﬂies (Figure 84), we explored an
artiﬁcial environment to visually express sound 2.

This departure from biological plausibility (digital ﬁreﬂies) is jus-

behaviour as a basis, we achieved visually interesting patterns and
movements that responded to sound characteristics. In this artefact,
digital ﬁreﬂies were attracted to light sources — a visual metaphor
for sound objects. When the ﬁreﬂies are close to the light, they gain
energy , and their bio-luminescence is emphasised, creating visually
captivating effects (see Figure 85). It is important to note that while

the behaviour of real-world ﬁreﬂies do not typically exhibit swarm

8.1.1.1 Experiment Setup
In this environment, the digital ﬁreﬂies were fed by the energy of
sound beats (rhythmic onsets). While responding to the surrounding
elements of the environment, they searched for these energies. Figure
86 illustrates the dynamics of an emergent environment.
Figure 86: Highlights of distinct moments in the environment (by order of
occurrence). The environment contains light sources representing
sound beats, which attract the ﬁreﬂies. The closer a ﬁreﬂy is to
the light, the more intensiﬁed its bioluminescence and the higher
its chance of collecting energy (life).
Sound analysis and representation : A preliminary analysis of sound
was made, where we identiﬁed sound objects on differente each mu-
sic samples, characterising them in 3 parameters: frequency , ampli-
tude and duration 3. The frequency determined the pitch of the sound,
amplitude determined how loud the sound is, and duration deﬁned
the rhythm of music and instants of music (sound beats).
After the sound analysis was performed, the sound objects were
mapped into a corresponding visual representation (see Figure 87):
• High frequencies (HIF) were positioned on the top of the screen,
and low frequencies (LOF) emerged in a lower position in the
vertical axis.
• Amplitude was translated into the object’s size, i.e., the size
is directly proportional to the amplitude. Lastly , frequency is
mapped into the object’s vertical position in the environment.
• Sound beats were mapped into instants (t 1,t 2,t 3,. . . ) which
deﬁned the objects horizontal position. Each sound object had
a pre-deﬁned duration, meaning that it is removed from the
environment at the end of its duration.
An additional characteristic presented in this artiﬁcial environment
regarding sound objects is collation 4. This way , when an object col-
lides with another one, a contrary force is applied, separating them.
3 The detected note onsets (or sound beats) were related to the temporal/horizontal
position of a sound event.
4 Although it is not directly related to sound, it is intended to aid the sound represen-
tation in a dynamic way .

Figure 87: Graphical representation of sound objects. (a) Sound beats in-

Fireﬂies (agents) behavior and graphic representation : The colours
were chosen according to the real nature of ﬁreﬂies. Since they are
visible at night, we opted for a dark blue in the background and
a brighter blue for the sound beats. As for bio-luminescence of the
digital ﬁreﬂies, we used yellow. Conversely , the agent’s size corre-
sponds to its energy at a given moment. Its size increased as the
ﬁreﬂy approached a light source and gained energy . When the ﬁreﬂy
lost energy or died, it would decrease in size or disappear from the
environment. A larger size indicated a higher energy level.

Because the sounds are presented from left to right, the agents are
initially born on the left side of the screen, vertically centred. Addi-

independence and resembled their original behaviour. The closer the
ﬁreﬂies were to a source of light (or sound objects), the more they are

Regarding the agents motion, they walked randomly until they
found something that affected their behaviour, such as a source of
light or other agents. We applied Reynolds ( 1999) ﬂocking behaviour
rules, meaning that neighbour agents could see each other and follow
them through. A demonstration video can be found in .2.
8.1.1.2 Discussion
In this experiment, we explored a multi-agent environment for sound
visualisation, aiming to understand the potential of intersecting com-
puter art and nature-inspired systems. For such, we examined the
system’s behaviour in response to four songs or melodic sequences.
These music tracks varied in rhythm, intensity , and frequency , allow-
ing us to illustrate and highlight how the system responded to dif-
ferent sound stimuli. Within the context of this thesis, swarm simu-
lations revelead particularly interesting because they allowed for an
expression of a large variety of different types of behaviours and were
interpreted as an intuitive and natural forms of interaction.

8.1.2 Harmonic Constellations
Building on the potential of the previous approach, we proposed to
explore multi-agent systems’ (MAS) mechanisms for chord formation
and the visual representations of music’s chords and notes with an
interactive setup. In this particular case, instead of analysing pre-
existing tracks, we explore music generation in real-time. This way ,
we enabled the exploration of new mappings between visual and au-
ditory stimuli in an interactive and responsive way .

ometrical objects. This geometrical approach was inspired by Munari

8.1.2.1 Experiment Setup
In this environment, we had two types of agents: notes and chords
(see Figure 91). Both were initially displayed in the environment ran-
domly . In general, chords wandered in the environment, looking for
notes within their ﬁeld of vision 5. Both notes and chords were posi-
tioned initially in a random location. This random placement ensured
a diverse starting point as the agents had no prior knowledge or pre-
deﬁned positions, allowing for unbiased emergent behaviours.
Figure 91: "Harmonic Constellations" interface overview.
Sound characterisation : In this version of the prototype, chords
were formed in root position. Notes were positioned in relation to
the root at intervals that are multiples of thirds. In detail, a chord
agent starts empty . Once a ﬁrst note is caught up, it assumes the role
of the chords root. Subsequent notes seen in the vision ﬁeld can be
5 An agent’s ﬁeld of vision is deﬁned by an angle and a distance depth to then capture
them according to a set of musical rules
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

8.1e x p l o r a t i o n nº 1: artificial life systems 125
caught according to probabilistic rules that take into consideration
the upward interval they form relative to the chords root. If a note
forms an upward 3rd interval with the root, there is a high chance
(of 90 percent) that the note will be caught. Notes that form intervals
of 7th, 9th, 11th and 13th, can also be caught, but with decreasing
probabilities. 5ths are not favoured, but can also exist in the chords
(lower probability).
We allowed chords to be formed as (possibly incomplete) sequences
of 3rds counting from the root, up to the 13th. We should clarify that
these probabilistic rules are not based on systematic empirical work,
however they tried to roughly reﬂect the musical practice in current
popular styles. Moreover, these rules do not distinguish major from
minor intervals, nor perfect from diminished/augmented ﬁfths inter-
vals, as we do not want to impose additional constraints to the chord
space to explore.
Audio-visual mappings: We used the metaphor of star constella-
tions to create visually recognisable music patterns (see Figure 92).
In essence, the musical features of pitch, loudness and note interval
were translated into four visual features (shape, lightness, size and
line width):
Figure 92: Concept
of "harmonic
constellations".
• Pitch: The pitch of a note was visually expressed through dif-
ferent object shapes. Inspired by the study by Ramachandran
and Hubbard on the "kiki" and "bouba" effect (Ramachandran
and Hubbard, 2001), we associated higher pitches with sharper
object shapes and lower pitches with softer object shapes. This
mapping aimed to convey the perceived softness or sharpness
of the sound based on its pitch characteristics.
• Loudness: A note’s loudness was represented by its visual shape’s
lightness and size. We used the energy metaphor, where notes
with higher energy (louder) were depicted as larger and brighter
shapes. In comparison, notes with lower energy (softer) appeared
smaller and dimmer shapes. This mapping was founded by
studies that correlated the visual characteristics of lightness and
size with the perception of loudness (Lindborg and Friberg,
2015; Marks, 1996).
• Notes interval : The interval between two successive notes in a
chord was translated into a direct mapping of the connection
length between their object shapes.
Figure 93: Springs
system illustrated.
In addition to these feature associations, a spring system (Figure
93) was applied to each chord to enhance the relationships between
notes. As for the users’ interaction, they could listen to each chord
and manipulate their position within the environment. A demonstra-
tion video can be found in Appendix, Section .2.
8.1.2.2 Discussion
In this study , we presented an agent-based system that facilitates the
exploration of musical chords through a graphic language in an inter-
active environment. The mappings between musical characteristics
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

126 the audio -visual interplay
and visual elements, such as pitch, loudness, and interval, provided
visual cues for the users on the nature of the music inhabiting a spe-
ciﬁc artiﬁcial environment. Furthermore, the user could listen to each
chord and move it in the environment.
Although we developed a functional prototype, further develop-
ments are still needed. A limitation of the presented system was the
static nature of the chords once the notes were absorbed, making it
predictable and somehow static. To address this limitation, we pro-
pose implementing a new algorithmic approach where external fac-
tors can be introduced to impact the formation of the chords’ notes.
Moreover, we note that one of the objectives of this thesis was to
develop a computational artefact that could explore a bilateral rela-
tionship between music and image. As such, a shared limitation of
the approaches described in previous experiments, regards the one-
way mapping of the A V relationship. Although the characteristics of
auditory stimuli clearly impacted the visual expression, the manip-
ulation or characteristics of visual stimuli did not impact the music
or sounds being generated. Therefore, we next present a novel ap-
proach to explore this challenge through Interactive Genetic Algo-
rithms (Sims, 1992).
8.1.3 Evolving L-Systems with Music Notes
Interactive genetic algorithms, ﬁrst proposed by Karl Sims, provided
a framework for interactive evolution, allowing users to participate
actively in the evolution and exploration of solutions. By incorporat-
ing an IGA into this experiment, we intended to involve the user in
exploring and inﬂuencing A V relationships. Moreover, we aimed to
foster a computational solution where audio and visual stimuli could
inﬂuence and shape each other.
For the interface metaphor, we researched the possibilities of us-
ing L-systems (Prusinkiewicz and Lindenmayer, 1990) to express the
A V language. Although L-systems have been employed in music gen-
eration or composition contexts (Kaliakatsos-Papakostas, Floros, and
Vrahatis, 2012; Lourenço, Ralha, and Brandao, 2009; Nelson, 1996; Pes-
tana, 2012; Soddell and Soddell, 2000), it is not common to ﬁnd the
opposite (image to music) or even less usual to ﬁnd attempts to have
it working in bilateral ways.
8.1.3.1 L-Systems
Lindenmayer Systems were ﬁrst proposed by Prusinkiewicz and Lin-
denmayer ( 1990) to study the development processes that occur in
multicellular organisms like plants (see Figure 94).
Figure 94:
L-systems
illustrated.
Formally , they are parallel rewriting systems operating on strings
of symbols; this is, a tuple G =( V, !, P), where V is a non-empty
set of symbols, ! is a special sequence of symbols of V called axiom,
and P is a set of productions, also called rewrite rules, of the form
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

8.1e x p l o r a t i o n nº 1: artificial life systems 127
LHS ! RHS. LHS is a non-empty sequence of symbols of V and the
RHS a sequence of symbols of V. An example of L-systems is:
G =( {F, [, ], +}, F, {F ! F[F][+F]})
As a generative system, a L-system works by starting with the
axiom, iteratively rewriting in parallel all symbols that appear in a
string using the production rules. Using the previous example of L-
system we obtain the following rewritings:
F 1! F[F][+F] 2! F[F][+F][F[F][+F]][+F[F][+F]] 3! ...
After n rewritings, we say we obtain a string of level n. The axiom is
considered the string of level 0. The symbols that appear in the string
were interpreted as elements of a particular structure. A classical in-
terpretation, the one used here, is the turtle interpretation, where the
symbols of a string are commands for a turtle moving in a 2D world.
The state of the turtle is deﬁned by two attributes: position (x, y) and
orientation ↵. The commands change these attributes eventually have
side effects (e.g., drawing a line).
8.1.3.2 Experiment Setup
The evolution of the visual expressions and music sequences occurred
in a multi-agent system scenario, where the L-systems were the agents
inhabiting a world populated with MIDI (Musical Instrument Digital
Interface) musical notes, which were the resources that these agents
sought to absorb. To evolve the sequence of notes and the correspond-
ing L-system, we recurred to an Evolutionary Algorithm. The EA was
interactive, meaning the user was responsible for assigning a ﬁtness
value (Sims, 1992).
The environment: Similarly to the multi-agent approaches in previ-
ous experiments, there were two types of entities in the environment:
agents and notes (as in Figure 95). Notes had immutable attributes:
their position and value. Also, they did not die or evolve over time. In
contrast, agents were entities with two components: (i) an L-system
that drove its visual expression and (ii) a sequence of notes that de-
ﬁned the L-system’s parameters at each level of rewriting. Agents
would move in the world by random walk, looking for notes to ap-
pend to their sequence. These notes were subject to change over time
through an Interactive Genetic Algorithm (IGA) (Sims, 1992).
Figure 95:
Environment’s
elements:
individuals and
notes.
The environment (Figure 96) would begin with a non-evolved in-
dividual (level 0) that wandered in the environment catching notes.
In this case, its growth was determined by the musical notes that it
would catch, creating a sequence of notes. The ﬁrst note caught would
makes it evolve to level 1, the second note to level 2 and so forth 6.
When the user selected an individual there were four possible op-
erations at the level of the interface: (i) Listen to the musical sequence
of the individual in question note by note; (ii) Listen to the musical
sequence of the individual in question in a simultaneous way (all

Apply a mutation, (iv) Choose two different parents, and apply a

erated from the selected one through the process of mutation and
crossover.
Audio-visual mappings: The representation of music had two dis-
tinct parts: (i) the visual representation of the notes spread across the
environment and (ii) the notes that the L-systems (individuals) catch.
In the ﬁrst representation, notes, was static because it directly rep-
resents the notes’ parameters. These notes were placed in the envi-
ronment as circles with a grey scale representing volume and size
representing note duration. The pitch was not represented.
Figure 97: Mapping process of the note’s characteristics (a) pitch, (b) dura-

The notes caught by the individual inﬂuenced its visual expression.
Broadly , musical notes were decomposed into auditory elements —
pitch, duration, and volume in the current implementation — each
mapped into corresponding visual parameters of the L-system: line
length, width, colour, and turning angle (see Figure 97). For instance,
the note’s duration affected branch length, volume inﬂuenced branch
stroke, and sonance determined branch colour.

In the absence of a reference point for comparison, the ﬁrst note
caught by the L-system has a colour assigned based on its pitch (see
Figure 98). Subsequent notes were assigned colours based on their
classiﬁcation sonance. The more consonant the melody , the more sub-
tle the change of colour. On the contrary , a dissonant melody would
produce sharp changes in colour tonalities (see example in Figure 99).
Figure 99:
Example of
sonance
expression. Top
image - consonant
individual, bottom
image - dissonant.
Evolutionary algorithm: The control of the evolution in the environ-
ment was achieved through natural selection and sexual reproduction
processes. Then, an interactive genetic algorithm (IGA) was employed
to evaluate the quality of candidate solutions based on user prefer-
ences (Holland, 1992). The musical sequence caught by an individual
comprised the genotype. Its phenotype was composed of sound and
image, i.e., L-system. The order of the genotype was deﬁned by the
order in which notes were caught.
Selection: We relied on Darwin’s evolutionary process for the selec-
tion of individuals. This means that if an organism fails some ﬁtness
test, it dies (Darwin, 1859). In this case, the selection process consisted
of a ﬁtness function provided by the user, allowing real-time feedback
and guiding the evolution in preferred directions. This would trans-
late into a lower chance of reproducing and, eventually , death. The
expected output was a computer program that would evolve in ways
that resemble natural selection.
In detail, the ﬁtness function measured the quality of a chromo-
some. This process was done interactively in this work and provided
by a human observer. Using an IGA allowed the user’s visual and au-
ditory perceptions to impact the selection of individuals to represent
its preferred sequences of notes, therefore serving as the basis for the
offspring generation.
Reproduction: As for the reproduction methods, crossover and muta-
tion were used to maintain diversity in the population. While crossover
facilitated a global search in the solution space, mutation enabled a
local search. We note that the original chromosomes were kept to pre-
serve diversity and allow parameter space exploration:
• Mutation process: Mutation introduced random changes to indi-
viduals’ genetic material(Wiggins et al., 1998). It was applied
to the sequence of notes caught by an individual, allowing for
variations in pitch, duration, and volume ﬁg. 100.
Our mutation mechanism received two parameters: the sequence
of notes that would be modiﬁed and the probability of mutation
of each note present in the genotype. The probability of muta-
tion would then decide which note(s) collected by that individ-
ual were to be modiﬁed. Each element in the sequence of notes
caught by the individual had an equal chance of being chosen
(uniform probability).
• Crossover process : Crossover allowed the exchange of informa-
tion between two or more chromosomes in the population (Wig-
gins et al., 1998). This mixing allows creatures to evolve much
more rapidly than they would if each offspring contained a copy
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

crossover example. The 2 parents on the top are crossed and give
birth to 2 different children on the bottom.
of the genes of a single parent, modiﬁed occasionally by muta-
tion (Holland, 1992).
In this case, we selected only two parents who would give birth
to two children. We started by selecting random cut points on
each parent, and then we gave birth to the children based on
these cut points. The resulting size of each child was variable
since the parents’ cut points were random.
Even though we worked with simple musical inputs, a great vari-
ety/diversity of images and melodies (audiovisual experience) were
produced as illustrated in Figure 101. A demonstration video of the
resulting artefact can be consulted in the Appendix, Section .2.

8.1.3.3 Discussion
In this approach, we developed an experiment to evolve L-systems in
an artiﬁcial environment supported by IEA. The user could impact
the individual’s chance of survival and reproduction according to its
appreciation. Each L-system resulted in a sequence of notes and had
a visual representation generated in real-time. To increase the vari-
ability of outputs, we implemented a generative solution and used
some operators from genetic algorithms.
Overall, achieving a balance between musical and visual elements
through L-systems proved to be a difﬁcult task, as there was limited
control over the structure. While the user’s input and preferences
played a crucial role in shaping the outcomes, it is worth considering
if alternative methods or automated processes could lead to similar
results. To this end, in future work, we should clarify how the users’
actions and inputs impact the system’s evolution. Driven by the need
for greater ﬂexibility in exploring cross-modal spaces, we next pro-
pose a GP approach to study this challenge.
8.2 exploration n º 2: genetic programming
GP offers several advantages in exploring large solution spaces as it
is the case of symbolic regression (Koza, 1993; Langdon et al., 2008),
automated art generation (Miranda and Al Biles, 2007), or interactive
EC systems (Johanson and Poli, 1998; Poli, Cagnoni, et al., 1997). In
this thesis, we investigate the potential of GP to evolve a system of A V
relationships. Our initial implementation of GP departed from "Tiny
GP", a Java approach proposed by Poli et al. (Poli, Langdon, and
McPhee, 2008), a well-documented framework carefully detailing GP
techniques. Abstractly , GP executes the following:
1. Generation of an initial population composed of random pro-
grams;
2. Fitness evaluation through the calculation of the output(y) of
each program( f(x) ) in the population based on a given input(x);
3. Tournament selection to pick most ﬁt programs;
4. Creation of new programs through genetic operations and re-
placement in the population;
5. Survivors selection;
6. Iterative execution, with the process returning to step 2 until the
best ﬁtness surpasses a predetermined threshold of acceptance;
7. Return of the program with the best ﬁtness.
In speciﬁc, the translation between music and image (the scope of
this thesis proof-of-concept) through GP can be broken down into the
following steps (see Figure 102):
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

132 the audio -visual interplay
Figure 102: Architecture overview for an evolutionary process founded on
genetic programming.
1. Generate a vector of music values to each music property (mP’values),
based on a perceived emotion (Em);
2. Based on music properties’ values vector ( ! mP’values), gener-
ate a music (M) with a given emotion expression (Em);
3. Feed the evolutionary engine (EE) with an input of M ( ! mP’values);
4. Evolution: individuals (or programs) create relationships between
the input parameters mP’values and an image of vP’values as
target vector (! vP’values), representing the desired visual prop-
erties that should be expressed in the output image to corre-
spond with the input music properties;
5. Each individual (or program) of the population generates a vec-
tor of values as an output;
6. Each individual (or program) generates an image (I) correspond-
ing to the output image vector ( ! vP’values);
7. A ﬁtness value is calculated (via automatic or user-guided path)
and attributed to each individual;
8. A new population of individuals is generated through crossover
and mutation techniques;
9. Repeat from step number 3. for a number N generations until
success criteria is reached.
10. Curation: The image I or images I’ that best ﬁt the input music
M are saved.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

8.2e x p l o r a t i o n nº 2: genetic programming 133
To identify the optimal conﬁguration for this multidimensional
problem, we conducted performance tests, testing the algorithm’s
ability to evolve A V relationships under several system instances with
different conﬁgurations. In these instances, we have expanded the
Tiny GP implementation to respond appropriately to our problem’s
needs, as described in the "improvement" paragraphs under each it-
eration subsection.
The success of each algorithmic approach relied on its ability to
efﬁciently translate between domains, particularly in cases where the
translation involved multiple inputs (M) mapping to multiple outputs
(N). This required considering different variables or properties in the
translation from music to image or vice versa. The goal was to gener-
ate results that could adapt to various testing conditions, speciﬁcally
different emotion expressions.
We began by setting the GP preparatory steps:
• Set of terminals: set of m’ music or i’ image variables as those
deﬁned in sub-section 7.1.1, and Z random constants;
• Set of primitive functions: arithmetic functions (+, -, *, /);
• Fitness measure: distance between the program output and the
targeted variables 7 ;
• Parameters for controlling the run: population size, crossover
probability , mutation probability , and program depth;
• Termination criteria and method for designating the result of
the run (for example, a run of 5000 generations).
This model underwent reﬁnement through multiple iterations, con-
tinuously striving to improve the quality and adaptability of the evolved
A V relationships. Next, we describe each algorithmic iteration and its
results.

As part of the performance test, we assessed the algorithm’s capabil-
ity to handle different data dimensions and studied how population
size impacted the performance. For such, we experimented with two
and three ﬁtness cases 8 within 1:1 A V mappings. Additionally , we re-
lied on a small population of 20 individuals and a larger population
of 50 individuals. All tests were conducted over 250 generations. The
experiment was conﬁgured as detailed in Table 18.

Another challenge we realised in this iteration concerns the
bloat limits. Individuals’ tree depth exceeded the desired limit
(e.g., > 1000), causing signiﬁcant computational overhead and
reducing the overall efﬁciency of the GP algorithm.
Although this approach was useful in isolating and quickly test-
ing the solution’s capacity to solve 1:1 A V pairs, it was a very
simple problem for the algorithm to solve. Moreover, it did not
consider the future need for a correlation between several prop-
erties. To help control the problem complexity ( 1:n, m:n) and
manage computational resources, some improvements over the
original Tiny GP algorithm were performed.
Improvements Bloat: To handle cases where the tree exceeds a spec-
iﬁed maximum limit. To avoid this condition, if an individual’s
size exceeds the desired limit (e.g., > 1000), it enters a regener-
ation cycle. Another attempt is made to generate new individ-
uals and replace those exceeding the size limit. This process is
repeated 10 times or until 2 valid individuals with a tree size
below 1000 are obtained.
m:n relationship : We extended the initially proposed correlation
of 1:n variables vector to an m:n relationship so we could char-
acterise music or image expression through a more comprehen-
sive set of variables. This was a critical and necessary change,
as it solved the problem of correlating multiple dimensions be-
tween the input and the target. Essentially , we created a 2D
matrix: one axis for the number of desired inputs and one axis
for the number of desired output parameters.

the population size, bloat control, and random constants in the
evolutionary process on A V mappings of different complexities.
Population size: Figure 104 suggests an increased performance as
the population size improved the ﬁtness for all mappings.
Overall, a larger population size can provide more diverse ini-
tial solutions. However, it is important to note that increasing
the population size will also increase the computational cost of

Bloat control : The following results suggest that the impact of
bloat control can depend on the complexity of the problem. For
simpler problems, bloat control might limit the exploration of
the solution space and prevent the algorithm from ﬁnding better
solutions. For more complex problems, bloat control can help
to keep the computational cost manageable, but its impact on
ﬁtness can vary . For most mappings (v 1,v 2,v 5,v 6, and v 7),
turning on bloat control seems to improve the ﬁtness (i.e., the
negative values decrease). This suggests that bloat control ef-
fectively limits unnecessary growth of the programs and helps
improve the quality of the solutions.

Improvements We have performed improvements on this algorithm
version to ensure the best individual will always be kept in the
next generation and to avoid premature convergence.
Elitism: A negative tournament selection was employed to elim-
inate the worst individual from the population. By favouring an
elitist solution, the best individual in each generation was pre-
served and carried over to the next generation without being
subjected to the selection process.
Mutation and Crossover : To avoid premature convergence and a
loss of diversity , we worked with a smaller population size and
replaced the point mutation (or node replacement mutation)
operation with a subtree mutation. While in a point mutation,
a tree node is randomly selected and then randomly changed
(keeping the size of the tree intact), in a subtree mutation opera-
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

8.2e x p l o r a t i o n nº 2: genetic programming 139
tion, a randomly selected subtree of an individual’s program is
replaced with another random one-generated subtree.
