8.3 summary
In this chapter, we investigated EC methods for an interactive explo-
ration of auditory and visual elements, steered by perception. Dur-
ing this process, we addressed challenges such as the computational
representation of multidimensional data and the dynamics of nature-
inspired computation. Additionally , we explored the transformation
of music into image elements, seeking visual metaphors that could
express speciﬁc music elements effectively .
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

146 the audio -visual interplay
Following a simpliﬁed approach in the auditory and visual expres-
sions of artiﬁcial life systems, we tested the system’s scalability and
adaptability under simpliﬁed conditions, setting a clear baseline for
future studies and allowing an incremental introduction of more ele-
ments (e.g. additional visual or auditory properties).
The ﬁrst set of experiments suggested interesting results when com-
bining artiﬁcial life systems with genetic algorithms, particularly in
the L-Systems experiment. However, these approaches seemed more
effective in mappings from music to image (M ! I), presenting some
challenges when establishing a common language from image to mu-
sic (I ! M). By allowing external inﬂuences, we aimed to shape the
composition process and create an engaging creative environment.
To address the challenges of IGAs within artiﬁcial life environ-
ments, we proposed an exploration through GP, which has proven
successful. Consequently , we adopted genetic programming as the
deﬁnitive technique for developing adaptive audio-visual (A V) lan-
guages, which we test with users in the next Chapters 9 and 10.

9AUTOMATIC EVOLUTION
In this chapter, we assess the system’s ability to align evolved A V
relationships with users’ emotional perceptions building upon the
ﬁndings presented in Chapter 7 and Section 8.2.
The automatic mode allowed us to systematically test the algo-
rithm’s capacity to generate and reﬁne A V relationships without man-
ual intervention in the evolutionary process, focusing instead on the
system’s accuracy in building relevant A V relationships. It comprises
a series of computer programs that are evolved to approximate a tar-
get output function, denoted as f(x), given a speciﬁc input x. The
outputs of this artefact were later evaluated through user tests 1.
9.1 architecture overview
Generally , the evolutionary process begins with a population of ran-
dom computer programs represented as tree structures. Each pro-
gram is a candidate solution to the deﬁned problem. The objective
was to evolve programs that could effectively address the target prob-
lem, which, in our case, corresponded to baseline ﬁndings (as pre-
sented in Chapters 4, 7).
The performance of each program in solving the target problem
is assessed through a ﬁtness function, reﬂecting its ability to approx-
imate the target. The process of evaluating, selecting, and applying
genetic operators was iterated over multiple generations, allowing
the population to evolve over time. In addition, termination criteria
were monitored to determine when the algorithm should stop. Once
the termination criteria were met, the individual or program with
the best ﬁtness was identiﬁed, and its corresponding program was
selected as the result of that particular run.
This evolutionary process, can be summarised into the following
steps:
1. Calculation of the ﬁtness of each program;
2. Manipulation of programs based on a predeﬁned set of prob-
abilities, which in turn can fall into one or more of the three
operations:
• Selection of two programs to perform a sub-tree crossover;
• Selection of one program to perform a sub-tree mutation;
• Reproduction of selected programs, making sure that the
best individual of the population passes to the next gener-
ation;
1 The present chapter contributes to research question nº 4: Can evolutionary processes
be used to explore A V relationships that ﬁt speciﬁc criteria?
147
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

148 automatic evolution
3. The individuals with the lowest ﬁtness are selected for replace-
ment through a negative tournament operation.
As we next exemplify , an individual program in the initial pop-
ulation evolved to map high-pitch, fast-tempo music vector into a
visual target vector with a blue hue, small size, irregular contours
and round shape values. In essence, the A V language undergoes a
transformation, evolving relationships between a set of inputs (music
characteristics) and a set of targets (visual characteristics), producing
candidate images (I’) as an output. We illustrate this A V evolution
process with a population size of two individuals (or programmes)
for a simpliﬁed demonstration:
input A music vector is given as input, and a target vector image is
deﬁned. e.g.
input → Mi [pitch: high, tempo: fast]
target → It [hue: blue, size: small, contour: irregular, shape: round]
initial state : Initially , the algorithm begins with a random popu-
lation of images. e.g.:
output → I1 [hue: red, size: big, contour: regular, shape: sharp]
output → I2 [hue: yellow, size: medium, contour: regular, shape:
sharp]
during the evolution : During evolution, the genetic program-
ming algorithm continuously reﬁnes the mappings within the
population. e.g.:
output → I1 [hue: red, size: big, contour: irregular, shape: sharp]
output → I2 [hue: blue, size: medium, contour: regular, shape:
round]
results : As the algorithm advances through the generations, the
mappings can undergo additional improvements. After several
iterations (i.e. generations) of evaluation, selection, and genetic
operations (such as crossover and mutation), the program’s map-
pings evolve towards the optimal solution. e.g.:
output → I1 [hue: green, size: medium, contour: irregular, shape:
round]
output → I2 [hue: blue, size: medium, contour: irregular, shape:
round]
curation : The image with the ﬁtness closest to zero is selected as
the optimal output. In this example, it would be I 2.
output → I2 [hue: blue, size: medium, contour: irregular, shape:
round]

pression or outcomes, we conducted preliminary user tests 2.

9.2 experiment setup and user tests 149
Seventeen participants evaluated the system’s expressive capacity
for the translation between domains. The age of participants ranged
from 26 to 35 years old, with a median of 30 years old. Eleven subjects
had a background in media design; four subjects had a background
in computer science; one had a background in human resources, and
the other one in healthcare. Although the tests were performed online,
the same instructions were given to all the participants, and they had
remote control of the screen to perform the test.
9.2.1 Interface and Conﬁgurations
To perform the user tests, we conceived an interface (Figure 111) that
would allow participants to interact in an intuitive way , without inter-
fering with the UX.
Figure 111: (a) Automatic evolution process overview to generate images
from music (M ! I), and (b) interface layout presented in user
tests.
The interface presented a set of four ordered music and a set of im-
ages randomly displayed - a strategy to control the interdependence
on perceived emotion and bias - which we would ask users to pair.
The set of four pieces of music was generated by the MG and pro-
vided as input to the GP system, with each one aimed to be perceived
as a distinct emotion: angry , calm, happy , and sad.
The images presented to users resulted from the system evolution
(see Table 28 for conﬁgurations) given a set of musical inputs (see
Figure 111 A).

We started by allowing for a pre-exploration phase to remove initial
learning. As they moved to the categorisation of music-image pairs,
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

150 automatic evolution
participants could listen to every music as often as needed during
the test. This is, they would choose when to play it or not, but we
suggested that they begin the test by listening to them all to have
an overview. Although music was played in a generative way , we
ﬁxed some parameters to a neutral state. We adjusted the musical
parameters considered more PR in expressing emotions, such as pitch,
resonance, loudness, tempo, and progression. The images had a 2D
static representation, and all the participants had access to the same
set of evolved images.
Then, the participants would be asked to match the pairs of music
and images (see Figure 111 B), considering that they should represent
or express the same underlying emotion or sensation. These would be
paired once a participant reached the desired combination of music
and image. Afterwards, we would ask participants if they recognised
the underlying emotions and sensations behind the pairs they had
connected and asked them to describe each pair.

We divided the user test results into quantitative and qualitative phases
to analyse them. In the ﬁrst phase, we present the data resulting from
the users’ perception and compare it to the expected mappings of the
previous system evolution. In the second part, we analyse the results
from a qualitative perspective, including the users’ open answers.

Considering the values obtained, we can understand that the users
most easily perceive the outputs regarding happiness and anger. It
is also noteworthy to point out that 17.64 % of participants have
switched calm and sad pairs of mappings. These results can be ex-
plained by the energy proximity between the two emotions. Both can
be classiﬁed as low arousal in Russell’s model of emotions (Russell,
1980) or a need for a more expressive visual representation. Compar-
ing the users’ perceptions with the expected results, we conclude that
participants’ preferences agree with the system’s evolved outcomes.
However, some improvements can still be made to further understand
the underlying motivation behind their preferences.
9.3.2 Qualitative Analysis
The users’ open answers integrated the last part of the test. They sug-
gested that emotions with contrasting valence and arousal - such as
happiness and anger - were more easily recognised than the less con-
trasting ones - calm and sadness. These answers are also suggestive
of the following: (i) The mappings of happiness and anger were very
clear, being the last one the most clear; (ii) The low complexity of the
visual output for calm had a high positive impact on its perception;
(iii) Calm and sadness are harder to perceive; (iv) It was easier to ac-
knowledge the underlying emotion through the image than through
the music.
Moreover, during the tests, users expressed other feelings and words
of EP in the following music(M)-image(I) pairs:

The qualitative analysis clariﬁed the results obtained from the quan-

Overall, results suggest the system’s ability to handle mappings
with distinct levels of complexity ( 1:1; 1:n; m:n), suggesting the tool’s
plasticity and capability to explore this cross-modal language. This
opens possibilities for creative applications where audio and visual
elements can be combined and interrelated.
9.3.2.1 Discussion
From the theoretical analysis of the literature, we could conclude that
“sadness”, “happiness”, “fear”, and “anger” are the emotions most
commonly associated with visual and musical properties, followed
by “tenderness" and “calm". Furthermore, we can conclude that the
most PR VP to express emotions are colour (brightness, hue, and
saturation), shape, edge, size and texture and that the most PR mu-
sic properties to express emotions are tempo, loudness, articulation,
high-frequency energy and harmony sonance.
The performed user tests favoured the integration of emotion and
perception ﬁndings in the representation and abstraction of auditory
and visual languages, as the evolved expressions were compatible
with users’ perception of emotions. For instance, anger and happi-
ness were the participants’ most easily perceived music-image pairs,
while calm and sadness had more divergent perceptions. User studies
regarding music and image are experimentally challenging and deli-
cate topics, as they suffer from two core challenges. First, we would
need an independent measurement of what emotions are. It is known
that subjects disagree about PE; hence, we cannot measure ground
truths, only provide tendencies. Secondly , music and image complex-
ity offer various cross-effects and biasing.
On an end note, user tests offered a quick approach to test the
limits of this multidisciplinary problem and contribute to a better un-
derstanding of the transposition of human creative processes through
EAs.
9.4 summary
In this chapter, we explored the computational generation of A V as-
sociations through GP algorithms while assessing their potential to
guide evolution automatically . The experiments conﬁrmed the algo-
rithm’s effectiveness in generating A V relationships towards a theo-
retical target vector. By integrating theoretical studies with practical
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

9.4 summary 153
evaluations, we were able to evaluate in a comprehensive way the
algorithm’s effectiveness and reﬁne it to enhance its performance in
evolving A V relationships.
To further improve and test the system’s plasticity , we propose de-
veloping a co-creative artefact. This new approach shall allow users to
customise A V associations according to their perceptual preferences,
enhancing the interaction between humans and machines and deep-
ening our understanding of this dynamic relationship.

10PARTIALLY-GUIDED EVOLUTION
10.1 architecture overview
In a new instance of the system, we have built a GP algorithm to
evolve a customised relationship between music and images, partially
guided by user preferences 1. Given a particular music set, the GP
engine generates images to represent the perceived emotions of that
set. The system outputs were evaluated by combining users’ input
with theoretical mappings 2.
In speciﬁc, provided a user evaluation of the system outputs, the
system will calculate the ﬁtness of candidate solutions (images) as
a sum of two terms: (a) theoretical mappings and (b) user evalua-
tion. This approach offers distinct advantages: an enhanced candidate
search, as the system, rather than departing entirely from random
possibilities, leverages the established connections to generate map-
pings that are likely to be relevant; and personalisation of outcomes
based on user preferences – by incorporating their evaluations, the
system drives the evolution process to align with the user’s prefer-
ences.
10.2 optimisation and experiment setup
While we departed from the automatic evolutionary mode (presented
in Chapter 9), we had to refactor this algorithm instance to align with
the new demands and ensure a good performance in light of these
changes.
10.2.1 Fitness Function
The ﬁtness function comprises two terms: user ﬁtness and theoret-
ical ﬁtness. Typically , GP algorithms rely solely on a theoretical ﬁt-
ness function that evaluates the performance of individuals based on
predeﬁned criteria. Combining it with a user ﬁtness layer added a
dynamic element to the evaluation process. By introducing a user ﬁt-
ness layer, we enabled human evaluation and feedback to integrate
the evolution of relationships between auditory and visual domains.
For the calculations, the theoretical ﬁtness was computed as de-
scribed in Chapter 9, and the user ﬁtness was calculated using a set of
variables: user ranking of each programme, step ﬁtness, range ﬁtness,
and cool down. To calculate the user’s ﬁtness, the program begins by
1 We preserved the same underlying set of four emotions (anger, calm, happy , and
sad) to drive the expressions of music or image.
2 The present chapter contributes to research question nº 5: Is it possible to give users
the capacity to intervene in the evolutionary process and approximate the artefact
outcomes to their preferences?
155

156 partially -guided evolution
mapping the ﬁtness value to the current best and worst ﬁtness values
and updating them if necessary . This calculates range ﬁtness, deﬁnes
percentiles, and creates steps. The range ﬁtness is calculated as the ab-
solute difference between the worst and best ﬁtness values, while the
step ﬁtness is calculated as the range ﬁtness divided by the number
of steps in the user ﬁtness.
range ﬁtness = |worst_ﬁtness - best_ﬁtness| ,
step ﬁtness = range_ﬁtness
number of steps in user_ﬁtness
Given that the evolutionary process operated through discrete gen-
erations rather than continuous progression, we implemented a stepped
function approach. This discrete methodology was justiﬁed by the
generational nature of the evolutionary algorithm, where ﬁtness bonuses
were adjusted through distinct increments rather than continuous gra-
dients. In speciﬁc, the number of steps corresponded to the levels at
which the user ﬁtness function privileged the selected individuals.
The determination of the ideal number of steps was driven by em-
pirical experimentation with the aim of balancing convergence and
population diversity .
The program then checks if the user has provided an evaluation for
the current candidate solution. If the user has not evaluated the solu-
tion, its ranking is set to 0, and no further calculations are performed.
When the user provides an evaluation, the program checks if the user
ranking for the candidate solution is positive or negative. For posi-
tive rankings, the user ﬁtness is calculated as the best ﬁtness value of
baseline ﬁndings, plus the user ranking multiplied by the step ﬁtness
value. Conversely , for negative rankings, the user ﬁtness is calculated
as the worst ﬁtness value of baseline, plus the user ranking multiplied
by the step ﬁtness value.
user ﬁtness =

best_ﬁtness + (user_ranking ⇥ step_ﬁtness)
worst_ﬁtness + (user_ranking ⇥ step_ﬁtness)
The programme updates the best and worst ﬁtness values based on
the population’s mapped ﬁtness values. In addition, the ﬁtness func-
tion considers the presence of a cool-down period. If the cool-down
counter is greater than 0, the user ﬁtness is adjusted by multiplying
it with the ratio of the cool-down counter to the cool-down period.
On the other hand, if the cool-down counter is not greater than 0, the
user ﬁtness is set to 0.

Finally , the program updates the ﬁtness based on the user ﬁtness,
theoretical ﬁtness and cool-down adjustment. It is calculated by com-
bining theoretical and user ﬁtness with a weighted average.

In sum, this enabled human users to contribute with their percep-
tion to evaluate creative outputs, ensuring that the evolution process
would align with the user’s preferences and facilitate a personalised
outcome.
10.2.2 User Interaction
In this instance of the algorithm, we also refactored the way users
would interact with the system. As the system’s demands evolved,
optimising the algorithm to handle larger populations and improve
overall performance became essential.
Previously , the algorithm ranked the population based on their the-
oretical ﬁtness. To address the interaction issue, we implemented a
user-guided selection process by displaying a subset of the complete
population. This decision signiﬁcantly reduced the cognitive load for
users and facilitated their interactions with the system.
As interactive evolutionary algorithms’ can cause user’s fatigue —
a well-known problem in interactive evolutionary computation where
the user must evaluate or classify many potential solutions (Takagi,
2001) — we selected a sample of the population to be displayed in
the interface, given the following strategy:
1. Rank the population based on their ﬁtness and storing it in a
list.
2. Select every other program to iterate over the ranked population
at regular intervals (Bäck, 1996; Lohr, 2021) e.g.
• The ﬁrst program in the ranked population is selected.
• The second program is not selected.
• The third program is selected.
...
• This pattern continues for the rest of the ranked popula-
tion.
3. Store the selected programs into a list of double arrays. Each
double array contains the program index and its ﬁtness value.
4. Pass the selected programs to the User Interface (UI) for display .
5. Introduce randomness and diversity to the selection process
by replacing the last two elements with two random programs
from the population.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

158 partially -guided evolution
By adopting a systematic approach (selecting every other program),
we ensured that the selection was spread across the range of ﬁtness
values in the population, potentially capturing both high and average
performers. Additionally , random programs were introduced to the
interface by replacing the last two elements of the selected programs
list with two random programs from the population, helping to main-
ing genetic diversity . Overall, by reducing the amount of information
being displayed to participants, we were able to:
• Improve User Focus: By displaying a subset of the population,
the UI allows the user to focus on a manageable number of
options at a time. This prevents information overload and helps
users make more thoughtful decisions.
• Enhance Decision-making Process: When presented with a large
number of options, users often experience decision paralysis,
leading to indecisiveness and frustration. The UI promotes a
more efﬁcient and effective decision-making process by narrow-
ing down the options to a subset.
• Simplify Interface Design: Displaying the complete population
in the UI would require a signiﬁcant amount of screen space
and potentially complex navigation mechanisms. By showing a
subset, the interface design can be simpliﬁed, making it easier
for users to understand and navigate.
• Improve Performance: Rendering and displaying a large num-
ber of images or graphical elements can put a strain on sys-
tem resources, potentially leading to slower performance. By
limiting the number of elements in the interface, system perfor-
mance can be improved, ensuring a smoother UX.
10.2.3 User Favorites
Figure 113: Galleries of favourites from some participants who performed

The introduction of user favourites added a new dimension to the
algorithm. When a user selects a favourite program, it is saved and
stored in the user’s favourite array . To accommodate this feature, we
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

10.3 user tests 159
implemented a method to replace an existing program in the popula-
tion with the user’s favourite program. Figure 113 illustrates some of
the participants’ gallery of favourites.
The impact of introducing a favourite program includes increased
genetic diversity , potential ﬁtness improvement, and inﬂuence on pro-
gram selection for future evolution. The favourite program is treated
like any other program in the population, this is, if the user decides
to reintroduce it into the population it goes through the same evolu-
tionary processes as the remaining candidates. The advantage lies in
the user’s preference for the program (i.e. positive ﬁtness), indicating
desirable characteristics or behaviour.
10.3 user tests
To study the impact and possibilities of the system and analyse the
subjects’ performance, user tests were conducted with a total of 20
individuals from different backgrounds (computer science and media
design). The participants’ ages ranged from 22 to 36 years old, with
a median age of 30.5 years old. At the beginning of each session,
participants were provided with a document to sign, stating their
agreement with using their test data for analysis purposes.
As illustrated in Figure 114, we created a custom interface to run
these tests. On the left bar, the selected music is highlighted (e.g. mu-
sic 3 in this case); next to it, there is another bar with a gallery of
the user’s favourites. The principal screen presents the population of
individuals of a given generation to be classiﬁed.

10.3 user tests 161
10.3.2 User Votes
At each generation, the user was shown four screens, one at a time.
Each screen was associated with one piece of music, and it could
switch between screens. Each screen displayed 20 images (therefore,
20 images x 4 pieces of music = 80). The presented images were a
selection of the ﬁttest and a few random ones to avoid premature
optimisation. Therefore, the users did not vote on all solution spaces.
The rating scale used in the evaluation was binary , allowing par-
ticipants to indicate whether they felt the visual expression corre-
sponded ( 1) or did not correspond (- 1) to the audio input. This bi-
nary scale was chosen to simplify the decision-making process and
minimise the need for extensive rationalisation. Users needed to vote
on each presented expression, otherwise the system would not let
them advance to the next generation of evolved relationships. This
user feedback deﬁned part of the ﬁtness evaluation used in the ge-
netic evolution process.
In addition, the user could indicate if a particular image matched
the music "well". Users could select a particular image as their favourite
to speciﬁc music. There could be only one favourite for each music
at a given moment. This favourite individual could be reintroduced
into the population later on. Favourites were only reintroduced into
the population when the user elected to do so. In the last presented
generation (7th), we were asked to replace the favourites with a more
suitable expression if that was the case.
The user voting process was designed to optimize both cognitive
load management and vote quality . For the ﬁrst part, we implemented
a binary voting system (+ 1/-1) that simpliﬁed decision-making and
reduced the need for extensive user rationalization, limiting the dis-
play to 20 images per screen and allowing screen switching to give
users control over their evaluation pace. To ensure vote quality , the
system required a user evaluation of all presented expressions before
advancement and provided the ability to reintroduce user favourites
into the population.
The user decision-making process was recorded so that we could
analyse it from the perspective of Explainable AI. This is, by capturing
the user choices through the evolutionary process, we were able to
create a transparent framework that provided insights into both the
algorithmic decisions and user interactions, contributing to:
• A clear understanding of the inﬂuence of both theoretical knowl-
edge and user preferences;
• A traceable evolution of solutions based on user interaction;
• A measurable impact of user interventions on the system out-
puts.

10.3.3 Scope Note
While the system efﬁciently generates correspondences between Mu-
sic and Image (M ! I, I ! M), as proven in Chapter 9, conducting
Image-to-Music user tests would require a signiﬁcant time investment
(several months). One of the reasons for this is that listening to mul-
tiple music combinations (on the I ! M axis) could quickly induce
fatigue and create complex testing conditions, requiring a careful and
extensive assemblage of tests. Therefore, we will address the I $ M
testing as part of future work.
10.4 results and discussion
In this section, we present the results of the user tests performed.
These tests aimed to investigate the users’ ability to customise A V
relationships according to their preferences and, as such, assess per-
ceived expressions as well as interaction with the system.
To provide a comprehensive analysis, we combined quantitative
and qualitative methods. The quantitative analysis aims to highlight
user tendencies and assess the signiﬁcance of the various factors that
may inﬂuence A V relationships; the qualitative analysis aims to ex-
plore insights into user behaviour, motivations, and decision-making
processes when interacting with the system.
10.4.1 Quantitative Analysis
By analysing a dataset which captured user rankings through the evo-
lutionary process, as detailed in Chapter 7, we aimed to measure their
tendencies of choices and assess its impact on the perception of A V
relationships. To structure our analysis, we formulated the following
hypothesis:
hypothesis 1 Investigate the relationships between image and mu-
sic properties and determine the relative importance of its rela-
tionship based on an underlying emotion. This is, how do dif-
ferent VP impact the representation of AP? What is the impact
of PE in shaping the A V relationship?
hypothesis 2 Assess the distance between user-selected A V prop-
erties and baseline A V associations. How far or close were the
user’s preferences from the state-of-the-art study in Chapter 3?
To uncover tendencies in the multiple dimensions of A V stimuli
and gain a comprehensive understanding of the impact of perceived
expressions, we relied on a Scatter Plot Matrix (SPLOM) to represent
the users’ data. This analysis resulted in the following ﬁndings:
Finding 1: Properties relevance — Observing the diagonal of each plot
in Figure 115, we can analyse the distribution of each visual

Finding 3: Emotional valence and arousal — It is interesting to observe
that negative emotions such as anger and sadness often entail a
greater complexity , with a need to rely on a broader spectrum of

in the users’ tendencies to select a broader range of properties
(represented by p 1,p 2,p 3, etc.) to distinguish between these
emotions.
Emotions with lower arousal levels, such as calm and sadness,
also exhibit a distinct pattern. Users demonstrated a more sig-
niﬁcant contrast between the different values of a given prop-
erty . This phenomenon may be justiﬁed by a decreased visual
intensity/simplicity in representing these expressions to the hu-
man eye, demanding a more curated experience to express them
accurately . In essence, lower arousal emotions seem to prompt
individuals to focus on distinguishing which particular proper-
ties contribute to the perception of these emotions.

more relevance in the computational expression driven by a
given emotion. From this analysis, we can conclude that while
certain visual concepts — such as regularity , shape edge, colour
and complexity — are easily recognised and agreed upon among
users, others — such as size, crowdedness, and orientation —
seem to strongly depend on the individual’s perception of A V
relationships or other contexts such as past experiences.
As a general observation, anger and happiness often preferred
irregular patterns, sharp edges, and larger sizes, which can be
justiﬁed to convey the movement, intensity , and dynamics of
these emotions. In contrast, calm and sadness, which are more

10.4.2 Users’ Favourites x State-of-the-Art Studies
We computed two metrics to examine the alignment between the
state-of-the-art study and user responses: euclidian distance and ab-
solute error. Moreover, the baseline and users’ response vectors were
normalised to ensure comparability in the Euclidian distance calcula-
tions.
The results of these calculations are summarised in the Table 33, dis-
playing the average values across the four different emotional states
(E1,E 2,E 3,E 4).
Analysing Table 33, we can conclude an overall varying degree
of alignment between users’ preferences and state-of-the-art studies,
where E 1 (Angry) has the closest distance to baseline, and E 4 (Sad)
has the largest divergence. The variations were as expected as the
system allowed participants to customise the A V relationship, and in
turn, this had in its expression.
E1 (Angry) and E 2 (Calm) displayed the closest distance of users’
preferences to the baseline when we look at the Euclidian and ab-
solute error distances, suggesting better model effectiveness at pre-
dicting users’ perception of A V relationships. When analysing the
standard deviation values, we can conclude that these two emotions
had the highest variability regarding the users’ response consistency;
however, these nuances in responses were limited to 2 values of dif-
ference in VP’ on a total of 9 VP.
While E3 (Happy) and E4 (Sad) were the least related to baseline, in-
terestingly , they had the biggest consistency among participants. This
suggests that we might need to rely on a broader set of parameters to
match the underlying users’ perception of these emotions.
On a more detailed analysis of the impact of each visual property in
the curated representation of different emotions, regularity(p 1), fore-
ground shape edge(p 2), size(p 4) and colour(p 5) have a relevant role
in the expression and perception of A V relationships as they show
a higher variation of values across all the emotions. Shape edge and
colour are shared with the patterns found in the previous sub-section
and with users’ qualitative responses when asked about the percep-
tual impact of VP. In contrast, P 8 (V . distribution) could be inter-
preted as less impactful in expressing A V relationships, as it had a
consistent std value with no variation between different emotions.
In general, the quantitative ﬁndings not only validated the pro-
posed framework but also suggested that the implemented computa-
tional solution was effective in driving audiovisual languages in a PR
way for humans. Furthermore, these results supported the hypothe-
sis that speciﬁc audiovisual relationships can enhance the perception
of different emotion stimuli.

10.4.3 Qualitative Analysis
Understanding the impact of the system on human perception can
guide future improvements and help to better align the system’s
output with User Perception (UP) and preferences. We have relied
on a qualitative study to complement the quantitative analyses, en-
abling further insights into user behaviour, motivations, and decision-
making processes. At the end of each session with a user driving the
system, we performed an interview of 10 questions. In this interview,
we analysed the circumstances in which users took advantage of the
system or where they felt limitations. We also inquired about their
satisfaction with the results and evolutionary process. The questions
posed to the participants were as follows:
q1 . Could the system produce outputs with distinct affective expres-
sions (yes/no)? Where " 1" is yes and " 0" is no.
Table 35: Question 1 - Summary Statistics
N Average Median Mode Standard Deviation
20 1 1 1 0
The unanimous response of all participants (N = 20) who an-
swered yes to this question provides evidence that they per-
ceived the system to be able to produce outputs with distinct
affective expressions.
q2 . Can you identify up to 3 visual and 3 AP that impacted your
perception/decision-making process?
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

Each participant was given a list of properties and asked to se-
lect up to three properties they considered the most relevant in
their decision-making process. The list of properties was elabo-
rated as follows:
• music properties: pitch (high, low), sonance (consonant,
dissonant), loudness (loud, soft), tempo (slow, fast), pro-
gression (major, minor).
• VP: shape regularity (regular, irregular), shape edge (sharp,
round), size (small, big), colour (yellow, grey , . . . ), crowd-
edness (crowded, clean), complexity (complex, simple), vi-
sual distribution (organised, chaos), orientation (horizon-
tal, vertical, diagonal).
Figure 116 presents the ﬁndings regarding the properties in the
auditory and visual domains that most impacted participants’
perception/decision-making processes. This analysis reveals the
AP of tempo, sonance, and progression as the most inﬂuential.
As for the VP, colour, crowdedness, and shape edge (round,
sharp) did stand out. It is noteworthy that participants with
a media design background considered shape edge the most
relevant visual property . Their visual training and expertise may
justify this.

q8 . Where did you feel freedom, and where did you feel limitations
in the system?
Participants expressed a sense of freedom in the process due to
the intuitive and easy interaction. They appreciated the ability
to save favourites and felt empowered by the choices they had
in the system. They also liked the freedom to select multiple
images and the ability to recombine characteristics to inﬂuence
the outcome. Overall, these results suggest that the system’s
interface was user-friendly and did not cause signiﬁcant fatigue
among users, hence not interfering in the experience negatively
and potentially encouraging longer usage times.
On the other hand, participants felt limited by the system’s ten-
dency to move away from their favourites. They desired multi-
ple favourites or a middle vote between like and dislike. They
also wanted more diversity in the presented images and more
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

172 partially -guided evolution
voting levels to express their preferences with greater granular-
ity . They felt that the number of interventions in the evolution-
ary process was limited and that image classiﬁcation was done
on a discrete scale instead of a continuous one.
Overall, while users appreciated the freedom provided by the
system in terms of interaction, selection, and saving favourites,
they also identiﬁed areas of improvement. These included the
system’s handling of favourite selections, greater diversity in
images, and the desire for more granular voting levels.
q9 . What is your impression of this tool, e.g., can you recall a do-
main or problem to which it could be applied?
Participants have identiﬁed applications to various domains and
problems. Some examples are the creation of soundtracks for
movie scenes, generating visual backgrounds for music con-
certs, and enhancing the A V experiences in public spaces.
In the realm of visual communication or graphic design, its use
was suggested for the generation of graphic design posters that
aimed to evoke emotions or generate ideas for design artefacts
that evoked speciﬁc emotions, generation of print objects or
artworks relating visual and auditory expressions such as the
generation of music for animated illustrations targeted at chil-
dren or the creation of music artworks and covers for platforms
like Spotify . Moreover, the automatic generation of art was sug-
gested through evolutionary processes, where visual and music
elements evolve and change dynamically .
Additionally , its use in psychology and CS was suggested to
analyse sentiment and its correlation with music, helping the
creation of personalised experiences, the study of cultural pref-
erences and psychological responses to music through visual
representation, and providing a visual representation/expres-
sion of music to aid the perception of people with hearing im-
pairments or vice-versa.

– The system helped deﬁne an image to represent a piece
of music, even when users had no mental image of it.

10.4.3.1 Field Observations
1. User Intuition and Impact of Generativity: Some users, driven
by their interest in understanding the differences between var-
ious stimuli, intuitively analysed the individual image compo-
nents. Also, the generativity aspects of music and image seemed
to have a smaller impact on their choices, such as whether they
ﬁlled out forms. However, the purpose of the system was to
present more than one type of expression, proving its capacity
for diversity , as previously mentioned in user tests X.
2. Impact of Music, Perception, and User Preference: Users re-
ported the ability to evolve the 4 expressions to their prefer-
ences, being only one characteristic away from "perfection" in
all expressions. A few users have also reported that the good
quality of the music presented in the test allowed them to con-
centrate on the task and feel engaged in the evaluation process.
Future work could consider adding more levels of expression.
3. Logic of Replacement and User Response Time: The ﬁrst iter-
ation of user tests took longer, while the following ones were
more ﬂuid and had more immediate responses. This indicates
that users’ response time improved as they became more fa-
miliar with the system. Participants also found it important to
understand whether the replacement of favourites followed a
speciﬁc logic. As a suggestion for future work, this replacement
could be done in an expression negatively rated by the user.
4. System Inﬂuence and User Selectivity: When participants seemed
more hesitant regarding a visual expression, the system helped
them bring a variety of expressions to their decision-making
process. This suggests that the system’s inﬂuence helped users
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

174 partially -guided evolution
in decision-making. Furthermore, we could notice that users be-
gan by having a wide set of choices. Still, after 3 or 4 interven-
tions, they would be more picky about image details, focusing
on 1 or 2 characteristics to tune.
5. Future Recommendations: Some users commented about rank-
ing all the similar visual expressions presented, as they knew
this could impact the evolutionary algorithm and the diversity
of individuals in future generations. To address this, the sys-
tem could present individuals with the most distinct individual
expressions in future iterations. Additionally , users with knowl-
edge and experience in evolutionary processes did not consis-
tently rank all similar individuals, aiming to avoid premature
convergence. Future work should study the classiﬁcation of in-
dividuals from a user perspective in further detail.
10.5 summary
Integrating user feedback in the evolutionary process allowed us to
explore a novel dimension within the ﬁeld of CC, enabling the gener-
ation of more diverse and perceptually relevant outputs. Both quanti-
tative and qualitative ﬁndings highlighted the system’s capability to
allow user customisation, which is crucial for accommodating indi-
vidual differences in perception and expression of A V Languages.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

11CONCLUSION
When studying audio-visual associations in a computational context,
several questions were raised: Is there a computational way to express
perceptually relevant relationships between music and image? Are
there shared characteristics? How can we explore these cross-modal
associations in an interactive and meaningful way?
Following these questions as a starting point to formulate our hy-
pothesis, we investigated the integration of cognitive science insights into
a cross-modal framework for the generation and expression of AV relation-
ships. In addition, we proposed a computational exploration through Evolu-
tionary Algorithms to allow for custom and adaptive AV relationships in
various emotional contexts. To test the hypothesis and address the re-
search questions (presented in Section 1.4), we developed a series of
experiments and systems.
Overall, the work was divided into three parts: (i) study and trans-
position of visual and auditory characteristics to be mediated by emo-
tions and perception; (ii) computational generation of image and mu-
sic in an abstract way; and (iii) development of a framework that inte-
grates theoretical knowledge and computational techniques to shape
the language; exploration of evolutionary processes and user interac-
tions for an adaptive A V language that meets speciﬁc preferences.
In Part i, we addressed research question nº 1: what sort of aesthetic
and affective-perceptual correspondences may exist between image and mu-
sic? Here, we explored historical and theoretical studies of A V cor-
respondences, focusing on characterising and analysing the relation-
ships between music, image, and emotion. We delineated two pri-
mary approaches to understanding A V correspondences: metaphori-
cal and cognitive. The metaphorical approach relies on intuitive and
culturally embedded associations, often resulting in expressive, artis-
tically driven works that evoke a broad spectrum of interpretations.
In contrast, the cognitive approach is rooted in evidence, seeking to
understand these relationships through the lens of neuroscience and
psychology , focusing on how the human brain processes and per-
ceives these sensory interactions.
Research suggests that a combined perspective would enhance the
aesthetic and emotional depth of A V works, ensuring they remain per-
ceptually relevant in different cultural and individual contexts. As we
explored the elements and relationships between audio, visual, and
emotional stimuli, we created a structured dataset that enhanced our
understanding of cross-modal associations and supported the devel-
opment of Creativity Support Tools. This dataset stood out from the
existing state-of-the-art in two ways. First, to the best of our knowl-
edge, little research has been performed to propose a structured con-
nection between these three areas (music, image, and emotions). Sec-
177
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

178 conclusion
ondly , we extended the depth of evidence-based research compared
to other studies by including a broader set of elements.
The deﬁnition of the A V language comprised a vocabulary and a
grammar. The vocabulary consisted of three main alphabets: visual
abstract symbols, basic general emotions, and musical features. The
grammar was responsible for representing associations among fea-
tures of our vocabulary sets. This approach introduced new possibil-
ities in the expression of audiovisual mappings at a perceptual level.
This work resulted in the following publication:
• Rodrigues, A., Oliveira, J., Lopes, D., Cardoso, A. & Machado, P .
(2023) Creative Emotions https://creativeemotions.dei.uc.pt
In Part ii, we addressed research question nº 2: how can we map im-
age and music computationally? and research question nº 3: How can we
systematise theoretical knowledge for the computational production of AV re-
lationships? To answer these research questions, we ﬁrst performed a
comprehensive overview of the intersection of computer science with
music and visual arts, emphasising the evolution and impact of com-
putational tools in these creative domains. From this, we concluded
that rule-based programming and nature-inspired computing were
the most promising approaches to explore this cross-modal problem.
Next, we conducted a series of computational experiments to test
and further understand the expressive capabilities of computational
systems in generating music and images guided by emotions. Spe-
ciﬁc music properties included pitch, loudness, timbre, progression,
and tempo. As for image generation, we began with a simpler system
focused on low-level features such as line dynamics, shape contours,
and colours. However, we soon iterated to achieve a broader set of ex-
pressive capabilities, including visual properties such as shapes, size,
colour, crowdedness, complexity , visual distribution and orientation.
Preliminary user tests showed that participants could recognise the
intended emotions from the music and image generations, conﬁrm-
ing the effectiveness of these computational approaches.
Both the theoretical and practical experiments developed at this
stage played an essential role in the future iterations of this work. To
clarify the complexity , we divided the problem into several compo-
nents to better understand which elements should be part of the so-
lution. Based on these objectives, we conceptualised a computational
framework to support the design language. The overall system was
based on three key components: an Image Generator, a Music Gen-
erator, and an Evolutionary Engine. While the ﬁrst and second mod-
ules were used to dynamically generate ﬂexible visual and musical
languages, the third module facilitated the translation and evolution
of expressions between A V associations, guided by a context of per-
ceived emotions. This work resulted in the following publications:
• Seiça, M., Rodrigues, A., Cardoso, F. A., Martins, P ., & Machado,
P. (2019) Computer generation and perception evaluation of music-
emotion associations. 14th International Symposium on Computer
Music Multidisciplinary Research (CMMR) (p. 789).
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

conclusion 179

ence on Digital and Interactive Arts (pp. 1-9).
• Rodrigues, A., Cardoso, A., & Machado, P . ( 2018). Generation of

• Rodrigues, A., Cardoso, A., & Machado, P . ( 2019). A Dynamic

• Rodrigues, A., Sousa, B., Machado, P ., & Cardoso, A. ( 2020).

• Sousa, B., Rodrigues, A., Coelho, N., & Machado, P . ( 2022) Made

• Maçãs, C., Rodrigues, A., Bernardes, G., & Machado, P . ( 2018).

In Part iii, we explored research question nº 4, can evolutionary pro-
cesses be used to explore AV relationships that ﬁt speciﬁc criteria? , and
research question nº 5, is it possible to give users the capacity to intervene
in the process and approximate the artefact outcomes to their preferences?
We utilised the digital computer’s capacity to transform and manip-
ulate multidimensional data to investigate A V associations creatively .
To achieve this, we conducted preliminary work exploring compu-
tational approaches towards the expression of audiovisual relation-
ships, speciﬁcally through artiﬁcial life systems, multi-agent systems,
genetic algorithms and genetic programming. We highlight the ex-
ploration of a multi-agent system to visually express sound in an
artiﬁcial life environment mediated by GA and later an adaptive A V
language guided by GP processes.
We tested the ﬁnal work in two key parts: automatic and partially
guided evolution. In the ﬁrst instance, we used GA to generate A V as-
sociations through an automated ﬁtness function. In addition, we per-
formed optimisation tests with various conﬁgurations to enhance its
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

180 conclusion
efﬁciency in evolving complex A V relationships. User tests were con-
ducted to evaluate whether the evolved relationships aligned with
human perception of the intended underlying emotions. In the sec-
ond instance, we combined the user inputs and perception with the
main theoretical ﬁndings explored in Part i. This iteration of the sys-
tem allowed us to investigate the capacity of the system to adapt to
users’ preferences and inﬂuence the outcomes.
Following a layered approach, we enabled a clear tracking of how
each property contributed to the ﬁnal system, making the decision-
making process transparent and interpretable through multiple mech-
anisms such as: the explicit mapping between auditory and visual
properties through measurable parameters (e.g. pitch, loudness, shape
edge, and color); and the dual-component ﬁtness function combining
theoretical foundations with user preferences. With this, we could
better understand how user interactions inﬂuenced the evolutionary
process to shape audio-visual relationships.
This investigation contributed to a better understanding of the cre-
ative processes behind the computational exploration of audiovisual
associations while providing the scientiﬁc foundations for solving
cross-modal problems and emotion-driven design artefacts, bringing
new possibilities for the conscious creation of Creativity Support Tools.
This work resulted in the following publications:
• Rodrigues, A., Machado, P ., Martins, P ., & Cardoso, A. ( 2015).
Sound visualization through a swarm of ﬁreﬂies . 17th Portuguese
Conference on Artiﬁcial Intelligence (EPIA) (pp. 664-670). Springer.
• Rodrigues, A., Cardoso, A., & Machado, P . ( 2016). Harmonic con-
stellation: An audiovisual environment of living organisms . 4th Inter-
national Workshop on Musical Metacreation (ICCC).
• Rodrigues, A., Costa, E., Cardoso, A., Machado, P ., & Cruz, T.
(2016). Evolving L-systems with musical notes . 5th International
Conference on Computational Intelligence in Music, Sound, Art
and Design (Evo MUSART) (pp. 186-201). Springer.
• Rodrigues, A., Sousa, B., Cardoso, A., & Machado, P . ( 2022).
"Found in translation": An evolutionary framework for auditory-visual
relationships. Entropy Journal, Special Issue on Artiﬁcial Intelli-
gence and Complexity in Art, Music, Games and Design III.
While the developed system showed signiﬁcant potential, challenges
such as ensuring diversity in the generated outputs, motion incorpo-
ration into images, and a more granular UI to ﬁne-tune A V relation-
ships were noted, providing directions for future work. Additionally ,
an expansion of user tests in the direction of Image to Music is recom-
mended since we focused on testing mappings from Music to Image
as a proof-of-concept.
As we reﬂect on the future of Computational Creativity , further re-
search on computational techniques, such as neural networks, could
bring novel perspectives on the interplay between individual creativ-
ity and interaction with creative processes, ensuring expressive and
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]

conclusion 181
perpetually relevant outcomes. Broadly speaking, how can different
cultural contexts and human interactions shape CC processes? Study-
ing interactive techniques where the public can collaboratively inﬂu-
ence and steer machines’ creative processes could lead to advances
in the computational generation of Cross-Modal Associations. Lastly ,
since this work was founded on principles of human perception and
creativity , ethical implications and potential biases introduced by co-
creative systems should also be considered.
[ December 28, 2024 at 15:00 – classicthesis ]
